COURSE STRUCTURE 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
INTRODUCTION 
The field of psychology known as counselling psychology entails both theoretical and practical study 
in a  number of major areas, including counselling process and outcome, supervision and training, 
career development and counselling, and prevention and health. Focusing on assets and strengths, 
person environment interactions, educational and professional growth, brief connections, and intact 
personalities are some common themes among counselling psychologists. 
Depending on the services they offer and clientele they serve, counselling psychologists work in a 
variety of locations. Some work as teachers, managers, researchers, and service providers in colleges 
and universities. Others work in independent practice, offering services to individuals, 
couples/families, groups, and organizations in the areas of counselling, psychotherapy, evaluation, and 
consultancy. 
This book, Counselling Process and Skills, is written with the distance learning student in mind. It 
is presented in a user-friendly format using a clear, lucid language. Each unit contains an Introduction 
and a list of Objectives to prepare the student for what to expect in the text. At the end of each unit are 
a Summary and a list of Key Words, to aid in recollection of concepts learnt. All units contain Self -
Assessment Questions and Exercises, and strategically placed Check Your Progress questions so the 
student can keep track of what has been discussed. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Course Objective 
1. To analyze theories of sensory transmission and evaluate their implications for human 
adaptation and cognitive functioning. 
2. To examine the neural basis of attention and assess factors influencing its role in everyday 
cognitive tasks and related disorders. 
3. To investigate cognitive mechanisms underlying thinking and language, with emphasis on 
theories, decision-making, and problem-solving processes. 
4. To critically evaluate memory functions, models, and disorders, and apply cognitive principles 
to real-life contexts and problem-solving situations. 
 
Course Outcome 
 
1. Students will be able to define counselling and explain its processes, different approaches 
(e.g., psychodynamic, behavioural, humanistic), and its application in the Indian context.  
2. Students will demonstrate the ability to use various counselling techniques (e.g., active 
listening, questioning, feedback), and skills (e.g., inside -outside, micro, macro, and self -
monitoring) effectively in mock counselling sessions.  
3. Students will compare traditional and modern models of counselling, including indigenous 
frameworks specific to Indian socio-cultural settings.  
4. Students will assess the development and importance of the working alliance and demonstrate 
strategies for effective closure or termination of the counselling process.

1.1 Objectives 
After going through this unit, you will be able to:  
• Discuss the definition and process of counselling 
• Explain the approaches to counselling and helping 
• Describe the development of the counsellor -counselee relationship as well as 
counselling in India 
1.2 Introduction 
Counselling is a form of ‘talking therapy’ that offers people a better chance to change how 
they feel and to live better. Counselling may be required by individuals severely affected by 
mental health illness. Such illness may have resulted from causes related to any form of 
abuse, troubled relations, adolescence, substance use, and so on. For instance, diagnosis of 
depression in adolescents can be difficult as a result of problems demarcating the normal, 
transient inconveniences and developmental issues that occur among this age group. Thus, 
appropriate counselling is essential to prevent the far -reaching effects leading to serious 
consequences. Hence, counselling as a therapy has proved effective in resolving and curing 
problems of different kinds associated with mental health of an individual. In this unit, we 
will discuss the definition and process of counselling, along with the approaches to 
counselling and helping. It will also focus on development of counsellor -counselee 
relationship and counselling in India.  
1.3 Defining Counselling 
The term counselling has been defined by different people in various ways. For instance, 
Rogers (1952) describes counselling as the process by which the structure of the self is 
relaxed in the safety of the client’s relationship with the therapist and previously denied 
experiences are perceived and then integrated into an altered self. But according to, Pepinsky 
and Pepinsky (1954), counselling refers to an ‘interaction which occurs between two 
individuals called the counsellor and the client that takes place in a professional setting and is 
initiated and maintained to facilitate changes in the behaviour of the client’. 
Gustad (1953) thought of counselling as a ‘learning oriented process that is carried out in a 
simple one to one social environment , in which the counsellor ( who is professionally 
competent in relevant psychological skills and knowledge) seeks to assist the client, by 
methods appropriate to the latter’s needs and within the context of the total personal 
programme to learn how to put, such understanding into effect, in relation to more clearly 
perceived, realistically defined goals to the end that the client may become a happier and 
more productive member of the society’. 
Similarly, Smith (1955) defines counselling as a process in which the counsellor assists the 
counselee to make an interpretation of facts relating to a choice, plan or adjustments which he 
needs to make. In the same year (1955), Hahn and MacLean defined counselling as a ‘process 
which takes place in a one-to-one relationship between an individual beset problems with 
which he cannot cope alone and a professional worker whose training and experience have 
qualified him to help others reach solutions to various types of personal difficulties’. 
3 
 
Patterson (1959) states that ‘counselling is a process involving interpersonal relationships 
between a therapist and one or more client by which the former employs psychological 
methods based on systematic knowledge of the human personality in attempting to improve 
mutual health of the latter’. 
Perez (1965) defined counselling as an ‘interactive process conjoining the counselee who 
needs assistance and the counsellor who is trained and educated to give this assistance’. 
Blocher (1966) explains counselling as ‘helping an individual become aware of himself and 
the ways in which he is reacting to the behavioural influences of his environment. It further 
helps him to establish some personal meaning for this behaviour and to develop and clarify a 
set of goals and values for future behaviour’. 
Along similar lines, Wrenn (1970) defined counselling as a process that is concerned with 
decision-making skills and problem resolution. The counsellor aids in decision making, in 
expanding his client’s range of alternatives or options open to him, in modifying his 
behaviour patterns in desired directions. 
Stefflre (1970) states that counselling is a learning -teaching process where the client learns 
about his life space as he has to make meaningful and informed choices, he must know 
himself, the facts of his present situation, and the possibilities.......as well as the most likely 
consequences of the various choices. Shertzer and Stone (1976) also define counselling as a 
learning process in which individuals learn about themselves; their interpersonal relationships 
and behaviours that advance their personal development. 
In 1992, Gladding stated that ‘counselling is a relatively short term, interpersonal, theory 
based, professional activity guided by ethical an legal standards that focus on helping persons 
who are basically psychologically healthy to resolve developmental and situational 
problems’. Unlike the above, Belkin (1975) believes that counselling cannot be specifically 
defined since it is a changing entity, but there are skills common to all such facilitative 
relationships. 
From the above -mentioned several , it may be observed that the emphasis placed on the 
various aspects of counselling by various authors differs. Cognitive factors were emphasized 
earlier but subsequently the scope was widened to include the affective factors as well. 
Similarly, in earlier times counselling process was viewed as a one -to-one process but in 
more recent years the scope of the relationship between the counsellor and client is 
broadening from dyadic relationships to group interactions. 
However, all the above-mentioned definitions contain certain similarities. For instance, all of 
them view counselling as a ‘process which involves bringing about sequential changes over a 
period of time leading to a set goal’. All the definitions focus on the counsellor –counselee 
relationship as something that is not casual but is characterized by warmth, responsiveness, 
and understanding. 
Problems can occur/recur during any point in life, therefore counselling must necessarily be 
an ongoing and continuing process concerning persons of all age levels and placed in 
different life situations. ‘The major objective of all types of counselling is to help individuals 
become self -sufficient, self -dependent, self -directed and to adjust themselves efficiently to 
the demands of a better, happier and a meaningful life’. 
4 
 
Counselling is also viewed by most as  both an art and a science. It is a science in the sense 
that these are many things known about the differences between effective counselling and 
ineffective counselling. In addition, research has pointed to several specific attributes of a 
good and a not -so-good counsellor. Also, there are specific types of behaviours that can be 
taken by counsellor to foster growth in the client.                                                                        
On the other hand, counselling is also considered to be an art, because there are certain 
unaccountable and immeasurable dimensions to counselling. Furthermore, counsellors add 
certain ‘artistic’ variations of their own when engaging in counselling clients, which are 
derived from personal flair. The artist angle can also be seen in the manner in which different 
counsellors apply various techniques and methods in a highly personalized manner in the 
counselling process. That is each and every counsellor applies in a very unique manner what 
is known about counselling. 
Several misconceptions are also held by people about counselling. Some of the serious 
misconceptions held about counselling are as follows:  
• Counselling is all about giving information, although some information giving  
can be a part of counselling. 
• Counselling is about giving advice, making suggestions and recommendations. 
• Counselling is about influencing the client’s values, attitudes, beliefs, interests, 
decisions etc. with or without any threat. 
• Counselling is all about interviewing clients.  
In fact, counselling is concerned with bringing about a voluntary change in the client. The 
counsellor provides facilities to help achieve the desired change or make the suitable change. 
The decisions and choices are eventually made by the client where the counsellor simply 
assists the client in arriving at these decisions through a warm and understanding relationship. 
1.3.1 Characteristics of Counselling 
Humans are highly complex beings both physically and psychologically and so are the range 
of problems and situations they encounter. Human deal with a myriad of situations ranging 
from emotional conflicts to basic decision making, to interpersonal relationships, to minor 
conflicts, to learning problems, to vocational problems etc. To help people deal effectively 
with all these problems, specialized skills and techniques are developed, which are provided 
by counsellors in the form of counselling. Counselling is characterized as a professional 
activity or a process that deals with “comparatively normal psychologically healthy people” It 
aims to bring about some learning or change in the behaviour of these “comparatively normal 
psychologically healthy people”. Counselling deals with personal, social, vocational or 
educational concerns and it includes many sub specialities such as career counselling, child 
counselling, marital counselling, etc. 
The term counselling over the years has been used synonymously with several other terms 
like advising, guidance, helping relationship, as a solution to human problems, psychotherapy 
etc. However, it both shares few things in common and yet differs from them in several ways. 
For instance, advising means ‘giving assistance to an individual who may face the problem of 
5 
 
taking a decision or making a choice’. This situation has several important features as it has 
an element of voluntariness and a ‘belief that the other person has the necessary experience, 
wisdom, and ability to advice’. The advice sought is usually for a ‘reality check’ of views by 
the client. The advice given is ‘not binding on the person who seeks it’, that is, the person 
may accept or reject it, may approach another person for the same which could also be 
rejected if found unsuitable or unacceptable. Advising has no psychological implications as 
regards individual development. Advice may be helpful only in a particular situation, it may 
not be generalized. Therefore, the role may be very limited. 
The second term with which counselling is used synonymous with is guidance. However, 
counselling is not only guidance. The term guidance refers to the “assistance given to an 
individual in making intelligent choices and adjustments.” It is based on the assumption that 
it is the duty and right of every individual to choose one’s own way of life as long as the 
choice does not interfere with or infringe on the rights of others. It is also based on the belief 
that the ability to make such choices is not inborn but like other abilities it must be 
developed.  
When we say that counselling is not all about guidance, we say so as guidance essentially 
consists of giving information in order to help people arrive at certain choices of their life. 
According to Pasricha and Jones, guidance activities include orientation, program testing, 
help in study skills, career and educational information. The term guidance has been 
explained by various people differently. For instance, Nelson -Jones (1994) defined guidance 
“as the help given by one person to another in making choices and adjustments and in solving 
problems.” According to Pasricha, guidance consists of a group of services which assist an 
individual with the knowledge and the necessary skills to make choices, plans and 
interpretations for adjustment so that the individual may make an effective member of the 
group. Pasricha states that there are three main aims of guidance: 
· To help the individual understand one’s own interests, abilities etc. 
· To provide information on educational and occupational requirements and 
opportunities. 
· To provide means for determining placement of individuals in a class, occupation or 
community. 
In short, information is necessarily given in counselling, but all information -giving is not 
counselling. Counselling differs from guidance in that it is therapeutic. 
The term counselling also differs from clinical psychology, as unlike counselling, clinical 
psychology is concerned with the description, classification, diagnosis, and prognosis of 
psychological illnesses. The clinical psychologist usually functions in a clinical setting, that 
is, in a hospital. Usually a clinical psychologist is found working in close association with the 
psychiatrist, thereby aiding and providing assistance to him in the diagnosis and evaluation of 
psychological disorders. As compared to a clinical psychologist, the counsellor deals with 
less severe psychological disorders that do not require hospitalization but which seriously or 
adversely affect ease of adjustment. 
6 
 
There are some who view counselling as another form of helping relationship. However, it 
may not be correct to see counselling just as a helping relationship as a helping relationship is 
characterized by certain essential features (Shertzer and Stone, 1968) as follows: 
 · It is meaningful because it is personal and intimate 
 · It is affective in nature involving mild to strong emotional relationships 
· It involves the integrity of the helper and the helped and is sustained 
voluntarily 
· It involves the mutual consent of the counsellor and the counselee, either 
explicitly stated or implicitly  
· It takes place because the individual in need of help is aware of one’s own 
limitations and inadequacies. 
 · It involves confidence reposed in the helper 
· It is often achieved and maintained through communication and interaction; it 
involves give and take, that is, it is not a one-way process. 
· It involves a certain amount of structure. The situation is either vaguely or 
clearly defined. 
· It is marked by the desire for change in the existing condition of the client, that 
is, it is concerned with the improvement of the client. 
Some even view counselling as a solution to human problems.  Counselling certainly helps 
people in dealing with their problems but it cannot be seen simply as a solution provider as in 
it the counsellor does not give solution as such or does not make choice and decision on the 
behalf of the client. Instead, “counselling aims at helping clients to understand and accept 
themselves ‘as they are’,” and enables them to work towards realizing their potential. 
Counselling often involves modification of attitudes, outlook, and behaviour. The nature of 
the counselling process also depends on the setting or the situation in which counselling is 
required. The counsellor has unconditional regard and acceptance for the client’s personality 
or self. In counselling, the counsellor does not attempt to solve the client’s problems or 
suggest choices that could reduce his/her emotional conflicts. Through counselling, the client 
is helped to discover for oneself the strengths and weaknesses one may have. Counselling 
aims at helping individual reach a stage or a state of self -autonomy through self -
understanding, self-direction and self-motivation. 
Counselling and psychotherapy are two terms that are often used analogously. Rogers (1942), 
Patterson (1973) etc. believed that there is no difference between the two. However, there are 
some who believe that they are similar and different in many ways. The two are similar in 
following ways: 
· Both share similar goals namely self -exploration, self -understanding, and 
behavioural change. 
· Both aim at eliminating self -defeating behaviours and enabling people to 
develop effective rational ways of dealing with various situations in their life. 
7 
 
· Both lay a great deal of emphasis on helping clients develop better decision 
making and planning skills and become autonomous beings. 
· The client-counsellor relationship is an integral part of both counselling and 
psychotherapy. 
Some view psychotherapy as a broader term which is a host of several therapies, with 
hypnosis at one extreme and counselling at another. Thus for them counselling is a form of 
psychotherapy. Rao (1989), draws the distinction between counselling and psychotherapy on 
the following four basis: 
 (i) Individual receiving help: In counselling, the person is designated as a client 
but in psychotherapy the person is designated as a patient. Counselling deals with people who 
are basically psychologically healthy whereas psychotherapy deals with people who are 
mentally ill. 
 (ii) Severity of problems concerned:  Psychotherapy deals with problems that 
have developed into rigid neurotic patterns embedded into an individual’s personality 
structure. Whereas, counselling deals with relatively less severe developmental and 
situational problems like learning difficulties, minor conflicts etc. 
 (iii) Person giving help:  Psychotherapy is generally provided by clinical 
psychologists who are trained in relevant psychological skills and knowledge whereas 
counselling is provided by counsellors who are trained in various counselling techniques. 
 (iv) The setting in which the help is rendered:  Psychotherapist providing 
psychotherapy are usually seen in hospital settings but counsellors providing counselling are 
seen in schools, rehabilitation centres etc. 
In addition to the above mentioned differences, there is also a difference in the techniques 
employed by the two. In psychotherapy, the treatment is given based on various therapeutic 
models which are based more on the medical model of treatment. Whereas, in counselling, 
the objective of self -enhancement, self -integration etc. are accompanied through the 
psychological techniques of establishing rapport, providing unconditional warmth, 
acceptance, regard and gaining insight. 
According to Belkin (1988) two other prominent positions which help to draw a distinction 
between counselling and psychotherapy are: 
(i) Seriousness of the client-patient problems and the emotional depth and intensity of the 
treatment:  “Counselling is concerned with helping individuals learn new ways of dealing 
with and adjusting to life situation and in developing sound decision making skills. It does 
not attempt to restructure personality as in psychotherapy but rather it attempts to develop 
what already exists” (Perry, 1955). 
(ii) The theoretical foundations, practical concerns, and discrepant historical origins. 
Counselling is directed at helping clients deal with their immediate problems and improve 
their life situations whereas  psychotherapy is more interested in certain areas of personality, 
particularly areas that are symptomatic of the past emotional disturbances or conflict. 
Psychotherapy is based more on the medical model of treatment, on the theories of 
personality, theories of psychopathology and a body of clinical te chniques. On the contrary, 
8 
 
counselling is built upon physiological foundations emphasizing more the person’s 
individuality, uniqueness and his phenomenological world. 
In short, some differences do exist between counselling and psychotherapy but they are 
quantitative— a difference in degree —rather than qualitative. In other words, they are not 
entirely separate process, but rather different ways of approaching the same fundamental and 
slightly complex phenomenon—the human being. 
1.3.2 Evolution of Counselling 
The field of counselling is also seen by many both as an art and as a science. According to 
Gustad (1953), ‘counselling is a learning oriented process, carried out in a simple one to one 
social environment, in which, the counsellor, professionally competent in relevant 
psychological skills and knowledge seeks to assist the client, by methods appropriate to the 
latter’s need and within the context of the total personal programme, to learn how to put, such 
understanding into effect in relation to more clearly perceived realistically defined goals to 
the end that the client may become a happier and more productive member of the society’. 
One can certainly say, that counselling is a ‘relatively short term, interpersonal theory based, 
professional activity guided by ethical an logical standards that focuses on helping persons 
who are basically psychologically healthy to resolve developmental and situational problems’ 
(Gladding, 1992). 
Counselling represents the fusion of many influences. It brings together the movement 
towards a more compassionate treatment of mental problems begun in mid -nineteenth 
century, France, the psychodynamic insights of Freud and psychoanalysis; the scientific 
scrutiny and methodology of the behavioural approach; the quantitative science of 
psychometrics; the humanistic perspective of client -centered therapy; the philosophical bases 
of existentialism, and the practical insights and applications that evolved from the vocational 
guidance movement. 
According to Belkin (1988), the profession of counselling is not some well -established entity, 
but rather it is a dynamic movement still in its developing stages. Perhaps because it is a new 
discipline, counselling in its slow process of gestation and development, has been going 
through a confusing time of doubt and self -examination, a period of finding itself and 
defining itself more precisely. 
Even today, confusion exists about the terms and roles of a counsellor, counsellor educator, 
counsellor psychologist, and clinical psychologist (White , Kagan, Harmon, Fretz and 
Tanney, 1984). In 1977, an entire issue of The Counselling Psychologist attempted to come to 
grips with the professional identity of counselling as a discipline and of counsellor as a 
professional. In his presidential address to Division 17 (the division of counselling 
psychologist) of the American Psychological Association (APA), Norman Kagan (1977) 
compared the role of counsellor to the primary care physician who are ‘broadly skilled and 
have a holistic approach to the individual’. The role, as he conceptualized it, was preventive 
as well as therapeutic. 
Ten years later, Fitzgerald and Osipow (1986), in trying to determine what counsellor and the 
counselling psychologists actually do in their professional time, conducted a national 
occupational analysis of the counselling profession. They surveyed members of Division 17 
9 
 
to determine their job titles, places of employment, and activities performed. The large 
majority worked in an academic setting, with a relatively large no. having full time private 
practice. About 13 per cent worked in counselling centres, another common employment 
setting. 
In short, the field of counselling is actually a growing, evolving, continually changing 
concept, responsive to a nexus of interlocking pressure and concerns. In the late 1980s, 
counselling is clearly a movement dedicated to personal and social growth and change, a 
movement which itself is still in the process of growing, changing and defining itself. 
Foundations of Counselling Philosophical, Sociological, and Psychological Foundations 
The counselling profession has its foundations in: laboratory psychology; innovative 
approaches to the treatment of the ‘insane’; the mental hygiene movement; the mental testing 
movement; the influence of humanistic psychology; and the vocational guidance movement. 
The laboratory psychology began in 1879 when Wilhelm Wundt set up the first experimental 
psychology laboratory in Leipzig, Germany. The field of psychology began to get recognition 
quite gradually, first in the academic profession and then in the public sphere. Enhancement 
in the fields of physiology and empirical enhancements in the area of philosophy also 
facilitated the growth of laboratory psychology. The physiological studies of E.H. Weber, 
Johannes Muller, and Hermann V on Helmholtz revealed that there is a significant relationship 
between physical and mental states.  
Wundt, thereby, tried to study the structure of the mind by using the method of introspection. 
This method required the subjects to verbalize and self -reflection all that they were 
experiencing while doing experiments. In fact the use of introspection dates back to the 
Greek philosophers, especially Plato. Using the method of introspection, Wundt began to 
study higher mental functions by directing them at self observation and tried to make them 
experimental, which eventually helped making psychology more empirical and scientific in 
nature. 
The major limitation with this method was that there was too much room for subjectivity. It 
was also difficult to replicate its findings, hence soon Wundt’s method of introspection was 
modified by William James (a functionalist), who attempted to discover its functions, instead 
of simply studying the structure of mind. William James and his followers gradually 
developed new experimental design to study perception, attention, responses to stimuli, habit 
patterns, and so on, with the idea of understanding why the mind functions as it does.  
Around the same time behaviourism appeared in 1912 which had a significant influence on 
the field of counseling. Unlike structualists and functionalists, behaviourists instead of 
studying consciousness began to study observable and measurable behaviour. With this as its 
working premise, behaviourists tried to “formulate, through systematic observation and 
experimentation the generalizations, laws and principles that underlie the individual’s 
behavior (Watson, 1919). 
The significant differences among the above-mentioned schools regarding what psychology 
should study, arose questions like —should psychologists be primarily concerned with 
feelings, perceptions, associations, and other manifestations of consciousness, or should they 
focus their attention on symptoms, actions, learning, and other observable and quantifiable 
10 
 
forms of behaviour? This brought counsellors in the field of counselling to address questions 
like—should the counselor be primarily concerned with the client’s condition of 
consciousness— his or her subjectivity —vague as this concept is, or should the counsellor 
deal primarily with the client’s observable and empirically modifiable behaviour? These 
fundamental questions still challenge us. The responsibility of each counsellor entering the 
profession to decide which proposition is acceptable and which assumptions will act as a 
guide in practice.  
Despite all the early school theorists who tried to investigate the complexities of the human 
psyche, there were no psychological attempts to treat emotional problems or emotional 
illnesses. In fact, no such discipline arose until the mid -nineteenth century when a number of 
disparate movements took hold and individually attempted to treat mental and emotional 
illness in a humane, constructive, scientifically valid manner. Before that, the mentally ill 
were treated in an inhumane manner by the magic healers or the priests or the medicine men. 
Yet their work ultimately led to the psychological treatment of emotional disorders.  
Soon asylums were developed to keep these mentally ill people. In France in 1793, a young 
psychiatrist, Dr. Philippe Pinel, was given charge of the notorious Bicetre asylum. Pinel 
believed that the main job of an asylum was to restore the mentally ill people to the level of 
functional mental health. His efforts lead to the mental health movement which met with 
much opposition from the community and from the national government. 
After Pinel, a significant contribution in the treatment of mentally ill was made by Jean 
Martin Charcot, who was a distinguished neurologist, with a special interest in psychosomatic 
diseases. He had spent several years diagnosing and treating patients who believed they were 
suffering from a form of paralysis or epilepsy, but who actually had nothing physically wrong 
with them. He labeled this disorder hysteria and formulated a detailed clinical description of 
it, distinguishing it from closely related symptomatic disorders. He pioneered the use of 
hypnosis as a clinical tool in the diagnosis of hysterical disorders, suggesting that hypnosis 
itself was a form of, ‘artificial’ hysteria. 
Charcot used hypnosis as a tool to diagnose hysterical patients but Janet (one of his 
colleagues) used hypnosis as a method to treat hysterical patients. He discovered through trial 
and error that persons with hysteria were able to recall memories and feelings from the past, 
under hypnosis. During hypnosis, when the client tended to remember his buried and 
forgotten memories, a sudden alleviation of the debilitating symptoms was seen. This sudden 
freeing of these dammed-up memories was termed as catharsis. 
Impressed by the works of Charcot, Freud along with Breuer, used the cathartic -hypnotic 
treatment in with hysterical patients. At the same time in the United States, there was no 
national policy, no prominent physicians and no humanitarian organization that watched out 
for the interests of the mentally ill. The mentally ill were separated from the society and were 
kept away from so called normal people by giving the rationale that this is going to be good 
for the mentally ill and for the general welfare. During this same time, the vocational 
guidance movement was in its flowering stages in the United States. 
That was the time when it was needed that the public should become sensitive towards the 
plight of mentally ill. Clifford Beers strongly believed that the mentally ill should be treated 
in a humanitarian manner. Clifford Beers was a minor clerk in the financial district of New 
11 
 
York City, who suffered from schizophrenia and had spent many of his years in mental 
institutions. During his stay at these mental institutions, he was quite outraged by the 
conditions in these institutes and often felt helpless as during his stay there he was unable to 
do anything about these mental institutions.  
However, after recovering to a level where he was functional enough, he wrote a book about 
his experiences A Mind That Found Itself which became an influential bestseller. His personal 
experiences in the form of the book instigated rapid and sweeping changes in the field of 
mental health care. They bought about a reform, which was much needed. Soon, he became a 
social activist and a crusader, helping to find the society for mental hygiene. 
Once the American public became aware of the need for mental health reform, the 
innovations of Freud and his contemporaries were quickly imported and put into practice. 
From the 1920s onward, psychoanalysis and its derivative approaches (especially Adlerian 
therapy) began to make an impact on the counseling movement. The laboratory work of 
Watson, Skinner, and the other behavioural psychologists also began to influence the 
counseling movement. 
Greatly influenced by Wundt’s work in Europe, several students from United States came to 
his laboratory to learn the new experimental methods being used there. Each student brought 
back to the United States a cornucopia of methodological procedures and intellectual insight 
which were to be applied in their own special areas of interests and expertise. One of the 
more famous of these students, was a young man named G. Stanley Hall who would 
ultimately have an important impact on the counseling movement, Granville Stanley Hall can 
be rightly said to be the father of American psychology, although James was the first 
American psychologist. James was the leader of the American Psychology movement and 
was responsible for giving psychology and ultimately counseling their orientation and 
direction. He established one of the first psychology departments, organized the APA, and 
was responsible for granting the first doctorates in this new field, thus providing increased 
recognition for the profession. 
In 1883, Hall set up a private laboratory for psychological study at John Hopkins University 
in Baltimore. Soon, psychological laboratories were formed at Harvard, Pennsylvania, 
Wisconsin, Columbia, Clark, Cornell, Indiana, Brown, Stanford, Yale, and Chicago 
Universities. Another significant contribution in the field of psychology was made by L. M. 
Terman, who was trained by Hall and is known for his studies of the gifted. 
At Clark University, Hall conducted his famous studies of children and adolescents, which 
can be considered as the first true psychological investigations of human development. His 
studies significantly helped in defining child and adolescent psychology as significant 
disciplines. These studies also became indirectly the source of American psychologists’ 
interest in the quantitative approach to science, which later had an enduring effect on the 
American testing movement. 
This testing movement became a significant part of counseling as counselors and educators 
began to recognize the value of testing. Then in the late nineteenth century, Sir Francis Galton 
tried to devise a simple measure of intelligence. Soon the profession of counseling became 
closely involved with and influenced by testing and measurement movement. Binet’s 
development of first IQ tests; Lewis M. Terman’s administration of the test to a large 
12 
 
population and Edward L. Thorndike’s myriad contributions to the testing movement all had 
a significant impact on the profession of counseling. 
Soon a large number of quantitative tools were developed to help assess the client’s needs, to 
measure their abilities, aptitudes, and problems and to evaluate more correctly the results of 
the counselling experience. By the 1950s there was no area of counselling that had not been 
profoundly influenced by the testing movement. Till date counseling considers assessment 
and appraisal as central to its effective functioning as a distinct profession.  
Both the behavioural laboratory psychology and use of empirical, quantitative methods in the 
form of testing offered a scientific dimension to counselling. However, the most notable 
reaction to the quantitative influence in counselling was provided by the humanistic approach 
which found the use of quantitative methods in the study of human psychology as too 
depersonalizing. The humanistic approach advocates that patients should be viewed as a total, 
distinct, and important individuals and not just as a proof for some tentative theory of 
behavior, learning, or psychosexual development.  
Carl R. Rogers was the pioneer of the nondirective psychology and his ideas have had a 
significant impact upon the development of counseling. Rogers’s influence upon the 
counselling movement is incalculable and the tradition he established set the stage for the 
merging of the terms counseling and psychology.  
Another school of thought called as the philosophy of existentialism, also had a significant 
influence on the field of counseling. Existentialism postulates that people are free agents, 
seeking meaning in their lives through their voluntary actions and they are responsible for the 
consequences of the actions taken by them. The impact of Existential approaches have been 
significant in the areas of vocational counseling, job training, personal work, and 
rehabilitation counseling. The revolution also began to influence the vocational guidance 
movement which was originated by Frank Parson. The vocational guidance aimed at helping 
individuals find themselves through their work and this goal of self -fulfillment made it 
compatible with the growing counselling movement. 
In addition, other changes at that time like industrialization, specialization, urbanization, the 
changing role of women, the growing need for education, rising enrollments, expanding 
curricula, rising secularism, the desire for useful education and new educational theories etc. 
further created and intensified the need for guidance-personnel work.  
In short, counselling has evolved in part from the vocational guidance movement, which was 
brought about largely by the changing social and demographic forces that played so crucial 
role in shaping the field of counseling the way it is today.

 1. Define counselling according to Pepinsky and Pepinsky (1954).

 3. Define clinical psychology.  
 4. Name some works during the testing movement which had a significant     
impact on the profession of counselling.

1.4 Counselling Process: Development of Counsellor-Counselee Relationship 
In this section, we will discuss the stages of counselling in detail. 
1.4.1 Initial Session  
The counselling process begins with the initial session. The initial counselling interview is 
often critical to the ultimate success or failure of the entire course of the counselling process. 
In the first session, both counsellor and client work to figure out if they want to or can 
continue the relationship. 
The initial counselling interview can be counsellor or client initiated and can center on the 
gathering of information or on relationship dynamics. When counsellor initiated, the 
counsellor should immediately state the reason for wanting to see the client but when client 
initiated, he should listen as hard as possible to what ever the client has to say (Benjamin, 
1981). Information -oriented interview focuses on gathering information through the use of 
probes, accents, closed questions and request for classification (Hackney and Cormier, 1988). 
Such interviews are usually conducted by counsellors who are employed by medical, mental 
health, correctional, rehabilitation and social agencies for psychological, vocational, or 
psycho-social assessment.  
Relationship-oriented interviews focus more on the client’s attitudes and emotions. Common 
counsellor responses include re -statement, reflection of feelings, summarizing feelings, 
request for classification and acknowledgement of non -verbal behaviour (Haurney and 
Cormier, 1988). 
Counsellors begin an initial interview by trying to make their clients feel comfortable 
(Hackney and Cormier, 1988; Pretrofesa et al. 1984; Shertzer and Stone, 1980). Basic 
attending behaviour and client observation skills helps in establishing and maintaining 
rapport which is vital for the disclosure of information and the ultimate success of 
counselling. Benefits derived from the initial interview can be enhanced by the counsellor 
through appropriately conveyed empathy, encouragement, support, caring, attentiveness, 
acceptance and genuineness. 
In the initial interview, it is critical that counsellors are able to convey primary empathy 
(Patterson and Eisenberg, 1983) via verbal and non -verbal communication. It refers to 
conveying that counsellor has understood the client’s major themes. Advanced empathy 
which is a process of helping a client explore themes, issues and emotions new to his or her 
awareness (Patterson and Eisenberg, 1983) is usually inappropriate for an initial interview 
because it examines too much material too quickly. In either case empathy may be fostered 
by attentiveness that is the amount of verbal and non-verbal behaviour shown to the client. 
Verbal behaviour like probing, requesting, clarification, re -statement and summarizing 
feelings and non -verbal behaviour like smiling, learning forward, making eye contact, 
gesturing and head moving (Mehrabian, 1970) indicate that the counsellor is interested in and 
is open to clients. Egan (1990), summarized five non -verbal skills as SOLER (smile, open -
posture, learning forward, eye -contact and relax). According to Eisenberg (1983), counsellor 
should generally avoid — advice giving, lecturing, excessive questioning and story telling. 
Sack (1985) believes that advice is effective only when asked by the client. 
14 
 
In the final part of building a counselling relationship the counsellor helps the client explore 
specific areas and identify goals. Instead of making unfocussed, unrealistic or un -co-
ordinated goals (Rule, 1982) counsellor should make specific, quantifiable, measurable, 
observable, behavioural, understandable goals which can be clearly stated; are relevant to 
self-defeating behaviour and are achievement and success oriented and are mutually agreed 
on by both the counsellor and the client (Dyer and Vriend, 1977).  
Difficulties like moving too fast or too slow, fear of intensity, client rambling, and   time and 
energy devoted to probing the past in the exploratory and goal -setting stage of counselling 
may inhibit building of a sound solid client-counsellor relationship. 
1.4.2 Termination  
Just as the initial interview and building of a sound client -counsellor relationship is essential 
for the counselling process to be effective, so is the careful termination of the counselling 
relationship. Termination refers to the decision, one -sided or mutual to stop counselling 
(Burke, 1989). It may produce mixed feelings on the part of both the counsellor (Goodyear, 
1981) and the client (London, 1982) and when handled incorrectly, it may cause harm than 
heal. Termination serves several functions like: 
· By signalling that something is finished it reinforces the client’s behavioural 
changes in the direction of the goals as stated by the client (Eisenberg and 
Delancy, 1977). 
· Both the client and the counsellor are motivated by the knowledge that the 
counselling experience is limited in time (Munro and Bach, 1955). 
· It maintains the changes already achieved by providing an opportunity for 
generalizing the acquired problem -solving skills in the real world, that is, it 
puts the insights into action (Gladding, 1990). Thus, what seems like an exit 
becomes an entrance. 
· It serves as a reminder that the client has matured (Vickio, 1990) and is 
capable of establishing mutually supportive relationships and lead a more 
independent and satisfying life (Burke, 1989). 
When to terminate the relationship is a question that has no definite answer. If the 
relationship ends too soon, the client may regress to earlier behaviour but if termination is 
never addressed, client may become too dependent. According to Hackney and Cormier 
(1988), there are several pragmatic universal considerations to keep in mind in timing the 
termination as follows: 
· It is easy to figure out, if the goals have been achieved in a relationship which 
was set up with a behavioural contract. 
· It is easy to figure out, if the client thinks they have accomplished what they 
wanted to accomplish, regardless of specific contracts. 
· Termination is appropriate if the client and the counsellor feel that the 
relationship is not helpful. 
 · If there is a move or prolonged issues, then termination is inevitable. 
15 
 
Termination is a complex area involving several issues as follows: 
1. Termination of individual session:  usually lasts for 45 to 50 minutes. 
Benjamin (1981) proposes two important factors in closing an interview as follows: 
· Both the client and the counsellor should be aware that the session is ending 
· No new material should be introduced or discussed during this ending. If introduced, 
then it should be the focus of the next session. 
There are several ways in which a counsellor can close an interview 
effectively. Some being: 
· By simply making brief statement indicating that the time is up (Benjamin, 1981); 
Hackney and Cormier, 1988) 
· By using non -verbal gestures like looking at watch or standing (Pietrofesa et al, 
1984). Non -verbal gestures are used with verbal indicators as each reinforces the 
other. 
Towards the end of the session, it is usually helpful by summarize what has 
happened in the session. The summarization may be client or counsellor initiated 
(Hausen, Stevic, Warner, 1986). An important part of terminating a session is setting 
up the next appointment (Housen et.al. 1986). 
2. Termination of counselling relationship: Counselling relationships vary in 
length and purpose. The client and counsellor must agree or when termination of the 
relationship is appropriate and helpful. Various behaviour that may signal the end of 
the relationship are: 
· Verbal messages about readiness to terminate 
· Decrease in intensity of work between the client and counsellor 
· Use of more humour or intellectualizing, and less denial, withdrawal, anger, mourning 
or dependence (Corey et al, 1979) 
  Hackney and Cormier (1988) believe that in a relationship that has lasted more 
than three months, the final three to four weeks should be spent discussing the importance of 
termination. Maholick and Turner (1979) suggests specific areas of concern when deciding 
whether to terminate counselling. They are: 
· Examining whether the client’s initial problem, symptoms and stress – producing 
feelings have been reduced or eliminated 
· Assessing whether the client’s copying ability and degree of understanding and 
relating to self and others has improved 
· Examining whether the client has acquired abilities to plan and work productively and 
can enjoy life 
Two other ways to facilitate the ending of the client-counsellor relationship are: 
16 
 
· Fading: by spacing the counselling sessions apart or simply shortening then (Cormer 
and Cormer, 1986). 
· By helping client to develop more successful problem -solving skills (Dixon and 
Glover,1984). 
 3. Resistance to terminate: Patterson and Eisenberg (1983) note that resistance 
is especially likely when the counselling relationship has been of long duration or has 
involved a high level of intimacy. Other factors that promote resistance include —losses, 
loneliness, unresolved grief, need gratification, fear of rejection and fear of having to be self -
reliant. Client resistance may be expressed in various ways like asking for more time at the 
end of a session or asking for more sessions once a goal has been reached or developing new 
problems like depression or anxiety that were not part of the client’s original concern. These 
resistances may be overcome gradually and slowly in various ways (Cormier and 
Cormier,1991). Some of the ways are as follows: 
·            By making the sessions less frequent 
· By highlighting the client’s skills, abilities and available resources 
· By prescribing a limited number of future session and / or concentrate with clients on 
how he or she will set themselves up for relapse (Anderson and Stewart, 1983) 
· For clients who have had traumatic past experience, fear of change may lead to client 
resistance Exploring with them the advantages of working in other therapeutic 
settings, such as support groups may seen helpful (Lerner and Lerner,1983). 
4. Counsellor’s resistance:  Goodyear (1981) lists eight conditions under which 
termination may be especially difficult for counsellor, for example: 
· It signals the end of a significant relationship. 
· It arouses the counsellor’s anxiety about the client’s ability to function independently. 
· It arouses guilt in the counsellor about not having been more effective with the client. 
· When the counsellor’s professional self-concept is threatened by the client who leaves 
abruptly and angrily 
· It signals the end of a learning experience for the counsellor (e.g. when the counsellor 
may be relying on the client to learn more about the dynamics of a disorder 
· It signals the end of a particularly exciting experiencing of living vicariously through 
the adventures of the client. 
· It becomes a symbolic recapitulation of other (especially unresolved) farewells in the 
counsellor’s life. 
· It arouses in the counsellor, conflicts about his or her own individuation.” 
To deal with it, the counsellor should seek consultation from colleagues or 
should himself or herself undergo counselling (Kovacs, 1965, 1976). 
 5. Premature termination: May occur under several circumstances as follows: 
17 
 
· Client is little motivated or committed to change his present circumstances (Baruth 
and Huber, 1985) 
· It may be expressed directly or indirectly by missing or being late for appointments 
Hausen, Warner and Smith (1980), suggest that topic of premature treatment should 
be discussed openly between client and counsellor so as examine the thoughts and 
feeling of client and counsellor and a premature ending can often be prevented. 
On finding that client wishes to quit the counsellor should set up an exit interview. 
Four possible benefits of such as interview according to Ward (1984) are: 
· It helps the clients resolve any positive feelings resulting from the counselling 
experiences itself. 
· It serves as a way to invite the client to continue in counselling if the client so wishes. 
· Another form of treatment or a different counsellor can be considered if the client 
wishes.  
· It increases chances that next time the client needs help, he or she will seek 
counselling. 
Cavanagh (1982) holds that in premature termination, counsellor often make one of 
the two mistakes: 
· They either blame themselves or the client for what is happening. 
· They may develop a cavalier attitude i.e. ‘it is too bad this client has chosen not to 
continue counselling, but I have got other clients too’. 
According to Cavanagh (1982) certain reasons why a client sometimes 
terminates prematurely are: 
· To see if the counsellor cares 
· To elicit positive feelings from the counsellor 
· To punish or try to hurt the counsellor 
· To eliminate anxiety 
· To show the counsellor that client has found a cure elsewhere 
· To express to the counsellor that the client does not feel understood 
Cavanagh (1982) believes that counsellors need to understand that regardless 
of what they do, some clients terminate counselling prematurely. 
6. Counsellor-initiated termination: According to Cavanagh (1982) some of 
the good reasons for the counsellor to terminate are: 
· Illness 
· Working through counter transference 
· Relocation to another area 
18 
 
· An extended trip  
· The realization that client needs could be better served by someone else. 
  Some of the poor reasons (Cavanagh,1982) are: 
· Counsellor’s feelings of anger, boredom, or anxiety which may make the client feel 
rejected and even worse than in the beginning. 
· The Counsellor may have negative feelings about the client 
London (1982) and Seligman (1981) stress that clients and counsellors should 
prepare as far as possible in advance for temporary termination by openly discussing 
about it among each others. 
7. Ending on a positive note: It is important that termination be mutually agreed 
upon, as far as possible. Patterson and Eisenberg (1983) suggests 8 guidelines for 
ending an intense counselling relationship on a positive note: 
· Be clearly aware of the client’s needs and wants 
· Be clearly aware of the counsellor’s needs and wants 
· Be clearly aware of one’s previous experiences with separation and one’s inner 
reactions to these experiences in order to avoid counter-transference. 
· Be aware of the client’s feelings about ending the experience 
· Share honestly with the client how the counsellor feels about the counselling 
experience 
· Reviewing and bringing the major events of counselling experience into the present to 
help client see what he has accomplished 
· Supportively acknowledge the changes the client has made and actively encourage 
him/her maintain it. 
· Invite the client to keep up -to-date on what is happening in his or her life through 
follow-up sessions 
8. Follow-up: reinforces the gains the client has made in counselling and helps 
both the client and the counsellor to re -evaluate the experience. Follow -up may take 
the following four forms as follows: 
· Inviting the client in for a session 
· Through a telephone call to the client 
· Send a letter to the client asking about his current status 
· Mailing the client a questionnaire dealing with current levels of functioning 
9. Referrals and recycling: referrals are made when counsellors are not able to 
help certain clients. Some of the reasons behind referring are as follows: 
· Client has a problem that counsellor does not know how to handle 
19 
 
· Counsellor is inexperienced in a particular area and does not have the necessary skill 
to help the client 
· Counsellor knows an expert who would be more helpful to the client 
· Counsellor and client has incompatible personalities 
· Relationship between the two is stuck in an initial phase of counselling  
  A client may resist a referral if he feels rejected by the counsellor. Patterson 
and Eisenberg (1983) suggest that a counsellor spend at least one session with the client in 
preparation for the referral. Also, the longer the client works with the counsellor the more 
reluctant he or she will be towards a referral. 
  However, recycling is basically the re -examination of all phases of the 
therapeutic process (Baurth and Huber, 1985). It may be that goals were not properly defined 
or an inappropriate strategy was chosen. Recycling gives both the client and the counsellor a 
second chance to achieve what each wants—a positive change. 
1.4.3 Stages of the Counselling Process 
Rogers (1958), examined the process of counselling in terms of the stages of development by 
which personality change take place. He concluded that, in successful counselling, the client 
moves from fixity to changeableness; from rigid structures to flow; from stasis to process. He 
pin-pointed seven crucial stages of the change process, as given below: 
 ·First stage: At the first stage, internal communication is blocked. There is no 
communication of self or personal meanings, no recognition of problems and usually no 
individual desire to change. At this stage, the client is closed, and communicative 
relationships are construed as dangerous as there is no desire to change. 
 ·Second stage: When the client feels himself to be fully accepted as she or he is the 
second stage follows naturally. The second stage is characterized by a number of factors, both 
positive and negative. Expression begins to flow in regard to non -self topics; problems are 
perceived as external to self; there is no sense of personal responsibility in problems; feelings 
are described as un -owned, or sometimes as past objects; feeling may be exhibited, but are 
not recognized as such or owned; experiencing is bound by the structure of the past; personal 
constructs are rigid, and unrecognized as being constructs, but are thoughts of as facts; 
differentiation of personal meanings and feelings are very limited and contradictions may be 
expressed, but with little recognition of them as contradiction. 
 ·Third and fourth stage: The third and fourth stages involve further loosening of 
symbolic expression in regard to feelings, constructs and self. These stages constitute an 
important moving forward in the process.  
 ·Fifth stage: In the fifth stage, feelings are expressed freely as being in the present 
and are very close to being experienced. Feelings tend to ‘bubble up,’ or ‘seep through,’ in 
spite of the fear and distrust which the clients feels at experiencing them with fullness and 
immediacy. There is a beginning tendency to realize that experiencing a feeling involves a 
direct referent and there is an increasing ownership of self feeling, and a desire to be these, to 
be the real self. 
20 
 
 ·Sixth stage: The sixth stage continues this process of growth, self -discovery and a 
self-acceptance, congruence, and responsibility. This is a crucial stage as at this point, the 
client has become very close to an organic being that is always in the process of growth; he or 
she is in touch with the flow of feelings; his or her construction of experiences is free flowing 
and repeatedly being tested against referents and evidence within and without; experience is 
differentiated and thus internal communication is exact. 
 ·Seventh stage: The client often enters the seventh and last stage without the need of 
the counsellor’s help. The client is now a continually changing person, experiencing with 
freshness and immediacy each new situation, responding with real and accepted feelings, and 
showing ‘a growing and continuing sense of acceptant ownership of these changing feelings, 
a basic trust in his own process’. This seven -stage conceptualization represents Roger’s 
clearest, most explicit description of the stages of personal growth, although these stages are 
implicit throughout his writings.

1.5 Role of Counsellors 
People become counsellors for many reasons —some functional and some dysfunctional. 
Some of the dysfunctional reasons behind becoming a counsellor according to James Guy 
(1987) are as follows: 
 ·Emotional Distress: Some individuals who have unresolved personal traumas wish 
to become counsellors in order to deal with their emotional distress. 
 ·Vicarious coping: Some people take up the profession of counselling as a way to 
live their lives through others. 
 ·Loneliness and isolation: Some people take up the profession of counselling as a 
way of dealing with their loneliness and isolation and to make friends 
 ·A desire for power:  Individuals who feel frightened and insecure in their personal 
lives try to seek to control others and hence take up the profession of counselling 
 ·A need for love: Sometimes narcissistic and grandiose people take up the profession 
of counselling believing that all problems are resolved through the expression of love and 
tenderness. 
 ·Vicarious rebellion: Some individuals who have unresolved anger, at times take up 
the profession of counselling and act out the anger through their client’s defiant behaviours. 
However, some of the functional reasons behind becoming a counsellor are the realization 
people have that, they possess the necessary skills to become a counsellor as mentioned 
below (James Guy, 1987): 
 ·Curiosity and inquisitiveness: Refers to a natural interest in people 
21 
 
 ·An ability to listen: People who possess this skill tend to find listening   stimulating 
 ·A comfort with conversation: Means that these people enjoy verbal exchanges  
 ·Empathy and understanding: People who possess this skill are able to put 
themselves in other people’s place 
 ·Emotional insightfulness:  People who possess this skill are comfortable dealing 
with a wide range of feelings. 
 ·Introspection: People who possess this skill have the ability to see or feel 
themselves from within 
 ·Capacity of self -denial: Refers to one’s ability to set aside their needs in order to 
listen  
 ·Tolerance of intimacy:  Indicates that the individual has the ability to sustain 
emotional closeness 
·Comfort with power: Suggests that the person has the ability to accept  power with 
a certain degree of detachment. 
·Ability to laugh: People who possess this skill can see the tragic or comic quality of 
life events. 
No matter whatever, be the reason behind becoming a counsellor, studying the techniques and 
theories associated with counselling certainly helps. For instance, Cavanagh (1982) pointed 
out that by studying counselling one may gain insight one’s own life, obtain knowledge and 
skill in relating to others and come to realize how the counselling process works. Overall, the 
effectiveness of counselling depends on numerous variables including the background, 
education, skill and experience of the counsellor. This also brings us to the question of—what 
qualities are indicative of the potential and actual effectiveness of a counsellor? It is 
important to answer this question for several reasons as given below: 
· It helps us better understand the nexus of subtle factors which contribute to the 
success of counselling 
· It is important to counsellor educators who must make crucial decisions in the 
selection and training of students of counselling. 
· It encourages aspiring counsellors to find within themselves and strengthen those 
qualities which have been indicated as predictors of successful counselling 
· It helps in developing techniques and strategies which are derivatives of effective 
counsellor qualities 
· It assists trainers and researchers in determining the probable outcomes of specific 
counselling interactions, based on tested criteria of counselling effectiveness. 
Much related to the above-mentioned question, is another question that is —Is there in fact 
such a thing as an effective counsellor, or are different counsellors effective for different 
reasons to different degrees with different clients? Research over these years has found 
evidence to support both the positions. While some studies have shown that there are 
22 
 
universally important counsellor qualities, other studies have indicated that certain traits work 
well with some clients but not with others. No matter what, one cannot deny the fact, that the 
counsellor’s traits play a vital part in the counsellor interaction and can be used as 
determinants of counsellor effectiveness. 
Corey, Corey and Callanan (1988) have observed that a counsellor’s values, beliefs, personal 
attributes, life experiences and way of living are intrinsically related to the way he or she 
functions as a professional. They identified ten personal qualities that are found in effective 
counsellors as mentioned below: 
· A sincere interest in the welfare of others 
· The ability to be present for others: The ability and willingness to be with them in 
their experiences of joy and pain. 
· A recognition and acceptance of one’s personal power: Being in contact with one’s 
own strength and vitality; without need to diminish others or feel superior to them. 
· The knowledge that one has found his or her own counselling style, that style being an 
expression of one’s own personality 
· A willingness to be vulnerable and to take risks 
· Self-respect and self-appreciation: A strong sense of self-worth 
· A willingness to serve as models for their clients 
· A willingness to risk making mistakes and to admit having made them 
· A growth orientation 
· A sense of humour 
In addition to the above-mentioned qualities, an effective counsellor should also have the 
ability to promote the welfare of the client and should be open -minded and flexible. By this 
we mean, that the counsellor should have the capacity to hear, believe, and respond in an 
open and non -judgemental fashion to clients who may have values, belief, and lifestyles 
different from his/her own. This quality is especially important in being able to work 
effectively with clients of the opposite gender or of a different ethnic origin and with clients 
with alternative lifestyles including sexual orientation. 
According to Carl Rogers; empathy, genuineness, and unconditional positive regard are three 
major qualities of an effective counsellor. In a massive research effort, Rogers and his 
colleagues (Rogers et al, 1967) found that regardless of the method or technique used by the 
therapist, effective therapy would take place, if the therapist fulfils the following three 
conditions: 
· The therapist responds as the real person he actually is in this relationship at the 
moment. He/she employs no artificial front (genuineness) 
· The therapist senses and expresses the client’s felt meaning, catching what the client 
communicates as it seems to the client (empathy) 
23 
 
· The therapist experiences a warm and positive acceptance towards the client 
(unconditional positive regard) 
Rollo May and others believe that individual who have experience some emotional distress 
and have been able to successfully resolve it are better able to connect with their clients 
authentically in ways not possible otherwise. 
Unlike Rollo May, Cormier and Cormier (1985), the most effective helper is one who has 
achieved a balance of interpersonal and technical competence. According to them, the six 
qualities of an effective counsellor are as follows: 
· Intellectual competence:  Counsellors must have a thorough knowledge of many 
theories, as well as the desire and ability to learn. 
· Energy: Counselling is emotionally draining and physically demanding. Counsellors 
need to have the ability to be active in their sessions. 
· Flexibility: Effective counsellors are not tied to one specific theory or set of methods. 
Instead they adapt what they do to meet the needs of their clients. 
· Support: The counsellor supports the client in making his or her own decisions helps 
engender hope and power avoids trying to rescue the client. 
· Goodwill: The nature of good will encompasses such qualities as the counsellor’s 
desire to work on behalf of the client in a constructive way that ethically promotes 
client independence. 
· Self-awareness: This quality includes knowledge of one’s self, including attitudes 
and feelings about self and the ability to recognize how and what factors affect those 
attitudes and feelings. 
According to Holland (1977), specific personality types are attracted to certain work 
environments and correspond best with those environments. The environment in which a 
counsellor works is primarily a social and problem -oriented one. It calls for skill in 
interpersonal relationships and for creativity. Wiggins and Weslander (1979) found empirical 
support for Holland’s hypothesis. They found in general that those counsellors who were 
rated ‘highly effective’, scored highest on the social and artistic scales of the vocational 
preference inventory (Holland, 1977). Counsellors who were rated ineffective, in general, 
scored highest on the realistic and conventional scales of this test. Other factors like sex, age 
and level of education, were found to be significant in predicting effectiveness. 
Counsellor’s effectiveness is also strongly affected by certain personality related factors like 
stability, harmony, constancy and purpose. According to Carkhuff and Berenson, 1967 and 
Kottler, 1986, the potency of counselling is related to the personal togetherness of counsellors 
and to their ability to be present with their clients. 
It has also been found that counsellors who have healthy personal lives and who learn from 
their mistakes as well as their success are more likely than others to grow therapeutically and 
to be able to sensitively concentrate on their clients problems to the fullest (Cormmier, 1988). 
24 
 
According to Belkin, an effective counsellor should possess the following three qualities —
knowing oneself; understanding others, and relating to others. The details of the same are 
given below: 
1. Knowing oneself: Before a counsellor can attempt to understand a client, he or she 
must have an objective and satisfying understanding of the self. Counsellors must be able to 
recognize and accept their strengths and weaknesses. In this regard, three personality 
characteristics most essential to effective counsellor are —security, trust and courage to 
counsel. 
· Security: The pre-requisite to security are self -confidence and self -respect. A secure 
counsellor is one who is free from fear and anxiety and is able to maintain a flexible and 
objective view of self. In one study, Milliken and Kirchner (1971), found that the “more 
anxious a counsellor was, the less accurate was his or her ability to recall words spoken and 
feelings expressed in simulated interviews.” Insecure counsellors tended to act more 
defensively with their clients than do secure counsellors.  This happens so, as insecure 
counsellors fear the client’s anger and rejection and consequently attempt more to please than 
to help the client. Insecure counsellors may also take advantage of their clients, manipulating 
a client into meeting the unhealthy needs of the counsellor. Unlike insecure counsellors, 
secure counsellors are more likely to allow their clients to be themselves. 
· Trust: it is believed that people who experience difficulty trusting others are usually 
themselves untrustworthy. Therefore, a counsellor who is suspicious, who questions every 
person’s motives, whose cynicism colours all interactions, is unlikely to relate to clients in a 
manner that contributes to growth and adjustment. Usually, untrusting counsellors tend to 
attribute feelings and ideas to clients, not because the client has expressed them, but rather 
because the counsellor considers such ideas and feelings common to all people. 
· The courage to counsel: a committed counsellor must be willing to bear the often 
unjustified brunt of the client’s anger. The counsellor must be willing to feel a profound and 
distressing sense of aloneness as the client progressing during the treatment, goes away and 
becomes autonomous. 
2. Understanding others: Requires open -mindedness, a non -judgmental attitude, 
sensitivity, empathy, and objectivity. 
· Open-mindedness: Refers to freedom from fixed pre -conceptions and an attitude of 
open receptivity to that which the client is expressing. The open -minded counsellor is able to 
accommodate the client’s values, insights, feelings and perceptions that are different from his 
or her own. Moreover, he or she is able to experience and interact with the client throughout a 
wide breath and range of feelings. Open -mindedness also implies the ability to listen, to 
respond, to interact with the client, free from the constraints of imposing value criteria. 
According to Anderson, Lepper and Ross (1980) a person who is not open-minded is likely to 
persist in believing incorrect things about a client even in the face of contradicting evidence. 
Thus open-mindedness is necessary for honest communication. 
· A non-judgemental attitude: An open-minded counsellor is also non -judgemental. It 
means that the effective counsellor should have a well developed and meaningful sense of 
values with which he or she feels satisfied. Adopting a non -judgemental attitude means that 
25 
 
the counsellor refrains from judging the guilt or innocence of the client, it does not mean that 
the counsellor may not objectively judge the attitudes, standards or actions of the client. 
· Sensitivity: Refers to a cognitive and an emotional response to the client as a total 
individual. It allows a counsellor to make a deeper and a spontaneous response to the needs, 
feelings, conflicts, doubts and so on of the client. 
· Empathy: It refers to an individual’s ability to put himself or herself emotionally and 
intellectually in the client’s place, be the client momentarily, think and feel as the client does. 
It requires that the counsellor must be able to maintain an individual identity and remain 
sensitively aware of the differences between himself or herself and the client. 
· Objectivity: Means to be able to stand back and observe what is happening from a 
neutral or non-imposing frame of reference. 
 3. Relating to others:  The qualities that help a counsellor to relate and 
communicate more effectively with the client are — genuineness, non-dominance, listening, 
positive regard and communication skills. 
· Genuineness: According to Rogers (1957), it means that within the relationship the 
counsellor is freely and deeply himself, with his actual experience accurately represented by 
his awareness of himself. The genuine counsellor, minimizes dependence upon roles and 
increases giving of self to the client. The genuine counsellor is open, honest and at all times 
herself or himself. 
· Non-dominance: The non-dominant counsellor is one who is capable of sitting back 
and allowing the client to initiate and direct the course of the counselling interview. 
Remaining non -dominant may produce tension and anxiety in the counsellor. Hence, non -
dominance appears as the ability to listen. 
· Listening: It is related to open -mindedness and sensitivity. It means sitting back, 
paying attention, not interrupting and not attempting to direct what client s are saying. It is 
related to non-dominance. 
· Positive regards: According to Rogers (1962) it means prizing the client as a person 
regardless of his particular behaviour at the moment. It is based on the assumption that 
“growth and change are more likely to occur, the more that the counsellor is experiencing a 
warm, positive, acceptant attitude toward what is the client.” It is a genuine and sincere 
feeling of affection for clients. It is an attitude that inevitably makes the client feels more 
secure, more worthwhile as a person, more willing to grow and prosper. It is often expressed 
as acceptance and warmth. 
· Communication skills: Since counselling is a verbal process, therefore the counsellor 
needs to have good communication skills. The counsellor needs to have a proficient 
language, good vocabulary, acceptable speech patterns and the like, and he should be able to 
direct communication to levels for which they are intended. One criterion of good 
communication is the ability to anticipate the effect that words will have on a client, to know 
in advance, before saying them, the inferences, denotations and connotations of the words 
used and the messages transmitted. A good counsellor is often concerned with the shades and 
levels of meaning each word has in their client’s thinking. 
26 
 
To conclude, one can say that it is very difficult to find a person who possesses all the 
qualities of a perfect counsellor. Temperament, background, and experience, make some  
better suited to become counsellors than others. Those persons whose personal traits match 
the demands of the profession are more likely to be personally and professionally satisfied 
with the role of counsellor.  
Most studies agree that personnel traits are important in determining counsellor’s 
effectiveness for e.g., Jovne (1981) found that counsellor’s personality characteristics are 
more important than skills. Murphy Etal (1975) also found a significant co -relation between 
counsellor’s characteristics and effectiveness.  
However, Messer and Boals (1981) in one study found out that if brief training is provided to 
individuals whose personalities make them suitable as counsellors, they can perform 
effectively.  
In addition to the training a counsellor receives, the counselling process also gets affected by 
the personality of the counsellor i.e. the kind of person the counsellor is. It can have either a 
functional or detrimental effect on the counselling process.  
Counsellors are real people with their own idiosyncratic natures, their own set of particular 
needs, their own values, problems conflicts, any of which may at times work to the detriment 
of the counselling process. Counsellor’s problems, perceptions, feelings and values exert a 
continuing influence upon the counselling process. It would be ideal if the counsellor puts 
aside all the personal problems and adjustment difficulties before entering the counselling 
room. Thus for counselling to be effective, the counsellors must have their own life in order. 
Some of the problems that counsellors face which may be detrimental to the counselling 
process are as follows :  
· Meaninglessness: A counsellor who carries a sense of meaninglessness is likely to 
restrict the emotional interchanges with others which may prevent him or her from 
experiencing life to the fullest. Meaninglessness is a serious obstacle in the counsellor’s 
striving for effectiveness in counselling exchanges with the client.  
· Alienation: It assumes many forms including a deep sense of isolation and loneliness, 
a loss of coherent sense of self and separation from others. 
· Burnout: It is characterized by loss of feelings for one’s work, despair of improvising 
the situation and a poor quality of one’s life. A counsellor experiencing burnout may also feel 
unable to cope with personal tasks and may experience pervasive physical exhaustion 
(Fimian, 1987). Thus burnout means becoming emotionally and physically drained to the 
point that they cannot perform meaningfully. Therefore, a number of researchers like Boy and 
Pine, 1980 etc., suggest ways counsellors can avoid or treat burnout. Some of these ways are 
associating with healthy individuals; working with committed colleagues and with 
organizations that have a sense of mission; being reasonably committed to the theory of 
counselling; using stress reduction exercises; modifying environmental stresses; engaging in 
self assessment; periodically reviewing and clarifying counsellor’s role, expectations and 
beliefs; obtaining personal therapy; setting aside free and private time; maintaining an 
attitude of detached concern when working with clients and last but not the least, retaining an 
attitude of hope. 
27
