· Loss of freedom: Freedom here means feeling free from inside. Feeling free refers to 
having a sense of being in control of one’s actions and of not being determined by outside 
forces. It seems to be increasingly threatened by the stresses of modern life, by the demands 
of impersonal bureaucracies, by the pressure of work, by our own aspirations to savour life 
and our talents to the fullest; and even occasionally, by the claims made on us by those we 
have. 
The above mentioned variables directly threaten the counsellor’s professional efficacy and 
personal life style. In addition to these variables, the counsellor’s values also affect the 
process of counselling as the counsellor’s professional qualities are always coloured by 
personal values and perceptions. Different types of values are as follows: 
 · Cognitive values: States that one thing is better or worse than the other. For 
example, a counsellor may believe that behavioural counselling is more effective in working 
with stutterers than is Rogerian counselling. The more the individual believes that a particular 
value is a cognitive value, the less likely he or she is to change that value. 
 · Preference values: They are clearly not objective, but rather are indicative of 
a personal preference. Individuals holding preference values recognize that they are values 
and not facts thus they are more likely to tolerate in other values which are different from 
their own. 
 · Moral values:  They are based on higher principles of conduct. They are 
taught to us when we are very young and although we are able to modify what we have 
earned, many of the moral “ism” instilled within us hold throughout our lives. 
 · Self-values: These represent our feelings of self -esteem and self -importance. 
Although some sociologists argue that this value is culturally determined, a more popular 
position is that one’s self-esteem is more dependent upon early upbringing, particularly upon 
the relationship with one’s parents. In either case, one’s feelings about oneself deeply 
influence choices, perceptions and judgements. 
 · Cultural values:  These overlap the other four categories, but they differ in 
that they are shared social values rather than individual values. Cultural values are so subtle, 
they often elude our rational ability to evaluate and react objectively. 
 The counsellors like any other person have values in each of these categories, and 
these values together help constitute the counsellor’s world view and influence his or her 
counselling activities in manifold ways. In short, the effective counsellor has to be a well 
constructed, well balanced, fully functional mature adult, capable of integrating into himself 
or herself a myriad of values, problems and existential conflicts. 
 Counselling is a process that involves interpersonal relationships between a therapist 
and one or more clients by which the former employs psychological methods based on 
systematic knowledge of the human personality in attempting to improve mental health of the 
latter (Patterson, 1959). 
 Building a relationship, the first stage of counselling, is a continuous process. It 
begins by having the counsellor win the battle for structure and the client win the battle for 
initiative. Here the counsellor aims to create an atmosphere where the client is comfortable 
and not hesitate to share his thoughts, feelings and problems. 
28 
 
 Clients and counsellors some times have different perceptions about the purpose and 
nature of counselling. Clients often do not know what to expect from the process or how they 
should act (Riordan, Matheny and Harris, 1978). In order to help clients gain new directions 
in their lives, counsellors provide constructive guidelines or establish structure on the basis of 
their theoretical “orientation to counselling, the personalities of their clients and the major 
problem areas with which they will deal.” Practical guidelines like fee structure, time limit 
(such as 50 minute session), action limits (for the prevention of destructive behaviour), role 
limits (what will be expected of with participant) and procedural limits (in which the client is 
given the responsibility to work on specific goals or needs) are part of building structure 
(Brammer and Shostrom,1977; Goodyear and Bradley,1980; Kelly and Stone,1982). 
 Through confrontation, persuasion (Kerr, Claiborn and Dixon 1982; Senour, 1982), 
acceptance, patience, understanding and by anticipating ‘anger, frustration and defensiveness’ 
that some clients display (Ritchie, 1986) counsellors can motivate reluctant or resistant 
clients to change. Also Sack (1988) recommends the use of pragmatic techniques such as 
‘silence, reflection, questioning, describing, assessing, pretending and sharing the 
counsellor’s perspective as ways to overcome client resistance’. 
 Counselling may occur in any setting, but some circumstances are more likely than 
others to promote its development. For example, a comfortable and attractive room endowed 
with soft lightening, quiet colour, harmonious, comfortable furniture, and an absence of 
clutter and distraction (Bhertzer and Stane, 1980). 
 A distance of 30 to 39 inches between the counsellor and client (Haase, 1970) and 
chair of 90 degree angel from one another (Benjamin, 1981) promote counselling 
relationship. But comfort level is influenced by cultural background, gender and the nature of 
the relationship (Shatzer and Stone, 1980; Sidski, 1979). 
 The perception of clients and counsellors of one another is very important. Clients 
who are ‘young, attractive, verbal, intelligent and successful (YA VIS) (Schofield,1964)’ may 
be treated in a much more positive way than clients who are ‘homely, old, unintelligent, non -
verbal and disadvantaged (HOUND) (Allen, 1977)’. Clients are likely to work best with 
counsellors perceived as trustworthy, attractive and knowledgeable (Strong, 1968) and 
showing commitment (Carkhuff and Berenson, 1969). 
 Regardless of the external circumstances and the initial perceptions, a counsellor who 
attends to the verbal and nonverbal expressions of a client is more likely to establish rapport. 
The relationship may be further enhanced by the counsellor’s conveying of empathy and 
other helpful skills like re -statement and reflection. When counsellors are attuned to their 
own values and feelings, they are able to be even more effective. 
Check Your Progress 
 7. State any two dysfunctional reasons behind becoming a counsellor.  
 8. What should be the layout of a place for counselling?  
 
 
 
29 
 
1.6 Approaches to Counselling and Helping 
An analysis of a set of facts in relation to each other is better known as a ‘theory’ has an 
effect on how “client communication is conceptualized, new interpersonal relationships 
develop, how professional ethics are implemented, and how counsellor’s view themselves as 
professionals” (Corey, Neukrug, Mc Aulilife, Prate and Lovell, 1990). Without theoretical 
packing, counsellors’ operate haphazardly in a trial -and-error manner and risk being both 
ineffective and harmful. 
 According to Shostrom (1977) “theory helps to explain what happens in a counselling 
relationship and assists the counsellor in predicting, evaluating, and improving results. It 
provides a framework for making scientific observation about counselling. Theorizing 
encourages the coherence of ideas about counselling and the production of new ideas.” 
Hence, counselling theory can be very practical in the sense that they help to make sense of 
the counsellor’s observation in work and thus provide direction. 
Boy and Fine (1983) further state that theory is the why behind the how of counsellor roles. It 
provides a framework within which a counsellor can operate. Boy and Fine have given the 
following six functions of theory that make counselling practical: 
· Theory helps counsellors find unity and relatedness within the diversity of 
existence. 
· Theory compels counsellors to examine relationships they could otherwise 
overlook. 
· Theory gives counsellors operational guidelines by which to work and helps 
them in evaluating their development as professionals. 
· Theory helps counsellors focus on relevant data and tells them what to look 
for. 
· Theory helps counsellors assist clients in the effective modification of their 
behaviour. 
· Theory helps counsellors evaluate both old and new approaches to the process 
of counselling. It is the base from which new counselling approaches are 
constructed. 
 The ultimate criterion for all counselling theories is how well they provide 
explanations of what occurs in counselling (Kelly,1988) and to what degree they organize 
information which is grounded in the reality of people’s lives (Young,1988). 
 In short, an effective counsellor decides which theory or theories and methods to use 
on the basis of educational background, philosophy, and the needs of the clients. All 
approaches are not appropriate for all counsellors or clients, but a theoretical base is very 
important for every effective counsellor. 
1.6.1 Psychodynamic Counselling 
Some of the proponents of psychodynamic counselling are discussed here.  
 
30 
 
Sigmund Freud 
Freud was the creator of the psychoanalytic approach. The Freudian view of human nature is 
essentially pessimistic, deterministic, mechanistic, and reductionalistic. According to Freud, 
‘human beings are determined by irrational forces, unconscious motivations, biological and 
instinctual needs and drives, and psychosexual events (stages) during the first five years of 
life’. 
Humans are viewed as energy system. According to the orthodox Freudian view, “the 
dynamics of personality consist of the ways in which psychic energy is distributed to the id, 
ego, and superego. Since the amount of energy is limited, one system gains control over the 
available energy at the expense of the other two systems. Behaviour is determined by this 
psychic energy.” 
Freud also emphasized the role of instinct. All instincts are innate and biological. Freud 
stressed the sexual instincts and the aggressive impulses. He saw all human behaviour as 
determined by the desire to gain pleasure and avoid pain. Humans have both life instincts 
(Eros), and death instincts (Thanatos). According to Freud, ‘the goal of all life is death; life is 
but a roundabout way to death’. 
The primary goal of analytic therapy is to reform the individual’s character structure by 
making the unconscious (repressed memories and wishes), conscious in the client. The 
therapeutic process focuses on re -living childhood experiences and assisting the client is 
working through a developmental stage not previously resolved. Past experiences are re -
structured, discussed, analyzed, and interpreted with the aim of personality reconstruction. 
These goals of psychoanalysis, which vary according to the client, focus mainly on personal 
adjustment, usually including a reorganization of internal focuses within the person and 
stresses environmental adjustment, especially in the areas of work and intimacy. 
A final goal of psychoanalysis is helping clients cope with the demands of the society in 
which they live by strengthening the ego so that perceptions and plans become more realistic. 
To accomplish these goals the psychoanalysts function as experts. They encourage their 
clients to talk about whatever comes to their mind, especially about childhood experiences. 
The therapist remains anonymous and engages in very little sharing of his or her own feelings 
and experiences, so that the client projects onto the analyst. The client’s projections are 
interpreted and analyzed. 
To create an atmosphere in which the client feels free to express difficult thoughts, the 
psychoanalyst after a few ‘face -to-face’ sessions, often has the client lie down on a couch 
while he/she remains out of view (usually seated behind the client’s head). The analyst listens 
for ‘gaps and inconsistencies in the client’s story, infers the meaning of the client’s reported 
dreams and free associations, carefully observes the client during the therapy session, and 
remains sensitive to clues concerning the client’s feelings toward the analyst’. 
The analyst’s role is to let clients gain insight by re -living and working through the 
unresolved past experiences that come into focus during sessions. The development of 
transference is encouraged to help client deal realistically with unconscious material. ‘The 
analyst becomes a current substitute for significant others in the client’s life’. The analyst 
pays particular attention to the resistance of the client and interprets it for the client. 
31 
 
The analyst is concerned mainly with assisting the client in gaining awareness of self, 
honestly and more effective personal relationships, in dealing with anxiety realistically and in 
gaining control over impulsive and irrational behaviour. The analyst is expected to be 
relatively objective as he or she receives the anger, love, adulation, criticism, and other 
intense feelings of the client. 
During therapy the client progresses, through certain stages —developing a growing 
relationship with the analyst, experiencing treatment crisis, gaining insight into his or her past 
and unconscious, developing resistance to learning more about himself or herself, developing 
a transference relationship with the analyst, deepening the therapy, ‘working through’ the 
resistance and uncovered material, and termination of therapy. ‘The client must be willing to 
commit himself or herself to an intensive and long -term therapy process’. Typically, a client 
comes to therapy several times weekly for a period of three to five years. The session usually 
last an hour. The client is typically ‘asked not to make any radical changes in his or her 
lifestyle during the period of analysis’. 
Overall, the counsellor employs both active and passive techniques. The following are five 
most prominent psychoanalytic techniques: 
 · Free association: Is a method of revealing past experiences and of 
discharging the emotions associated with past traumatic situations. This is known as 
catharsis. The client is instructed by the analyst to clear his or her mind of day -to-day 
thoughts and preoccupations and to say whatever comes to mind, regardless of how painful, 
silly, trivial, illogical, or irrelevant it may be. 
 · Dream analysis:  Freud saw dream analysis as ‘the royal road to the 
unconscious’, —he believed that in dreams the unconscious wishes, needs, and fears are 
expressed. The counsellor is especially sensitive to two aspects of any dream —the manifest 
content (obvious and apparent meaning) and the latent content (hidden but true meaning). 
 · Analysis of resistance: Client’s resistance to therapeutic process may take 
many forms, such as, missing appointments, being late for appointments, not paying fees, 
persisting in transference, blocking thoughts during free association, or refusing to recall 
dreams or early memories. The analysts interpretation of the resistance is aimed at helping the 
client become aware of the reasons for the resistance so that he or she can deal with them. 
 · Analysis of transference:  The analyst encourages the transference neurosis 
by his or her neutrality, objectivity, anonymity, and relative passivity. Interpretation of the 
transference relationship enables the client to work through old conflicts that keep him or her 
presently fixated and that retard his or her emotional growth. In essence, the 
psychopathological effects of an undesirable early relationship are neutralized by working 
through a similar emotional conflict in the therapeutic relationship with the analyst. 
 · Interpretation: Encompasses explanations and analysis of a client’s thoughts, 
feelings, and actions.  The analyst points out, explains, and even teaches the client the 
meaning of the behaviour that is manifested by dreams, free association, resistances, and the 
therapeutic relationship itself. 
In addition, to the above -mentioned techniques, psychological assessment instruments, 
especially projective tests, are often employed. Psychoanalytic counsellors also use 
32 
 
diagnostic labels to help themselves classify clients and to develop appropriate treatment 
plans for them. 
But psychoanalysis has often been minimized as a technique applicable to the counselling 
setting. Its critics argue that it places too much emphasis on sexuality and early childhood 
experiences; is quite time consuming and expensive; does not deal directly with the problem; 
brings to the surface more repressed material than is necessary; works for only certain kinds 
of clients (for example, does not work with clients older than fifty years); and has many 
concepts that are not easily communicated or understood. Also, its appropriateness for 
females continues to be questioned. 
Despite these criticisms, variation of the basic psychoanalytic techniques has several 
applications in counselling as follows: 
· Understanding the resistances that take the form of cancellation of 
appointments, fleeing from therapy prematurely and refusing to look at oneself  
· Understanding the value and role of transference that occurs in many 
counselling relationships 
· Understanding how overuse of ego defences can keep a person from 
functioning effectively and recognizing the ways these ego defences operate 
both in the counselling relationship itself and in the client’s daily life 
· Group and family counsellors are deeply influenced by its basic instincts into 
family dynamics and transference 
· Several psychological tests like Thematic Apperception Test (TAT), Rorschach 
Ink-blot Test, which are sometimes used in counselling settings are rooted in 
psychoanalytic theory. 
It seems to be effective in treating several disorders like hysteria, narcissism, obsessive 
compulsive disorder, anxiety, phobia, sexual difficulties etc. (Arlow, 1989). Its methods 
dream analysis, free association are extensively used and are valid till date. In short, this 
approach has grown and developed through the years and continues to evolve. 
Adler  
Adler’s psychology is also known as individual psychology as he focused on the evolution of 
a style of life and its effects upon individual behaviour. ‘Style of life’ is the  principle scheme 
governing the individual’s personality. It explains the uniqueness of the person. It is this 
uniqueness that departs Adler’s psychology from classical psychoanalysis. Adler considered 
each person to be a “unique configuration of motives, traits, interests, and values.” 
According to Adler, the inferiority feelings (fanaticized or real) stimulate every person to 
attain the same goal of superiority. Different people adopt different ways to attain this goal 
which forms one’s style of life. This style of life determines how a person confronts the three 
life problems of adulthood, namely —social relations, occupation, love, and marriage. He 
described four different styles of life, each conceptualized in terms of the degree of social 
interest and activity, as given below: 
33 
 
 · The ruling type:  Refers to the style of life in which an individual is high in 
activity but low in social interest. Such people attempt to deal with life problems by 
dominating them. It corresponds to Hippocrates choleric type of personality. 
 · The getting type:  Refers to individuals who expect to be given everything 
they desire for. These individuals are low in social interest and activity. This type corresponds 
to Hippocrates phlegmatic type of personality. 
 · The avoiding type:  Refers to individuals who try to avoid defeat in life’s 
problems by avoiding the problems themselves. These individuals are also low in social 
interest and activity. It corresponds to Hippocrates melancholic type of personality. 
 · The socially useful type : Refers to those individuals who exhibit activity in 
the service of others. Such people confront the life tasks and attempt to resolve them in a 
manner consistent with the needs of other individuals. It corresponds to Hippocrates sanguine 
type of personality. 
Contrary to Freud, Adler theorized that the style of life is formed very early in childhood—by 
the age of four or five— and from there on experiences are assimilated and utilized according 
to this unique style of life. Attitudes, feelings, and apperceptions  become fixed and 
mechanized at an early age and thereafter it is almost impossible for the style of life to 
change albeit the ways of expressing them may change. 
Adler further believed that specific inferiorities, either imagined or real, that the person has, 
determines the style of life is. By the term inferiority Adler meant organ defectiveness or 
psychological or social disabilities. For instance, “Napoleon’s conquering style of life was 
determined by his slight physical stature, and Hitler’s craving for world domination was 
determined by his sexual impotence.” 
He observed that the person with a defective organ often tries to compensate for the weakness 
by strengthening it through intensive training. He equated inferiority with unmanliness or 
feminity, the compensation for which was called the ‘masculine protest’. Later he stated that 
feelings of inferiority arise from a sense of incompletion or imperfection in any sphere of life. 
He contended that inferiority feelings are not a sign of abnormality; they are the cause of all 
improvement in the human lot. 
Although he believed that inferiority feelings were painful, but relief from these feelings was 
not always necessarily pleasurable. Perfection not pleasure was for him the goal of life. 
Adler regarded social interest to be innate in nature and believed that humans were social 
creature by nature, not by habit. The aim is to attain a perfect society, which includes matters 
like cooperation, interpersonal and social relations, identification with the group, empathy, 
and so forth. 
He believed that people are embedded in a social context from their first day of life. For 
instance, cooperation manifests itself in the relationship between the infant and the mother, 
and thereafter the child is continuously involved in a network of interpersonal relations that 
shape the personality and provide concrete outlets for striving for superiority. Striving for 
superiority becomes socialized; the idea of a perfect society takes the place of purely personal 
ambition and selfish gain. By working for common good, humans compensate for their 
individual weakness. 
34 
 
Unlike Freud, Adler believed that expectations of the future rather than experiences of the 
past motivated human beings.  He perceived man as largely a conscious and a creative 
person, whose actions are strongly driven by what he expects for himself in future. He further 
stated that “humans are conscious beings and are usually aware of the reasons for their 
behaviour. They are conscious of their inferiorities and conscious of the goals for which they 
strive. More than that, humans are self-conscious individuals capable of planning and guiding 
their actions with full awareness of their meaning for their self-realizations.”  
Adler also gave the concept of the creative self, which refers to the self a person makes based 
on hereditary factors and life experiences. This creative self is responsible for giving meaning 
to life, forming the goals as well as the means to the goal. He further believed that the 
neurotic rigidly over -compensate for perceived inferiorities in an individual, that is people 
develop neurotic symptoms as a way of protecting themselves against their feelings of 
inferiority and soon this becomes a vicious cycle. 
When individuals are not able to deal with their problems of life, then in order to protect 
themselves they develop safeguards, which are analogous to Freudian defense mechanisms. 
Adler gave the following three general categories of safeguards: 
 · Excuses: refer to any attempt to avoid blame for failures in life. 
 · Aggression: refers to blaming self or others for failures. 
· Distancing: includes procrastination, claims of helplessness or attempts to 
avoid problems 
Adler believed that complexes characterize the individual as schemata or personalities traits 
do. The various complexes given by him are as follows: 
· Inferiority complex:  refers to feelings of being significantly inferior from 
others. 
· Superiority complex: refers to feelings of being significantly superior from 
others. 
 · Redeemer complex: represents desire to save someone. 
 · Proof complex: refers to the fear of failure and the desire to be always right. 
 · Predestination complex:  refers to the belief that whatever happens to an 
individual is inevitable. 
Adler believed that the body has its own language known as the ‘organ dialect’. For instance, 
he considered enuresis as an expression of hostility to parents or a means of attracting their 
attention. Similarly insomnia was seen by him as an expression of ambition. Organic 
disorders usually express the craving for power. Along similar lines Adler believed that each 
emotion has its own somatic expression and the choice of the organ usually depends upon the 
individual’s childhood experiences subordinated to his/her craving for overcoming his/her 
feelings of inferiority. For example, a healthy individual may desire to become sick of the 
illness could bring him/her more superiority than health does. 
35 
 
Adler further believed that there is a constant reciprocal influence between the body and the 
mind. The style of life can exert a permanent influence on the somatic development of the 
individual. 
Adler concluded that the position within the family (such as eldest child, second child, or the 
youngest child) is extremely important in determining how an individual deals with reality, 
that is, what would be his/her style of life. The first -born or the eldest child is gets plenty of 
attention until the second child is born; then suddenly with arrival of the second child, the 
first one has to quit his/her favoured position and has to share his/her parent’s affections with 
the new baby. This experience may affect the eldest child in various ways, —he or she may 
start hating people thereby protecting him or herself against sudden reversal of fortune and 
feeling of insecurity. Eldest children are also likely to take an interest in the past, when they 
were the centre of attention. Neurotics, criminals, drunkards, and perverts, are often first born 
children, as per Adler’s observation. The eldest child is more likely to develop into a 
responsible, protective person if the parents handle the situation wisely by preparing him/her 
for the appearance of their sibling. 
The second or middle child is usually ambitious as he/she is constantly trying to surpass its 
elder siblings. He/she also tends to be more rebellious and envious but by and large is better 
adjusted than either the elder or younger sibling. 
The youngest child is usually the ‘spoiled’ child. Next to the eldest child he/she is more likely 
to become a ‘problem’ child and a neurotic maladjusted adult. 
Historically, research has not been able to demonstrate any consistent pattern correlated with 
birth order. Recent studies (Becker and Carrol, 1962; Stotland and Cottrell, 1961; Stotland 
and Dunn, 1962,1963; Stotland and Walsh, 1963) have reported personality constellations 
correlated with birth order, but these patterns are the result of empirical investigations, and 
there is to necessary relationship with Adler’s predictions. 
Some of the major criticisms of Adler’s psychology are as follows: 
· Adler’s approach has been criticized on the grounds that Adler believed that 
general sociability is innate. But research shows that this sociability can be 
taught, fostered and improved. 
· Adler believed that the goal of superiority was the governing principle. Many 
researchers question superiority as the governing principle of mental life 
· Adler’s work left several questions unanswered —What is the creative force? 
Why does it become goal directed? How does it involve heredity and 
environment? Why don’t people become reconciled to their feelings of 
inferiority? Are all people striving for superiority? Do experimental and 
clinical studies bring evidence in support of the generality of inferiority 
feelings and the drive towards superiority?    
Some of the major contributions of Adler are as follows: 
· Adler helped to shift the focus of psychodynamic theory from the id to the 
ego. 
36 
 
· Adler  helped patient’s change their illogical, mistaken ideas and expectations 
in the belief that to feel and behave better, one has first to think more rationally, an 
approach that anticipated contemporary developments in Cognitive Behaviour 
Therapy (CBT). Adler’s interest in growth, in the prevention of problems, and the 
betterment of society influenced the development of child guidance centres and parent 
education.   
· Adler’s most important contribution, however, was his concern with the social 
context of personality. Psychological disturbance, he claimed, has its roots not so 
much in early childhood experiences as in people’s present circumstances, particularly 
their relationships with others. 
1.6.2 Humanistic Counselling 
Existential and humanistic approaches have the following main characteristics: 
· They focus on the primacy of affect as a cause of and / or contributor to the 
development of certain human actions and reactions (Zojone, 1984). 
· They stress helping clients cope with or change their emotions as a premise for 
making other life alternations. 
· They emphasize human phenomenology and how a person’s views of the 
environment affect behaviour. 
· They emphasize the client -counsellor relationship more than the specific 
techniques in bringing about a change in the client (Baruth and Huber,1985). 
 · They focus on the unique growth and development within each individual. 
· The existential -humanistic approaches also emphasize the philosophical 
concerns of what it means to become fully human. 
Humanistic psychotherapy evolved, both in Europe and in US, as a reaction to the 
determinism of psychoanalysis and to the mechanism of the behavioural psychologies. 
Although several people like Soren A. Kierkegard (1813 -1855) (Father Figure of this 
movement), Paul Tillich (1886 -1965), Martin Heidegger (1889), Jean Paul Sartre (1905 -
1980), Eugene Minkowski (1855), Ludwig Binswanger (1981) etc. played an important role 
in the formulation of this school, but Rollo -May (1909) and Viktor E. Frankl (1906) have 
contributed the most to psychotherapy and counselling. 
The term ‘logotherapy’ was used by Frankl as early as 1938. ‘Logo’ is derived from Greek 
which denotes ‘finding meaning’. The impact of the concentration camps crystallized 
Frankl’s thoughts about the meaning of life and suffering, and it was partly his determination 
to shave his beliefs that kept him alive. 
According to Frankl (1962), the ‘meaning of life always changes’ but it never ceases to be. 
One can discover life’s meaning in three ways: 
 · By doing a deed, i.e. by achieving or accomplishing something 
 · By experiencing a value, such as a work of nature, culture, or love 
37 
 
 · By suffering, i.e. by finding a proper attitude towards unalterable fate 
Some of the key concepts of humanistic-existential approach are as follows: 
· Existence precedes essence: i.e. what a person does with his or her life 
determines what and who one is. 
· Human beings are capable of self awareness. An individual is free to choose 
and is responsible for the consequences of his or her choices— the existence. 
· One’s life is always lived with a view – toward death. One’s authenticity 
derives from one’s own ability to be aware of this and to courageously 
confront our existence. 
· An individual’s existence is never completely separate from the existence of 
others and the world and the existence of the world is never completely 
separate from his existence. 
· Perception is more valid when we try to understand another person from his or 
her subjective point of new (phenomenology).  
· Existential anxiety results from the awareness of being finite and from facing 
the inevitable prospects of eventual death (non-being).  
· Normal anxiety occurs when an individual feels helpless in a specific 
situation. It is natural for everyone to experience existential neurotic anxiety 
which stems from deeper maladjustments. May (1977) argues that normal 
anxiety may be healthy and motivational and can help people change.  
· Existential guilt  is the result of failing to fully become what one is able to 
become.  
· Human are relational beings who strive towards discovering a purpose in life 
and creating values that will give substance to living. Failure to create 
meaningful relationships results in conditions such as isolation, 
depersonalization, alleviation, estrangement and loneliness. The human being 
strives for self-actualization. 
· Pathology is viewed as a failure to use freedom to actualize one’s potential i.e., 
it is a failure to make meaningful choices and to maximize one’s potential.  
The therapeutic process involved in existential-humanistic approach is as follows: 
· Therapeutic goals: the existential counsellor’s primary goal is to understand 
the client or as a person, a being and as a being -in-the-world. Existential 
therapy aims at heaving clients experiences their existences as authentic by  
 (1) Becoming aware of their own existence and potentials 
 (2) By becoming aware of how they can open up  
 (3) Act on that potential 
38 
 
According to Bugental (1985), authenticity is defined as a central concern of psychotherapy 
and the primary existential value. Basically, the goal of existential therapy is to expand self -
awareness and thus increase choice potentials to live an authentic life i.e. to become free and 
to be responsible for the direction of one’s life. Existential therapy also aims at “helping 
clients face the anxiety of choosing for themselves and accepting the reality that they are 
more than mere victims of deterministic forces outside themselves.” 
 · Role of the counsellor:  Basically, counsellors concentrate on being authentic 
with their clients and entering into deep and personal relationship with them for which the 
counsellor sometimes shares personal experiences with the client, thereby helping a client 
realize a shaved humanness and struggle.  
  Buhler and Allen (1972), suggest that existential counsellors focus on person 
to person relationships that emphasize mutuality, wholeness, and growth. The counsellor 
serves as a model in the relationship for ways to achieve individual potential and make 
decisions. The counsellor concentrates on helping the client experience current subjective 
feelings, gain clearer self-understanding and move towards the establishment of a new way of 
being in the world. “The focus is on living productively in the present and not recovering a 
personal past.” (May and Yalom, 1989). 
 · The client’s experience in therapy In existential therapy clients are able to 
experience subjectively their perceptions of their worlds. They must be active in the 
therapeutic process, for they must decide what fears, guilt and anxieties they will explore.  
  In essence, clients in existential therapy are engaged in opening the doors to 
themselves and this experience is often frightening, or exciting, or joyful or depressing or a 
combination of all of these.  
  Gradually, they become aware of what they have been and who they now are, 
and they are better able to decide what kind of future they want, through the process of their 
therapy, they can explore alternatives for making their visions become real.  
 · Existential techniques: Kemp (1971) points out that in existential 
counselling, ‘technique follows understanding’. Thus, existential techniques are free to use 
techniques as widely diversified as desensitization and free association or to dissociate 
themselves from these practices entirely (Corey,1991). Confrontation is also used by the 
therapist. Clients areconfronted with the idea that each one of us is responsible for our own 
life. 
Frankl (1967) discusses an important technique which he calls ‘paradoxical intention’, i.e. the 
counsellor tells the client to wish for their worst fears, attempt to do them, or think intensely 
about them. For instance, a phobic patient who had a fear of sweating and was encouraged by 
the therapist to show people whom he met, how much he was able to sweat. As a result, 
whenever he met someone who aroused anxiety, he tended to tell himself that now he would 
sweat at least ten litres and by doing so, he was eventually relieved of his fear. 
Some techniques such as imagery exercises, awareness exercises, and goal setting activities 
are borrowed from other models. For example, by using Eugene Gendlin’s focusing approach 
(Gendlin 1981), individual are encouraged to discover and change the deepest feelings within 
themselves by discovering what he calls their felt sense. By focusing on a different aspect of 
39 
 
one’s experience, an individual can learn to interpret and react to inner feelings which were 
previously beyond the realm of awareness and change. 
In short, the existential point of view has rightly focused on the basic conflict of human 
nature—the kind of ambivalence that results in inaction or renunciation of freedom. In 
practice, existential counselling does not appear to apply to the common conflicting problems 
of human beings. 
Evaluation of existential-humanistic approach 
Some of the criticisms of the existential approach to therapeutic practices are as follows: 
• It revolutionized the profession of counselling by linking counselling with 
psychotherapy. 
• This approach has generated a great amount of research and initially set the standard for 
doing research on the counselling variables. In fact, Roger was the first practitioner to 
make audiotape recordings of the counselling sessions. 
• According to Grummon (1972), the person -centred approach is effective as it helps 
improve psychological adjustment, learning and frustration tolerance and helps 
decrease defensiveness. 
• The approach focuses on the importance of an open client -counsellor relationship and 
the short term nature of the process. 
• It emphasizes that the client and not the counsellor is the centre or the focus of the 
counselling process and therefore the initiative to overcome the problems should come 
from the client itself. 
• It emphasizes the importance of the counselling relationship in facilitating change. 
• It also stresses the counsellor’s attitude than the technique used by him/her in making 
the therapeutic relationship effective. 
• The approach takes a relatively short time to learn and lays emphasis on mastering the 
listening skills. In this regard, it forms the basis for training many paraprofessional 
helpers. 
• It also adopts a positive view of human nature and believes that people can change and 
it thus emphasises the change process. 
• Although these are some justifiable grounds on which to criticize, the Humanistic - 
Existential approach does emphasize unique aspects that the other therapeutic 
approaches ignore. For example, its emphasis the following aspects: 
• It focuses on human nature and emphasizes the uniqueness of each individual. 
• It emphasis the importance of the therapist-client relationship 
• It believes that the client has the freedom to decide his or her own destiny. 
• It provides a philosophy on which, the therapeutic practice is based. 
40 
 
• This approach places the person into central focus.  
• It focuses sharply on the central facts of human existence, self consciousness, and the 
consequent freedom.  
• It sees death in a positive light by postulating that death gives life its meaning.  
• This approach has contributed a new dimension to the understanding of anxiety, guilt, 
frustration, loneliness and alienation, as it states that anxiety is not necessarily a 
negative condition. It sees anxiety as a part of human life and can motivate some 
individuals to make healthy and productive decisions. 
• This approach lays stress “on continued human growth and development and offers 
hope to the clients through directed readings and therapeutic encounters with the 
counsellor.” 
• This approach is also effective in some cross -cultural counselling situations because its 
global view of human existence allows counsellors to focus on the person the client is 
irrespective of his social background (Jackson, 1987). 
• In addition, this approach provides a sound philosophical base on which to build a 
personal and unique style of the practice of therapy because of it addresses itself to the 
core struggles of the contemporary person. 
• In short, this approach has both its positive and negative components. On one hand it 
has certain lofty and abstract concepts in existentialism that are often difficult to grasp 
and then more difficult to apply in practice. On the other hand, it has much to offer 
clients who function psychologically and socially at a relatively high level. However, 
this approach is severely limited in its applicability to clients who function at low 
levels, to client in crisis states and to clients in poverty conditions as, clients who 
struggle to meet their basic survival needs are really not that interested in self -
actualization or in the existential meaning of life. 
1.6.3 Transactional Analysis 
Transactional analysis is an optimistic theory, which was formulated by Eric Berne in the 
early 1960s. Its basic assumption is that people can change despite any unfortunate events of 
the past. It believes that people have choices in their lives. In this sense, this approach is also 
anti-deterministic in nature. 
James and Jongeward (1971) emphasize that, ‘transactional analysis is a rational approach to 
understanding behavior and is based on the assumption that all individuals can learn to trust 
themselves, think for themselves, make their own decisions, and express their feelings”. 
It focuses on four major methods of understanding and predicting human behaviour as 
follows: 
• Structural analysis: It refers to understanding what is happening within the 
individual. 
• Transactional analysis: It refers to describing what happens between two or 
more people. 
41 
 
• Game analysis:  It refers to understanding transactions between individuals 
that led to bad feelings. 
• Script analysis:  It refers to understanding the life plan that an individual is 
following. 
Structural analysis 
There are  three functional ego states —child, parent and adult of each person. According to 
Berne (1964) “an ego state refers to a consistent pattern of feelings and experiences directly 
related to a corresponding consistent pattern of behaviour.” 
The first to develop is the child ego state. It is that part of the personality which is 
characterized by childlike behaviours and feelings such as inquisitiveness, affection, 
selfishness, meanness, playfulness and manipulation. The child ego state consists of two 
subdivisions the natural (free) child and the adaptive child. The natural child is the part of the 
person that is spontaneous, impulsive, feeling oriented, often self -centered and pleasure 
loving. The natural child is also intuitive, creative, and responsive to non -verbal messages. 
The adaptive child refers to the compliant part of the personality that conforms to the wishes 
and demands of parental figures. These adaptations of natural impulses occur in response to 
traumas, natural life experiences, and training. 
The parental ego state incorporates the attitudes and behaviours (the ‘do’s’, ‘should’s’, and 
‘ought’s’ ) of the parental figures. Outwardly, these messages are expressed through 
prejudice, criticism, and nurturing behavior. Parental messages are present throughout a 
person’s life. It is believed that a response to any thoughtful question that occurs within ten 
seconds  usually comes from the parent ego state. Like the child ego state, the parent ego 
state also consists of two subdivisions, namely: the nurturing parent and the critical parent. 
The nurturing parent is the part of the person that comforts, praises, and aids others, whereas 
the part of the person that finds fault, displays prejudices, disapproves, and prevents others 
from feeling good about themselves is the critical parent. These two parts of the parent are 
recognized through non -verbal behaviours such as pointing a finger at someone and verbal 
statements such as ‘that’s too bad, but don’t worry’ etc. 
The adult ego state is not subdivided according to a person’s age, nor does it have any 
relation to it. It is the objective, thinking, data -gathering part of the person. The adult ego 
state tests reality, is rational and organized. It expresses itself through phrases such as ‘I 
understand’ and ‘I am going to’. 
Sometimes the different ego states operate simultaneously. The transactional analysis aims to 
first identify or determine the ego state a person is frequently using in most of his or her 
dealings and situations. Thereafter it aims to help the client to be able to balance responses 
from different ego states when necessary and appropriate. 
Transactional analysis (TA) 
The second way of understanding and predicting human behaviour involves diagramming 
ego states transactions. Transactions may occur on one of three levels: complementary, 
crossed, or ulterior. In complementary transactions, the activated ego state produces an 
expected response. For example, a parent to parent transaction can be more nurturing or 
42 
 
critical. In a crossed transaction, an inappropriate ego state is activated, producing an 
unexpected response. Such transactions are often painful and people engaged in such 
transactions tend to withdraw from each other or switch topics. An ulterior transaction is one 
in which two ego states operate simultaneously and one message disguises the others. 
Ulterior transactions appear to be complementary and socially acceptable even though they 
are not.  
Game analysis 
Games are ulteriorly motivated transactions that appear complementary on the surface but 
end in bad feelings. People play games to structure time, achieve recognition, make others 
predictable, and prevent intimacy. Since intimacy involves risks, therefore games keep people 
safe from exposing their thoughts and feelings. There are first -degree, second -degree, and 
third-degree games and all have predictable ends. 
First-degree games are played in social circles with anyone who is willing to participate. 
They generally lead to mild upsets. The second -degree games usually occur in more intimate 
circles and involve bigger risks. People involved in these games often end up with bad 
feelings. A third -degree game usually involves tissue or physical damage (for example 
fighting etc.); and the players end up in jail, the hospital, or the morgue. According to Berne 
(1964), most games carry the danger of causing permanent damage but only few games have 
a positive or neutral outcome; as during games people avoid meaningful and healthy human 
interactions. 
Individuals who play games operate from one of three positions: victim, persecutor, or 
rescuer (Karpman, 1968). To keep games going there is often a switch -off where people 
assume new roles.  
Script analysis 
Berne believed that everyone makes a life script, or life plan, early in childhood —by the age 
of five. These scripts are often based on one’s interpretations of external events and they 
determine how one interacts with others. Positive messages received as a child form the basis 
for constructive scripts, whereas negative messages are more powerful and may become the 
basis for destructive scripts.  
A life script involves the ability to get strokes (verbal or physical recognition) for certain 
behaviour. Most life scripts revolve around giving and receiving ‘strokes’. Berne points out 
that negative strokes (punishment) are better than no strokes at all (being ignored). Strokes 
result in the collection of either good or bad feelings, known as ‘stamps’. When individuals 
collect enough stamps, these feelings are then frequently reflected in terms of their 
behaviours. Healthy people give and receive positive strokes most often. 
Below are given some common negative script patterns: 
• Never scripts: a person never gets to do what he or she wants because the 
parent forbids it. 
• Until scripts: a person must wait until a certain time to do something before he 
or she can have a reward. 
43 
 
• Always scripts: a person tells himself or herself that it is necessary to continue 
doing the same thing. 
• After scripts: difficulty is expected after a certain event. 
• Open-ended scripts: a person has no idea what he/she is supposed to do after a 
given time. 
There are also mini-scripts within people’s lives that focus on minute-by-minute occurrences. 
Some of the most common mini -scripts are ‘be perfect,’ ‘be strong’, ‘hurry up’, ‘try hard’, 
‘please someone’. These five messages, called drivers, allow people to temporarily escape 
their life scripts. 
The ideal life script in TA is expressed as the position that I’m ok: you’re ok (a ‘get -on-with’ 
position) (Harris, 1967). But people may operate from three other positions too, that is; I’m 
ok; you’re not ok (a ‘get -away-from’ position); I’m not ok: you’re ok (a ‘get -nowhere-with’ 
position): and I’m not ok (a ‘get-rid-of’ position).”  
Everyone operates from each of these four positions at various times in their lives. But well -
functioning individuals learn to recognize unhealthy positions and modify thoughts and 
behaviours accordingly. According to Berne, life scripts can be rewritten if a person becomes 
more conscious of what he or she is thinking and makes sincere efforts to change. 
Role of the counsellor 
In TA the initial role of a counsellor is of being a teacher, where the counsellor begins by first 
explaining to the client the language and concepts of TA, as a new way of thinking about self. 
Then the counsellor contracts with the client for specific changes and helps the person 
achieve them by developing the necessary skills needed to bring about the constructive 
changes (Corey, 1991). 
TA counsellors do not rely heavily on formal psychological tests but instead assess the client 
functioning through an egogram or another less formal method. The purpose is to determine 
how a client is spending time and from which ego states he or she is operating.  
Goals 
The primary goal of TA is to help people transform their life in order to attain health and 
autonomy. Counsellors generally help their clients to identify and restore distorted or 
damaged ego-states, to develop the capacity to use all ego states,  to use the adult ego state 
with its reasoning powers, to alter inappropriate life scripts, and to adopt a position of ‘I’m 
ok; you’re ok’ (Berne, 1966). 
In the process of becoming autonomous, clients exhibit more awareness, intimacy, and 
spontaneity; become free of games; and eliminate self -defeating scripts. They become more 
in touch with their past but, at the same time, are freed from previous negative influences. A 
major emphasis of TA is on learning about the self in order to decide what one wishes to 
become (Goulding & Goulding, 1979). 
 
 
44 
 
Techniques 
There are several techniques used in TA to achieve the above-mentioned goals such as 
‘structural analysis, transactional analysis, game analysis, and script analysis’. Some other 
techniques that are also used in this regard are as follows: 
Treatment contract: refers to a specific, concrete contract that emphasizes agreed upon 
responsibilities for both counsellors and clients (Dusay and Dusay, 1989). The treatment 
contract tells both the client and counsellor when counselling goals have been achieved. 
Interrogation: involves speaking to a client’s ego state until the counsellors receives an adult 
response. This technique should be used carefully as when not used properly, it can be 
ineffective. 
Specification: refers to identification of the ego state that initiated a transaction. 
Specification takes place from the adult ego state of both client and counsellor. 
Confrontation: involves the counsellor’s pointing out inconsistencies in the client’s 
behaviour and speech.  
Explanation: occurs on an adult -to-adult ego state level. The counsellor teaches the client 
about some aspects of TA. 
Illustration: enlightens the client or elaborates a point through the use of story. 
Confirmation: is used when previously modified behaviour occurs again and the counsellor 
points this out to the client. Only when the client has a firmly established adult ego state can 
this technique be effective. 
Interpretation: involves the counsellor’s explaining to the child ego state of the client the 
reasons for the client’s behaviour. This technique is mainly a psychodynamic procedure and 
is used only when the client has an effectively functioning adult ego state. 
Crystallization: consists of an adult -to-adult transaction in which the client comes to an 
awareness that individual game playing may be given up if so desired. 
Almost all the techniques in TA involve some combination of questioning, confrontation and 
dialogue. The following are among the questions most frequently asked by TA counsellors: 
 · What are the nicest and worst things your parents ever said to you? 
 · What is your earliest memory? 
· What is the family story about your birth? What is your favourite fairy tale, 
story, or song? 
 · How would you describe your mother and father?  
 · How long do you expect to live? 
To be most effective, a TA counselor must always be careful to assess the ego strengths of 
individual clients. He/she should always keep in his/her mind that a client is usually capable 
of making new decisions about life once he or she discovers different aspects of the self. 
 
45 
 
Evaluation 
The major strengths of TA are as follows: 
· The approach uses terms that are easily understood and are clearly defined 
hence it can be used in a number of settings and with varied population. 
· This approach can be easily and effectively combined with other action-
oriented counselling theories. 
 · This approach puts the responsibility for change on the client. 
 · The approach is goal-directed. 
It has certain limitations too. Some being 
 · It has been criticized for its primary cognitive orientation. 
 · It has been criticized for its simplicity, structure and popularity. 
 · This approach lacks adequate research 
Unfortunately, this approach has not developed much since Berne’s death in 1970. 
1.6.4 Behavioural Counselling 
The behavioural counselling approach believes that emotional, learning and adjustment 
difficulties can be treated through a variety of prescriptive, mechanical, usually non-dynamic, 
techniques and procedures. It involves the “systematic application of principle of learning to 
change maladaptive behaviour toward more adaptive ways (Krumboltz,1966).” 
The behavioural approaches encompass within them a wide variety of ideas, practices and 
theories. At one end of this continuum are behaviourists like Skinner who largely employ 
learning principals to modify behaviour without taking into account the underlying thoughts. 
On the other end of the continuum are researchers like Donald Meichenbaum (1977) and 
Aaron Beck (1976) who emphasize the importance of mental processes in human behavior 
and view thoughts as a type of behaviour. However, it is John Krumboltz (1966) who is 
known to popularize behaviourism in counselling. 
According to Cormier and Hackney (1987), clients who appear to improve successfully using 
behavioural counseling are: 
 · Predominantly goal-oriented and have a need for achievement and results. 
 · Action-oriented with a need to do something 
· Interested in changing either a discrete response or a limited number of 
behaviours. 
In general, behaviour counsellors see human beings as essentially shaped and determined by 
their socio-cultural environment. They concentrate on processes closely associated with overt 
behaviour and focus on ‘here and now’ as opposed to the ‘then and there’ of behaviour. They 
believe that all behaviour -—adaptive and maladaptive is learnt, hence unhealthy behaviours 
can also be modified using learning principles. They focus on setting well -defined therapy 
goals for clients and reject the idea that the human personality is composed of traits. They 
46 
 
further stress the importance of obtaining empirical evidence and scientific support for any 
technique used. 
In general, behaviourists believe learning and thus development occurs in one of the three 
following ways: 
1. Respondent learning: Formulated by Ivan Pavlov. According to this learning occurs 
through the association of two stimuli. Many human emotions, such as phobias, arise because 
of paired associations. For example, a person may have an accident after eating a certain 
food. The association of the food with the accident, even through the two are unrelated, may 
result in that person’s eventual fear or avoidance of the food. Thus, people often associate 
feelings with certain events and vice versa. The sound of music, the smell of certain odors, 
the sight of certain colors, and the touch of a stranger are experiences that individual may 
react to emotionally because of respondent learning. People need not be actively involved 
with environment forces for learning to occur. Once learned, these associations can be 
unlearned, with new ones taking their place, a process known as counter-conditioning. 
2. Operant conditioning: Formulated by B.F. Skinner, who believed that when a certain 
behaviour is followed closely by a reinforcer (a reward), chances that the behaviour will 
occur in similar and identical circumstances increase. Thus, individual’s behaviour can be 
shaped through rewards and punishments. 
3. Social modeling:  Formulated by Albert Bandura (1977), who believed that most 
learning is a result of copying or imitating. Thus, according to social modelling, people 
acquire new knowledge and behaviour by observing other people and events without 
engaging in the behavior themselves and without any direct consequences to themselves 
(Bandura, 1977). Learning through social modelling also emphasize the self -regulation of 
behaviour and thus states that learning may occur independently of reinforcement 
(Hergeuhahn, 1984). 
Characteristics of behaviour therapy 
The key characteristics of behaviour therapy are that it focuses on overt and specific 
behaviour or behaviours; has well defined specific treatment goals; formulates a specific 
treatment procedure appropriate to a particular problem and does an objective assessment of 
the outcomes of therapy. 
According to Eysenck and Rachman (1985) an abnormal behaviour develops along the 
following three stages: 
· In the first stage a series of traumatic events produce autonomic reactions in an 
individual (considered as unconditional responses) which in turn may result in neurotic 
behaviour patterns. 
· In the second stage, based on the classical conditioning principles, anxiety seems to 
get generalized to unhealthy proportions. 
· In the final stage, instrumental avoidance of painful or anxiety -provoking situations 
takes place by the individual. 
 
47 
 
The therapeutic process 
the primary goal of behaviour therapy is to acquire new behaviours, eliminate maladaptive 
behaviours, and strengthen and maintain desirable behaviours. The specific and observable 
goals for the client are decided during the history taking. Blackhain and Siberman (1971) 
suggest the following four specific steps that counselor and client should take in the process 
of defining goals: 
· Define the problem:  It involves defining a problem in specific, concrete and 
observable terms. Thus, a client is asked to specify when, where, how, and with whom the 
problem arises.  
· Take the developmental history:  It in the counsellor tries to find out how the 
problem had originated and how the client has handled past circumstances. 
· Establish specific goals:  Here behavioural counsellors help clients to break down 
goals into small, achievable units and also help them to develop needed skills by setting up 
learning experiences for clients (Krumboltz & Thoresen, 1976).  
· Determine the best methods for change:  Of the various behavioural methods 
available, the counsellor picks up the best possible method to achieve desired goals. If one 
method does not work, then it may be modified or a new one may be tried. Continuous 
assessment of the effectiveness of methods is also critical. 
According to Krumboltz and Thoresen, while formulating an acceptable goal in behavioral 
counselling the counsellor should keep the following things in mind: 
 · Goals are chosen are as desired by the client. 
 · Willingness of counsellor to help the client achieve these goals. 
 · Assessment of counsellor of  the extent to which the client achieves the goals. 
Role of the counsellor 
In behaviour therapy the counselor adopts an active directive role, where the client learns, 
unlearns, or relearns specific ways of behaving. In the process, the counsellor functions as a 
‘consultant, teacher, adviser, reinforcer, and facilitator’ (Gilliland, James & Bowman, 1989). 
He or she may  instruct, supervise or may support people in the client’s environment who are 
assisting in the change process. An effective behavioural counsellor operates from a broad 
perspective and involves the client in every phase of the counselling. Some social learning -
oriented counsellors serve as models for emulation, whereas others who are more respondent 
or operant focused are more directive end prescriptive in offering assistance. 
Counsellors using a behavioural approach differ widely regarding the use of psychological 
tests and diagnoses. Most employ some form of assessment device with their clients, to 
measures behaviour and actions. 
According to Goodstein (1972) the role of the counsellor is to facilitate the development of 
socially appropriate behaviour by systematically reinforcing the client’s behaviour. The 
attention and approval (or disinterest or disapproval) of the therapist serves as powerful 
reinforcers of client behaviour. 
48 
 
The client’s experience in therapy 
Behaviour therapy provides the therapist with a system of well-defined procedures to employ. 
It emphasizes the importance of client awareness and participation in the therapeutic process. 
The clients are actively involved in the selection and determination of goals. They must 
possess the motivation to change, and must be willing to cooperate in carrying out therapeutic 
activities, both during the therapeutic sessions and outside therapy in real -life situations. The 
success of therapy depends on how actively  the client participates in the therapeutic process.  
In behaviour therapy the client is encouraged to experiment with new behaviours for the 
purpose of enlarging their repertoire of adaptive behaviour. They are also helped to generalize 
and transfer the learning acquired within the therapeutic situation to situations outside 
therapy. The therapeutic relationship between the client and the therapist must be warm, 
empathetic, authentic, permissive, and acceptable. Behaviour therapists regard these 
conditions as necessary, but not sufficient, for behaviour change to occur within the 
therapeutic process.  
Techniques 
Some of the best researched and most effective behavioural counselling techniques available 
are—token economy, behavioural contract, extinction, reinforcement, punishment, aversive 
therapy, systematic desensitization, modelling etc.Aanother technique that is used frequently 
in behavioural counselling is self-regulation behaviour programme. Neilans and Israel (1981) 
suggested a four component model for a self -regulation behavioural change program as 
follows: 
1. Self-monitoring: involves observing one’s own behaviour and charting or recording in 
an organized way relevant behaviour. 
2. Self-goal setting: involves setting of a specific goal or goals, thereby establishing 
criteria for subsequent performance evaluation. 
3. Self-evaluation: involves making a comparison between the individual’s performance 
(as monitored and recorded) and the performance criterion (the goal).  
4. Self-reinforcement: refers to withholding of necessary, appropriate rewards after 
evaluating one’s performance with respect to the goal. 
Evaluation of behavioural counselling 
Ironically, what has often been cited as the greatest fault of behavioural therapy proves to be 
its greatest advantage in the counselling setting; namely that, it deals directly with the 
symptoms. Some of its major contributions are given below: 
· An important contribution of the behaviour therapy approach is the systematic way 
that its therapeutic methods and techniques have been subjected to experimental 
verification. Thus the procedures are in a continual process of refinement and 
development. 
· The approach is quite specific in nature and is helps to keep clearly in focus the 
client’s goals. 
49 
 
· It stresses the therapist’s and the client’s mutual progress in reaching those goals. 
Some of the major criticisms of behavioural counselling are as follows: 
· Behavioural counselling is criticized for not treating causes but superficially dealing 
with symptoms. As when the underlying causes are not dealt with and only superficial 
symptoms are treated then there are high changes that if one group of symptoms is 
removed, another group of symptoms will take its place. 
· Another major criticism of behavioural counselling is that it focuses almost 
exclusively on treating well -defined, overt symptoms such as phobias, tension states, 
speech disorders in addition to drugs or alcohol abuse, and sexual problems etc. 
· Broader problems of personal and social adjustment, which practitioners most often 
encounter in psychotherapy are not dealt with in this modality. 
· Behavioural counselling may change behaviour, but it does not change feelings. Some 
critics agree that feelings must change before behaviour can change. 
However, this approach has made significant contributions to both clinical and educational 
settings too. Behavioural counselling approaches are especially popular in institutional 
settings, such as mental hospital or sheltered workshops. They are the approaches of choice in 
working with clients who have such specific problems as eating disorders, substance abuse 
and sexual dysfunction. Behavioural approaches are also useful in addressing anxiety, stress, 
assertiveness, parenting and social interaction (Seligman, 1986; Thoresen and Coates, 1980). 
Ranchman (1967) described studies that demonstrate that behaviour therapy does work in 
successfully treating and wide range of neurotic conditions, including phobias, hysteria, 
sexual disorders, tics, tension states, and children’s disorders. 
Carkhuff and Berenson (1967) assert that the clinical and research evidence suggest that 
behaviour modification techniques are well suited for use in experimental setting; efforts in 
human engineering and situations where there is an acute shortage of time. 
1.6.5 Cognitive Theories 
Cognitive theories of counselling focus on thinking. They believe that thoughts influence 
feelings and behaviours. If a person changes a way of thinking, feelings and behaviours will 
get modified automatically. These theories are successful in working with clients who: 
 · Are average to above-average in intelligence 
 · Have moderate to high levels of functional distress 
 · Are able to identify thoughts and feelings 
 · Are not psychotic or disabled by present problems 
 · Are willing and able to complete systematic homework assignments 
 · Possess a repertoire of behaviour skills and responses 
· Who process information on a visual and auditory level (Cormier & Hackney, 
1987). 
50 
 
Cognitive approaches are also frequently used in working with clients who have inhibited 
functioning, such as depression. 
Aaron Beck's Cognitive Theory 
Beck’s cognitive theory considers the subjective symptoms such as a negative view of self, 
world, and future defining features of depression. The model assumes that 
psychopathological states represent extreme or excessive forms of normal cognitive, 
emotional, and behavioral functioning. Additionally, the cognitive theory posits that anxiety 
and depression can be distinguished by their cognitive content, with thoughts of personal loss 
and failure specific to depression and cognitive content involving physical or psychological 
threat and danger specific to anxiety. Although initially conceptualized as a model relevant to 
anxiety and depression, the cognitive model is now applied to a wide range of disorders such 
as eating disorders. 
A central tenet of the cognitive theory is that our thinking influences our emotional and 
behavioral experiences and vice versa. When applied in therapy, there is an emphasis on 
working toward changing one’s thoughts as a means to altering feelings and behaviors.  
Cognitive theory has focused on thought processes that become habitual and automatic. 
These automatic thoughts are theorized to differ depending on the specific psychological 
problems a person is experiencing. How these thoughts become automatic is a matter of 
scientific study. Beck originally posited that individuals derive meaning from their 
experiences and, with time, rely on what he called cognitive schemas (i.e., hypothetical 
organizing structures of experience represented in thoughts) to provide meaning to 
experience. For example, someone with a history of limited close relationships may, over 
time, develop a schema like ‘I am unlovable.’ Schemas can constitute particular 
vulnerabilities to interpret certain types of experiences inaccurately. For instance, if a person 
believes they are unlovable, then they may interpret a colleague not inviting them to a party 
as further evidence of their unlovable personality rather than considering that the party was 
only for that person’s family members or that they were actually invited but did not see the 
invitation. 
Beck also developed the notion of the cognitive triad to describe how depressed adults tend to 
think about the world. The triad refers to thoughts about self, world, and future. In all the 
three instances, depressed individuals tend to have negative views. Thus, a depressed 
individual would tend to think they are a worthless person living in a futile and unforgiving 
world with a hopeless future. Beck’s work also led to the identification of particular patterns 
of habitual and maladaptive thinking that he called errors of thought. These included the 
following: 
· Arbitrary Inference:  It is referred to an individual quickly and on the basis of 
limited evidence, draws a conclusion. Example: That person did not smile at me. He hates 
me. 
· Selective Abstraction: It occurred when an individual focuses only on some of the 
available evidence to draw a conclusion. Example: My boss mentioned that my report needed 
some edits. He hated it. 
51 
 
· Overgeneralization: It is referred to an individual takes one negative event and 
assumes that it has meaning that pervades his/her whole like. Example: I missed that shot. I 
am never going to succeed in anything. 
Rational Emotive Behavioural Counselling  
Among the best-known cognitive theories are Aaron Beck’s Cognitive Therapy, Albert Ellis’s 
Rational Emotive Therapy, and Eric Berne’s Transactional Analysis. RET assumes that 
people are both inherently rational and irrational, sensible and crazy (Weinrach,1980). 
According to Ellis (1979), people are active thinking beings and are capable of engaging in 
both rational and irrational thinking 
Thus RET is based on the assumption that human beings are born with a potential for both 
rational thinking and irrational thinking and verbalizing, loving, communication with others, 
and growth and self-actualization. They also have propensities for self-destruction, avoidance 
of thought, procrastination, endless repetition of mistakes, superstition, intolerance, 
perfectionism and self-blame, and avoidance of actualization of growth potentials. They have 
the tendencies within —both to stick with old, dysfunctional behaviour patterns and to 
discover a variety of ways to engage in self-sabotage. 
It further asserts that people have vast untapped resources for actualizing their potentials and 
that they can change their personal and social destinies by altering their thought patterns. 
According to it, people are born with the tendency to insist that all wants, demands, wishes, 
and needs be met in life; if they do not immediately get what they want, they condemn both 
themselves and others (Ellis, 1973). 
RET stresses that humans think, emote, and behave simultaneously. Rarely do they emote 
without thinking and feelings are usually excited by the perception of a specific situation. It is 
imperative to understand how a person emotes, thinks, perceives, and acts, in order to 
understand self-defeating behaviour. 
According to Ellis, people have within themselves the means to control their thoughts, 
feelings, and actions, but they must first realize what they are telling themselves (i.e., their 
‘self-talk’) in order to gain command of their lives (Ellis, 1962). This is a matter of personal, 
conscious awareness. 
RET is based on the assumption that psychopathology is originally learned and aggravated by 
the inculcation of irrational beliefs from significant others during childhood. The negative 
and irrational believes get further re -instilled by the processes of autosuggestion and self -
repetition. In this manner the dysfunctional attitudes remain alive and operative within us. 
RET sees emotions as the products of human thinking. For instance, when we think 
something is bad, then we feel bad about that thing.  Thus, Ellis (1967) maintained that 
emotional disturbance therefore, essentially consists of mistaken, illogical, invalid table 
sentences or meanings which the disturbed individual dogmatically and un -challengingly 
believes, and upon which he therefore emotes or acts to his own defeat. 
RET insists that at the core of most emotional disturbances lies blame. Hence in order to 
overcome an illness, one first needs to stop blaming ourselves and others and instead should 
learn to accept ourselves in spite of our imperfections. Anxiety stems from internal repetition 
of the sentence “I don’t like my behaviour and would like to change” and the self -blaming 
52 
 
sentence “because of my wrong behaviour and my mistakes, I am a rotten person and I am to 
blame, and I deserve to suffer”.  According to RET, this anxiety is unnecessary. A person can 
be helped to see that these precise, irrational sentences are false and does not help in anyway. 
In RET the therapists teaches clients how to feel unhurt even when they are unaccepted and 
unloved by significant others. Ellis (1962) gave the following eleven common irrational 
beliefs that people often carry: 
1. It is absolutely essential to be loved or approved of by every significant person 
in one’s life. 
2. To be worthwhile, a person must be competent, adequate, and achieving in 
everything attempted. 
3. Some people are wicked, bad and villainous and therefore should be blamed or 
punished. 
 4. It is terrible and catastrophic whenever events do not occur as one hopes. 
5. Unhappiness is the result of outside events, and therefore a person has no 
control over such despair. 
6. Something potentially dangerous or harmful should be cause for great concern 
and should always be kept in mind. 
 7. Running away from difficulties and responsibilities is easier than facing them. 
8. A person must depend on others and must have someone stronger on whom to 
rely. 
 9. The past determines one’s present behavior and thus it cannot change. 
 10. A person should get upset over the problems and difficulties of others. 
11. There is always a right answer to every problem, and a failure to find this 
answer is catastrophic. 
The A-B-C theory of personality is central to RET theory and practice. A is the existence of a 
fact, an event, or the behaviour or attitude of an individual. C is the emotional consequences 
or reaction of the individual; the reaction can be either appropriate or inappropriate. A (the 
activating events) does not cause C (the emotional consequence). Instead, B, which is the 
person’s belief about A, causes C, i.e., the emotional reactions. For example, if a person 
experiences depression after a divorce, it may not be the divorce itself that causes the 
depressive reaction but the person’s beliefs about being a failure, being rejected, or losing a 
mate which cause depression. Thus human beings are largely responsible for creating their 
own emotional reactions and disturbances. 
Disturbed emotional reactions such as depression and anxiety are initiated and perpetuated by 
the self-defeating belief system, which is based on irrational ideas that one has incorporated. 
These emotional disturbances can be eliminated or modified by directly working with the 
individual’s belief systems and teaching them how to actively and vigorously dispute (D) 
their irrational beliefs.” This disputation can takes place by the use of a highly active -
directive, didactic, philosophic, homework-assigning therapist. 
53 
 
RET assumes that, since people’s irrational beliefs and values are causally linked to their 
emotional and behavioural disturbances, the most efficient way of helping them to make 
basic personality changes is to confront them directly with their philosophies of life, to 
explain to them how their ideas make them disturbed, to attack their irrational ideas on 
logical grounds, and to teach them how to think logically, thereby enabling them to change or 
eliminate their irrational ideas. RET thus confronts clients with their irrational beliefs and 
attacks, challenges, questions, and disputes those beliefs. 
After the A-B-C comes D, disputing. Essentially, D is the application of the scientific method 
to help clients challenge their irrational beliefs that lead to disturbances in their emotions and 
behaviour.    
The goals of RET focus on helping people realize that they can live more rational and 
productive lives by critically examining their most basic values. RET asks clients to avoid 
making ‘should’, ‘must’ and ‘ought’ statements. It further helps client to see irrational 
thinking develops and how silly the consequences of such thinking are. 
Another goal of RET is to assist people in changing their self -defeating habits of thought or 
behaviour. One way this is accomplished is through teaching clients the above mentioned A -
B-C theory of personality. It also makes use of a number of homework assignments, such as 
‘shame attack exercises’ which enables the clients come to a realization that the world does 
not stop if a mistake is made or if a want remains unfulfilled. A client also learns that others 
are ‘fallible human beings’ and need not be perfect. Finally a client learns that goals may be 
achieved without catastrophizing personal situations.
