In the RET approach counsellors play an active and direct role. They are instructors, teaching, 
and correcting the client’s cognitions. They encourage clients to engage in activities that 
bring forward their irrational beliefs and help them to eventually dispute them and replace 
them with more rational beliefs. According to Ellis (1980), an RET counsellor needs to be 
bright, knowledgeable, empathetic, persistent, scientific, interested in helping others and 
themselves users of RET. 
The main assessment conducted by RET counsellors is the evaluation of a client’s thinking. 
Some formal tests are used to measure rational/irrational thinking, but the evaluations process 
is primarily accomplished in counselor/client sessions. As a rule, RET practitioners do not 
rely heavily on the diagnostic categories. 
To quite a large extent, the client’s role in RET is like that of a student, learner where the 
client learns how to apply logical thoughts to problem solving. The client’s acquisition of 
emotional insight into the sources of their disturbances is a crucial part of the therapeutic 
process. 
According to Ellis (1973), a good rapport between the client and the therapist is most 
important followed by personal warmth and affection and a personal relationship between 
therapist and client. 
RET contends that people can experience personality change through many diverse avenues 
such as having significant life experiences; learning vicariously by reading about other’s 
experiences; entering into a relationship with a therapist; viewing movies; listening to tape 
54 
 
recordings; practicing specific homework assignments; spending time alone for thinking and 
meditating; and in many other ways deciding to make lasting personality change. 
RET makes use of a number of diverse techniques, two primary ones being teaching and 
disputing. Disputing thoughts and beliefs takes one of three forms: cognitive, imaginal, and 
behavioural. The process is more effective when all three forms are used (Walen, DiGiuseppe 
and Wessler, 1980). Cognitive disputation involves the use of direct questions, logical 
reasoning, and persuasion. Direct questions may challenge the client to prove that his or her 
response is logical. Sometimes these inquiries involve the use of the word why, which is 
seldom used in counseling because it makes most people defensive and closes the doors for 
exploration. Examples of questions in cognitive disputation include ‘why must you?, and  
‘why must that be?’ During these inquiries, clients learn to distinguish between rational and 
irrational thoughts. They also learn the superiority of rational thoughts. 
Another form of cognitive disputation involves the use of syllogisms, which help clients and 
counsellors to understand more thoroughly inductive as well as deductive fallacies that 
underlie emotions.  
Imaginal disputation depends on the client’s ability to imagine and employs a technique 
known as Rational Emotive Imagery (REI) (Maultsby, 1984). REI may be used in one of two 
ways. First, the client may be asked to imagine a situation where he or she is likely to become 
upset. The client examines his or her self -talk during that imagined situation. Then the 
counsellor asks the client to imagine the same situation again but this time with a more 
moderate and rational self -talk. Second, the counsellor asks a client to imagine a situation in 
which the client feels or behaves differently from some real instance. The client is then 
instructed to examine the self-talk used in this imagined situation. 
The Emotional Control Card (ECC) is a device that helps clients reinforce and expand the 
practice of REI (Sklare, Taylor & Hyland, 1985). Four emotionally debilitating categories 
(anger, self-criticism, anxiety, and depression) are listed on wallet -sized ECCs (Ellis, 1986). 
Under each category, a list of inappropriate or self -destructive feelings and a parallel list of 
appropriate or non -defeating feelings are written. The client is advised to make use of these 
cards and rectify their quality of feelings, when faced with troublesome situations. 
Behavioural disputation involves behaving in ways that are opposite of the client’s usual 
ways. This is achieved by the use of role plays and completion of homework assignments 
given between sessions. 
Two other powerful techniques are confrontation and encouragement. In it, sometimes, the 
RET counsellor empathetically challenges the client who claims to be thinking rationally but 
in reality is not doing so. 
One interesting variation on RET is Rational Behaviour Therapy (RBT), which was 
formulated by Maxie Maultsby (1971). RBT emphasizes cognitive change in a way more 
behavioural than originally conceptualized by Ellis. It involves checking the activating events 
and disputing one’s self talk by using questions, such as “is my thinking here based on 
obvious fact?” 
In RBT, clients regularly complete homework assignments in rational self -analysis (RSA), in 
which they write down significant events in their lives and their thoughts and feelings 
55 
 
associated with those events. The beliefs of the persons are then evaluated for their degree of 
rationality and changed in accordance with the rules of rational behaviour. 
According to Ellis (1973), RET is likely to be more effective with clients who are willing to 
engage in hard work; in practicing the homework assignments and are not seriously disturbed 
or have a single major symptom. Hence, it is a choice of treatment for moderate anxiety; 
marital problems; sexual difficulties; neurotic personality disorder; juvenile delinquents and 
adult criminals; borderline psychotics, psychotics who have some contact with reality, and 
clients with psychosomatic problems. 
RET has a number of strengths, as mentioned below: 
 · The approach is clear, easily learned, and effective.  
 · It is appropriate for the treatment of affective disorders, anxiety disorders, and 
adjustment disorders. Ellis (1977) provides research data on the effectiveness of RET with 
different types of clients in specific settings. 
· The approach can easily be combined with behavioural techniques to help 
clients experience more fully what they are learning.  
 · The approach is relatively short-term, usually lasting from ten to fifty sessions.  
 · Clients may continue to use the approach in their lives on a self-help basis.  
 · The economical and efficiency aspects of RET are impressive. 
· The approach has generated an impressive amount of literature and research 
for clients and counselors. 
· The approach has continued to evolve over the years as techniques have been 
refined. 
The approach also has certain limitations as follows: 
· The approach cannot be used effectively with individuals such as 
schizophrenics, who have severe thought disorders, and it is not productive 
with people who are severely mentally impaired.  
· The approach is limited if its practitioners do not combine its early cognitive 
base with more behavioural and emotive techniques.  
· According to Bernard (1981), RET is too dogmatic, especially in its 
assessment of a client’s problem and is the interpretation of the client’s private 
thoughts. 
· Since, the therapist has a large degree of power by virtue of persuasion and 
directiveness, psychological harm is more possible in RET than in the less 
directive client -centered approaches. Therefore, the therapist’s level of 
training, knowledge and skill, perceptiveness and accuracy of judgment is 
particularly important in RET.    
 
56 
 
Applications 
RET, as applied to one -to-one work, is generally designed for relatively brief therapy and 
teaches clients many of the basics of how to attack the underlying sources of their problems. 
RET is also suitable for group therapy. In the group setting members also have an opportunity 
to experience assertive training, role -playing, and a variety of risk -taking activities. They can 
learn social skills and practice interacting with others in after -group sessions. RET has also 
been intensively adopted in the clinical, school, social work, industrial, family and health 
settings.

1.7 Helping Relationship and Helping Process 
Helping is a bond created between two or more individuals with the intention of providing 
and receiving assistance. Helpers must be able to comprehend emotions and experiences by 
putting themselves in the client’s shoes. It is a partnership in which at least one party seeks to 
further the other’s development, maturation, or enhanced functioning. The therapy 
relationship should ideally have a distinct beginning and end. It moves through the 
commitment, process, change, and termination phases. However, we take ‘helping’ for 
granted. It is a well-known word in our language. Social workers, youth workers, and support 
personnel are a few examples of professions that have historically been referred to as ‘helping 
professions’. ‘Do you need some help?’ is a common question we ask as a society as whole. 
The definition of helping isn’t always clear. There are some features we should consider 
when defining ‘helping partnerships’.  
1.7.1 Definition of Helping 
The idea of assisting is closely associated with the social professions, such as social work, 
youth work, and community work (Banks, 2004). People involved in informal education and 
social pedagogy in general can certainly say the same. People who are dealing with 
challenging decisions and circumstances, anxious thoughts, and/or a sense of missed 
opportunities may likely feel they need someone to listen to them, give them support, and 
help them understand what is happening so they can go on.  
Other individuals may occasionally decide that giving people this ‘assistance’ is in their best 
interests. In his 1975 book, The Skilled Helper , Gerard Egan made a strong case for the 
importance of helping in the counselling field and claimed that it involves two fundamental 
objectives. Each of these options is based on what the person who needs aid needs. The 
primary concern are the ones who need assistance in problem -solving. The goal is to ‘help 
57 
 
clients manage their problems in living more effectively and develop unused or underused 
opportunities more fully’. The second objective focuses on their general capacity for 
problem-solving and creating opportunities for them. This goal is to make clients ‘become 
better at helping themselves in their everyday lives’ (Egan, 1998). 
In addition to being part of counselling and advice, helping is frequently used to refer to 
specific teaching moments, such as ‘helping’ someone with their schoolwork or filling out an 
income tax form. It also refers to providing practical aid, such as clothing or money, or direct 
physical support, such as assisting someone in bathing or using the restroom. The helping is 
characterised and driven by discourse, which occurs in a wide range of contexts (many not 
created by the helper), and occurs in many different places. However, we feel that 
characterising the job solely in terms of counselling or teaching oversimplifies it. It requires 
referencing to a variety of ways of thinking and behaving to make sense of what these people 
are actually doing and expressing. To provide direct aid and be perceived as people of 
integrity, it is necessary to listen and discuss issues and problems with others. It is also 
necessary to teach and offer counsel.  
Helpers focus on education, relationships, and working with others to put their knowledge 
into practise. But it also overlaps with the counselling field. They achieve this not by 
adopting the role of counsellor but rather by having a specific type of skills (British 
Association for Counselling and Psychotherapy, 2002 and Higson, 2004). Counselling is 
founded on a particular set of traditions of thinking and practices and involves a more 
formalised relationship than what is being talked here. Because of this, the relationship in 
therapy and counselling differs from the helping relationship that are formed generally. 
Helpers must therefore be aware of who they are and be genuine. If as helpers, they don’t 
know who they are, they won’t be able to understand the issues of other people. 
Smith and Smith (2008) contend that in addition to self -awareness, helpers also require a few 
other characteristics. People are pulled into relationships with individuals who are perceived 
as being compassionate, dedicated, and wise when they are looking for someone to assist 
them, reflect on, and improve their lives. They are likely to look around for assistance from 
persons they may readily and confidently approach. They seek people they already know for 
supportive relationships.  
For a helping relationship to be effective, a helper must possess the following skills: 
· Compassion: Being in tune with oneself and other people (in fact, the entire world) is 
what compassion is. It embodies goodness at its most innate level. It is a balance that allows 
love to flow outward without expecting anything in return. People aren’t used as tools and 
exploited in it. It views people as perfect, just the way they are (Brandon, 1990). The way 
many people talk about professionalism makes it difficult to deal with concepts like these, yet 
there is a lot of data that shows people are better equipped to examine concerns and issues 
when they are with a helper who welcomes and respects them, listens, and cares. 
· Caring: It is helpful to first make the distinction between ‘caring about’ and ‘caring 
for’, as Nel Noddings suggests. According to Noddings, showing someone you care for them 
entails feeling pity for them. It also requires being receptive to what the other person is 
saying as well as what they may be feeling and considering. But there is also another aspect 
to this. We must consider the interests of the person we are caring for while making 
58 
 
decisions. Caregivers must react to those that they are responsible for in ways that are, 
ideally, beneficial. One more step is required before this can be referred to be ‘caring’. The 
person being cared for must also have some awareness that an act of care has been 
performed. Caring entails a connection, a relationship, and some degree of reciprocity 
between the caregiver and the one being cared for. Both parties benefit from the relationship 
in various ways. Being cared about is more ethereal. When we talk about caring for someone, 
we typically mean something more subtle than providing them with immediate assistance. 
For instance, we might be concerned about the suffering of people in developing nations. 
This may lead us to do something about it – but the result is rarely cared for.  
· Wisdom: Nel Noddings contends that it is through our families and personal 
connections that we first learn what it is to be cared for. Then, gradually, we learn to care for 
ourselves and, consequently, to care for others (Noddings, 2002). Noddings contends that this 
concern is unquestionably the basis of our feeling of fairness. Helpers, according to Smith 
and Smith (2008), should develop wisdom in both themselves and the people they support. It 
is this quality that draws people to them in particular for assistance. The issue with wisdom is 
that it is typically attributed to specific people by others rather than being asserted by them. It 
typically indicates that the individual so described is seen as possessing a keen understanding, 
a respect for the truth, and the capacity to make wise decisions. According to Smith and 
Smith, this requires helpers to have an understanding of the kinds of things that might 
contribute to happiness and human wellbeing. They also need to be knowledgeable, 
particularly about themselves and their relationships, about ‘what makes people tick’, and 
about the systems of which we are a part. 
1.7.2 Stages of Helping 
Stage theory is frequently used in works that examine counselling and/or assisting 
techniques. When considering counselling or more formal relationships, this is possible 
because they typically entail some kind of written contract or agreement to work together. 
This normally includes information regarding the quantity, time, length, and frequency of 
sessions. Thus, it is rather simple to imagine the various levels or stages that a helping 
relationship might entail. For instance, Gerard Egan’s (2002) influential approach is 
organised around these three stages: 
Stage 1: What is happening? Assisting clients in identifying the main problems that require 
changing. 
Stage II: What options make sense to me in? Assisting clients in setting goals. 
Stage III: What must I do to obtain what I require or desire? Assisting clients in creating 
plans to achieve goals 
Since the 1975 publication of the first version of his book, he has made changes to these 
stages. He then went through the following stages: Establishing and exploring a supportive 
relationship; coming to new conclusions and providing fresh angles; and lastly, taking action 
by assisting the client in adopting those plans. The changes are intriguing since they take into 
account model feedback, research into the assisting process, and years of running training 
programmes. 
59 
 
A three stage concept is also employed by many other authors. The helping or working 
relationship is viewed as having a beginning, middle, and end when it is put into its most 
basic (and likely most beneficial) form (see, for example, Culley and Bond 2004). A 
comparable framework is presented by Alistair Ross in 2003: beginning, developing, and 
letting go. Stage models, however, are less useful to many social pedagogues and informal 
instructors. The type of connection typically involved in informal and community education, 
as well as activities like pastoral care, does not typically entail an explicit contract, and the 
amount of time, duration, and frequency of encounters (rather than meetings) varies greatly. 
For instance, endings might be really abrupt. Having said that, by emphasising beginnings, 
middles, and ends, such models do assist us in considering what may be involved at various 
points in relationships and in creating suitable responses (Smith, 2008). 
1.7.3 Helping as a Skill 
In a lot of the literature on helping and helping relationships, ‘helping skills’ are explored. So 
far, we’ve approached helping as a process and an orientation. It has been asserted that the 
relationship between the helper and the helped and who they are as persons determines 
whether the help is helpful or not. While important, skills are not the primary concern in this 
setting. There is a risk of becoming overly skill -focused. It is simple to lose sight of who we 
are, what our function is, and how we relate to this person as we fall into the pattern of 
practising a specific talent. As an example, consider listening. If we focus too much on 
listening as a skill, we may find ourselves spending more time seeming to listen (through 
posture, expression, and head nodding) than truly doing so.  
If we pay attention to what is being said as well as what is not being said, we will be able to 
convey this to the other person through the questions we pose, the remarks we make, and the 
relationships we establish (Smith, 2007). Having said that, there are some apparent areas of 
skill that we can draw upon, and these have to do with the way in which we encourage 
dialogue and exploration. As an example, we could consider what Sue Culley and Tim Bond 
(2004) refer to as ‘foundation skills’. They group these around three headings, which we have 
discussed in case of counselling as well: Attending and listening; Reflect ive skills; and 
Probing skills. 
Check Your Progress 
 13. What are the stages of helping according to Gerard Egan? 
 14. What are the foundation skills for a helper?  
 
1.8 Counsellor Goal-Setting Process 
Goals are important because it provides a sense of direction and purpose which are necessary 
for a meaningful evaluation of the usefulness of counselling. Patterson (1970) observes that 
the counsellor’s own values enter into counselling and psychotherapy in the choice or 
acceptance of a goal. Krumboltz (1966) believes that the client’s goals should be compatible 
with, though not necessarily identical to the values of their counsellors. Several psychologists 
believe that goals must be specific and mutually a rrived at for every client. Counsellor has 
60 
 
specific goals which are unique to each client and involve the consideration of the client’s 
expectation as well as the environmental aspects. 
Krumboltz (1966) has given three criteria’s for judging counselling goals: 
(i) The goal of counselling should be capable of being stated differently for each 
client. 
 (ii) They should be compatible with the values of the counselling. 
 (iii) The degree to which the goals are attained should be observable. 
George and Christiani have given five major goals of counselling regardless of the school of 
thought to which the counsellor belongs to: 
 (i) Facilitating behaviour change. 
 (ii) Improving client’s ability to establish and maintain relationship. 
 (iii) Enhancing the client’s effectiveness and ability to cope. 
 (iv) Promoting decisions making process. 
 (v) Facilitating client’s potential and development 
Narayan Rao (1989) talks about three types of goals: 
(i) Immediate goals:  Refer to the problem for which the client is seeking 
solution, here and now. 
(ii) Mediate goals and process goals:  They shape the counselee and counsellor 
inter relations and behaviour. They comprise facilitating procedure for 
enhancing the effectiveness of counselling. They include empathic 
understanding, warmth, friendliness which provide for inter -personal 
exploration , which , in turn , helps the client in his/her self -exploration and 
self-understanding and eventually leads to the long range goals. 
(iii) Ultimate long range goals:  Reflect the counsellor’s philosophy of life. It 
includes self -actualization, self -realization, self -enhancement and self -
transcendence to make the client autonomous. 
Parcoff (1961) distinguishes between immediate and ultimate goals. 
According to him, the former refers to the steps and stages in counselling 
process which lead to the realization of the ultimate goal.  
Patterson (1970) gave a model for describing immediate, mediate and ultimate 
goals. These goals are as follows: 
· Immediate goals:  Intrapersonal exploration, self -disclosure, self -exploration 
and self-awareness. 
· Mediate goals: Development of individual potentials, self -understanding, 
education, employment and satisfaction in work. 
61 
 
· Ultimate goals: Self-actualization, self-realization, self-enhancement, high on 
functionality, acceptance of self and others, empathy, deep interpersonal 
relationships, openness to experience, creativity, honesty, responsibility, 
genuineness, responsible, independence and consistency between self and self-
ideal. 
With respect to behavioural change, Krumboltz (1966) postulated three action –oriented 
goals— altering maladaptive behaviour , learning the decision making process, and 
preventing problems. There are then, three basic goals of counselling— self-exploration, self-
understanding, and behavioural change. There is no set time schedule for the 
accomplishments of each, but counsellor behaviour at each stage is designed to facilitate goal 
attainment. 
Nelson and Jones (1982) have given a number of different dimensions against which the 
goals of counselling may be viewed. Goals of counselling also differ with respect to the 
theoretical orientation or approach followed by the counsellor. These goals are given below: 
· Person-centered approach: When the counsellor adopts the person -centred 
approach, then the goals of counselling are realistic perception, openness to 
experience, trust of organismic valuing process, rationality, responsibility, self-regard, 
acceptance of others, and capacity for good personal relations, creativity and ethical 
living. 
· Reality approach: When the counsellor adopts this approach, then the goals 
of counselling are attainment of successful identity, acceptance of reality, acceptance 
of responsibility of one’s own behaviour, capacity to formulate and implement plans, 
involvement with others, and engagement in worthwhile activity and ethical 
behaviour. 
· Rational-emotive approach:  When the counsellor adopts the rational -
emotive approach, then the goals of counselling are rationality, appropriate 
emotionality, maximization of pleasure, minimization of anxiety, minimization of 
hostility, enlightened self-interest, self-direction, tolerance, acceptance of uncertainty, 
flexibility, commitment, risk-taking, self-acceptance. 
· Transactional analysis approach:  When the counsellor adopts the 
transactional analysis approach, then the goals of counselling are autonomous 
behaviour, awareness, spontaneity, intimacy, integrated adult attractiveness and 
responsiveness, objective data processing, personal responsibility, ‘I’m Ok -you are 
Ok’ life position and absence of games. 
· Freudian psychoanalytical approach:  When the counsellor adopts the 
psychoanalytical approach, then the goals of counselling are absence of neurotic 
symptoms, absence of debilitating repressions, ego strength, and ability to meet 
instinctual needs, capacity to love, capacity to work and adherence to realistic moral 
standards. 
· Behavioural approach: When the counsellor adopts the behavioural 
approach, then the goals of counselling are absence of deficits in behaviour, absence 
of weakness in behaviour, absence of mal -adaptive behaviour, absence of debilitating 
62 
 
anxiety reactions, capacity to relax, assertive behaviour, adequate sexual functioning, 
self-control and adequacy as behavioural model. 
· Occupational approach:  When the counsellor adopts the occupational 
approach, then the goals of counselling are optimum matching of person and work, 
formulation of realistic vocational self -concept, adequate exploration of career 
preferences, decision -making skills, absence of maladaptive anxiety reactions, 
capacity to plan, vocational satisfaction and stability, effective performance, 
transcendence of adverse socialization, optimization of opportunity structures and 
healthy leisure behaviour. 
Nelson and Jones (1982) have categorized goals of counselling on seven major dimensions:  
· Medical psychological: Goals may be based on a medical or sickness model 
involving the treatment, and if possible, the cure of mental diseases. Such medical 
classification scheme focuses on mental disorder frequently of an organic nature like 
schizophrenia, paranoid states, neurosis etc. The goals for counselling are in 
psychological rather than medical term. Especially with move severely disturbed 
clients, there may be overlap between the medical and the psychological goals. 
· Ultimate or mediating (Parloff 1961):  Ultimate goal of all therapy is to 
reduce discomfort and increase the effectiveness of the client or patient in his 
biological, personal and social functioning and make him autonomous. These goals 
are same irrespective of the school of thought to which a counsellor belongs to. 
Mediating goals involve the counselling psychologist or clinician’s assumptions about 
the steps to be taken in achieving the ultimate goal and here the goals appear to differ 
from one theory of counselling to another. Thus, the major difference between the 
theoretical positions may revolve around means rather than ends. 
· Internal or external:  Internal goals are those that clients state as their own 
and are frequently stated by them as problems, whereas, external goals are those 
stated for the clients by other people, e.g: parents, spouses or counsellor. Sometimes 
the differences between internal and external goals are difference in the language in 
which they are stated rather then real differences. 
· Intrinsic or derived:  According to Rogerian view, the goals of counselling 
are largely intrinsic to the organism that is to provide the emotional climate with core 
condition in which client’s can become more attuned to and act on their basic 
directional tendency towards self -actualization. Humanist’s believe that the goals of 
counselling should be to help the client to engage in a continuous process of 
autonomous self -realizing. However, behaviourists like skinner believe that 
counselling goals are largely determined by or derived from the environmental 
contingencies of reinforcement in their societies. 
· General or specific: Goals may be stated in broad terms, such as a high level 
of self-realizing (humanistic theorist) or in specific terms (behaviourists), such as the 
steps in a behavioural counselling approach using successive approximation to 
acquire a particular piece of behaviour systematically or to counter condition a 
specific phobic area. The issue of general and specific goals is related to theoretical 
assumptions about human nature and the ways in which people acquire, perpetuate 
63 
 
and can change their behaviour. Sometimes in practice, counsellors offer their clients 
a basic helping relationship focused on improving their general sense of worth and 
ability to cope with life, at the same time focusing on specific areas of behaviour or 
thinking when they and their clients deem it appropriate. 
· Goals as developmental tasks: Some writers like Super and Ginzberg believe 
that it is hard to consider counselling goals independent of the individual’s age and 
hence developmental status in his culture or sub -culture. Erikson’s eight ages of man 
and their developmental conflict is based on this assumption. These ages and conflicts 
are given below 
Age Conflict 
Oral sensory Basic trust vs Mistrust 
Muscular-Anal Autonomy vs Shame doubt 
Locomotor-Genital Initiative vs Guilt 
Latency Industry vs Inferiority 
Puberty and Adolescence Industry vs Role confusion 
Young adulthood Intimacy vs Isolation 
Adulthood Generativity vs Stagnation 
Maturity Ego integrity vs Despair 
 
               
Another developmental view point, which focuses on behavioural goals, is that of 
Havighurst. His six major age periods, with illustrative developmental tasks are: 
· Infancy and early childhood (birth to 6 years) — forming concepts and 
developing language. 
· Middle childhood (age 6 years to 12 years) —Learning an appropriate 
masculine or feminine social role 
· Adolescence (age 12 years to 15 years) —Achieving more mature relations 
with both sexes. 
 · Early adulthood (ages 18 years to 30 years)—Getting started in an occupation. 
 · Middle age (age 30 years to 60 years)— Existing teenage children. 
· Later maturity (age 60 years +) — Adjusting to decreasing physical strength 
and health. 
Topic area 
Another dimension against which the goals of counselling are frequently viewed is that of 
topic area. A common distinction is that between personal, educational, occupational 
counselling and their related goals. For instance, goals in the topic area of personal relations 
might include increasing socially assertive responses and learning to discriminate between 
insults and friendly teasing. These goals might than be further broker down into sub goals. 
Individual, institutional, and social 
Many counselling goals are stated in terms of individual and small group. However, a number 
of theorists such as Rogers, Freud, Skinner are concerned with large group and societal goals. 
64 
 
At the institutional and societal levels counselling goals may provide a basis for social and 
political change. 
According to Narayan Rao, some of the major goals of counselling generally accepted by 
counsellors are given below:  
· Achievement of the mental health:  The need of mental health is identified as an 
‘important goal of counselling by some individual who claim that when one reaches or attains 
positive mental health, one learns to adjust and responds more positively to people and 
situations’. Others believe that prevention of emotional tensions, anxieties, indecision and 
similar problems is also an important goal of counselling. They hold the view that 
counselling should lead to positive feelings of warmth and of being liked. Kell and Mueller 
(1962) hold that the promotion and development of feelings of being liked, sharing with, and 
receiving and giving interaction rewards from other human beings is the legitimate goals of 
counselling. 
· Resolution of problems: In behavioural terms, three categories of behavioural goals 
can be identified, namely, “altering maladaptive behaviour, learning the decision making 
process and preventing problems (Krumboltz, 1966).” Wolpe (1958) feels that the goal of 
counselling is to relieve the suffering and disability of the clients. 
· Improving personal effectiveness: There is a close relation between the preservation 
of good mental health and securing desirable behavioural changes. Blocher(1966) defines an 
effective person as “one who is able to commit himself to projects, investing time and energy 
and willing to take appropriate economic, psychological and physical risks. He is seen as 
having the competence to recognize, define and solve problems. He is seen reasonably 
consistent outside and within his typical role situation. He is seen as being able to think in 
different and original, that is, creative ways. Finally, he is able to control impulses and 
produce appropriate responses to frustration, hostility and ambiguity.” 
· Counselling to help change: According to Blocher (1966), there are two additional 
goals of counselling. One is that counselling should maximize individual freedom to choose 
and act within the conditions imposed by the environment. And the second goal is that, 
counselling should increase the effectiveness of the individual responses evolved by the 
environment. Tiedeman(1964) holds that the goal of counselling is to focus on the mechanism 
of change and that the counsellor should be helped in the process of becoming. Shoben(1965) 
also considers personal development as the goal of counselling. 
· Decision making as a goal of counselling:  It is through the process of making 
critical decisions that personal growth is fostered. Reaves and Reaves (1965) point out that 
the objective of counselling is to stimulate the individual to evaluate, make, accept and act 
upon his choice. Counselling enables the individual to make decision independently. In other 
words, the counsellor should know how and why the client made the concerned decisions. In 
the process of decision -making, information may be required and the counsellor should 
provide it or help obtain it. The information might have to be clarified, sorted out and 
analysed. The counsellor should help in such a manner that the client is able to make 
responsible decisions. Tyler (1961) also defines the goal of counselling as to help the 
counselee utilize the resources - intra personal and environmental –available and make 
decisions based on them to cope with life situations. 
65 
 
  Sometimes the clients have goals which are vague and their implications are 
not fully appreciated. In that case, it is the job of a counsellor to clarify the counsellor’s goal 
based on is background, professional training and rich experience. 
  Sometimes some goals may reflect the subjective expectations of the 
counsellors themselves. The different goals elucidated by the counsellor gives the counselee 
the choice of making free, informed and responsible decisions, being fully conscious of their 
own strengths, weaknesses, assets, and abilities. 
· Modification of the behaviour as a goal:  Stress the need for modification of 
behaviour is advocated by Behaviour oriented counsellors, for example, removal of 
undesirable behaviour or action or reduction of a maladaptive behaviour such that the 
individual attains satisfaction and effectiveness. Similarly, growth–oriented counsellors stress 
on the development of potentialities within the individual. Existentially -oriented counsellors 
stress self enhancement and self -fulfilment. One needs to remember that one cannot obtain 
self enhancement or self fulfilment without first removing undesirable behaviour. 
1.8.1 Limitations of Goals 
The various goals of counselling also have certain limitations as counselling goals frequently 
have to ‘tailor-made’ to meet the realities of clients and their situations. Also goals vary from 
client to client, from one counsellor’s theoretical orientation to the other, and also from one 
problem to the other. Thus limitations in goals are caused by the client, the counsellor or the 
contextual considerations. 
In short, the counsellor’s decisions about goals are often more complex than they appear at 
first. It is important for counsellors to think through their goals in respect of both their overall 
roles and how they behave with specific clients or groups of clients. While formulating goals 
the counsellor needs to become aware of some of their underlying or hidden assumptions as 
counsellors, like this client, have to resist tendencies to engage in self-protection.

 15. What are the criteria for judging counselling’s goals?  
 16. What are the ultimate long-range goals? 
 
1.9 Counselling in India 
Since the beginning of time, counselling in its broadest sense has existed in some form or 
another. In every society, the elders not only established the cultural norms of behaviour but 
also advised the younger generations to adhere to them. In India, parents and teachers in 
particular believed that one of their fundamental and sacred duties was to provide counselling 
in the form of advice and direction. The frequently recited proverb ‘ Mata, Pita, Guru, 
Devam’ (Mother, Father, Teacher, God) served as a reminder to the young people of both the 
counsellors and the importance of who should provide counselling at different periods of life. 
India’s ancient epics are rife with different forms of counselling. 
 Elders were more than willing to take on the role of counsellors, and children quickly 
complied when they sought counsel. Many of these situations may be brushed off as simple 
66 
 
instances of ‘giving advice’. However, it is not difficult to recognise the scientific 
methodology and moral principles of contemporary counselling procedures in the majority of 
those historic events. The conversation between Krishna and Arjuna on the battlefield of 
Kurukshetra is the most famous counselling scenario in the epics. Devoted researchers in this 
field may need to provide an answer as to whether this discourse exhibited all the traits of 
contemporary counselling. The applicability and relevance of contemporary counselling 
practices in various cultures are frequently questioned. 
 Naturally, the beginnings of psychology in India should be the starting point of the 
counselling movement in the country. The first Chair in psychology was endowed at the 
Mysore University in south India. A psychology department at the Calcutta University was 
established not more than a year prior to this, with a lecturer serving as the Head of the 
Department. Since that time, more than 20 years, psychology has made little progress at the 
other universities in India. Midway through the 1940s, Patna University established a 
psychology department, which was swiftly followed by similar establishments at Banaras 
Hindu University, Lucknow University, and other universities. 
 However, a large portion of the advancement shown in the creation of new 
departments occurred in the 1960s. Since then, the various departments have been focusing 
on experimental approach as they prepare students for postgraduate degrees in psychology. 
Alongside this development, applied psychology became established as a separate division 
inside the existing psychology departments. Both the department of psychological services at 
Patna University and the applied psychology branch of the Calcutta University were founded 
in 1938 and 1945, respectively. However, at universities where psychology was provided, 
counselling psychology or its predecessor, vocational counselling, did not rank as an essential 
service. 
 In order to offer counselling on a small scale, voluntary private organisations were 
first created in Calcutta and thereafter in Bombay. The David Hare Training College in 
Calcutta came to be identified with the guidance movement. In 1941, Batliboy and 
Mukherjee established the Batliboy V ocational Guidance Bureau in Bombay. Since they had 
previously worked in the Calcutta Bureau, they already had some experience. The Batliboy 
bureau operated for six years before ceasing to exist. However, at this time it provided the 
community with useful services. It successfully carried out a brief orientation course in 
teacher guidance for the first time. This was the precursor to all subsequent career masters 
training programmes offered in various locations throughout India (Khorshed, 1963).  
 The Acharya Narendra Dev committee’s 1938 report on the value of counselling and 
assistance in education led to the recognition of counselling as a crucial service in India. 
Counseling and guidance were viewed as new, developing forces that were crucial to the 
educational system. In the 1980s and 1990s, there was not the same enthusiasm  and interest 
in counselling and guidance. The quantity of research material that was accessible for 
evaluation provided evidence of this fall in interest. Despite all of th is, there has been a sharp 
rise in demand for counselling services nationally in recent years. In 2005, the NCERT 
included counselling as a core service in its national framework curriculum. The school 
industry has given counselling the most attention. The CBSE decided to make qualified 
school counsellors a requirement for all of its schools during its National Conference in 2001. 
Over the past few years, more training alternatives have become accessible, ranging from 
67 
 
 
full-time postgraduate degree programmes to certifications and diplomas. Few university 
departments of psychology, education, and social work provide postgraduate degrees. There 
is a postgraduate diploma in guiding and counselling offered by NCERT, Government of 
India. Private organisations provide postgraduate certificates and diplomas in particular 
counselling areas. The option of distance learning is offered for certificate programmes. 
According to experts in this subject, India’s culture may have impacted by contemporary 
counselling practices. 
 Counselling’s actual nature and purpose are still not well understood. Since anyone 
can become a counsellor, there is currently no licencing system in place, and there is no 
organised way to keep track of skill development. The cultural sensitivity of current curricula 
was recently evaluated, and the results showed that relatively few Indian counselling models 
have developed (Arulmani, 2007). The effort appears to have been to adapt western ideas 
without much thought —if any —to ‘discovering’ fresh ideas and validating them for the 
context of India. A gradual  movement towards more varied counselling approaches is more 
encouraging. 
 Even though there aren’t many of these courses, the ones that do exist have useful 
learning objectives that aim to introduce students to the availability and potential of 
alternative healing methods with a particular emphasis on indigenous and culturally 
acceptable and used therapeutic approaches. The availability of counselling services is a 
serious issue. Less than 10% of the sample, according to a 2006 survey by Arulmani and Nag 
in 12 distinct Indian regions, had access to any kind of counselling. The function of a 
counsellor is also not entirely clear. The full spectrum of mental needs is covered by referrals 
to counsellors, from severe psychotic problems to concerns about parenting, childhood 
disorders, adolescent struggles, and reproductive health (including HIV/AIDS issues). Other 
types of referrals a counsellor might get include those involving marital conflict, 
interpersonal issues, academic and educational challenges, stress -related disorders, substance 
addiction, career development counselling, and inquiries concerning sexual orientation.

 18. What is the contribution of NCERT and CBSE to the counselling movement in   
India? 
 
1.10 Answers to Check Your Progress Questions 
1. According to Pepinsky and Pepinsky (1954), counselling refers to an ‘interaction 
which occurs between two individuals called the counsellor and the client that takes 
place in a professional setting and is initiated and maintained to facilitate changes in 
the behaviour of the client’. 
2. Several misconceptions  are held by people about counselling. Some of the serious 
misconceptions held about counselling are as follows:  
a. Counselling is all about giving information, although some information giving can be  
a part of counselling. 
68 
 
b. Counselling is about giving advice, making suggestions and recommendations. 
3. Clinical psychology is a branch of psychology which is concerned with the 
description, classification, diagnosis, and prognosis of psychological illnesses.  
4. Some works during the testing movement which had a significant impact on the 
profession of counselling: 
a. Sir Francis Galton tried to devise a simple measure of intelligence.  
b. Binet’s development of first IQ tests; Lewis M. Terman’s administration of the test to 
a large population  
c. Edward L. Thorndike’s myriad contributions. 
5. Verbal behaviours like probing, requesting, clarification, re -statement and 
summarizing feelings and non -verbal behaviour like smiling, learning forward, 
making eye contact, gesturing and head moving indicate that the counsellor is 
interested in and is open to clients. Egan (1990), summarized five non -verbal skills as 
SOLER (smile, open-posture, learning forward, eye-contact and relax). 
6. Four possible benefits of an exit interview according to Ward (1984) are: 
a. It helps the clients resolve any positive feelings resulting from the counselling 
experiences itself. 
b. It serves as a way to invite the client to continue in counselling if the client so wishes. 
c. Another form of treatment or a different counsellor can be considered if the client 
wishes.  
d. It increases chances that next time the client needs help, he or she will seek 
counselling. 
7. Some of the dysfunctional reasons behind becoming a counsellor according to James 
Guy (1987) are as follows: 
a. Emotional distress: Some individuals who have unresolved personal traumas wish to 
become counsellors in order to deal with their emotional distress. 
b. Vicarious coping: Some people take up the profession of counselling as a way to live 
their lives through others. 
8. A comfortable and attractive room endowed with soft lightening, quiet colour, 
harmonious, and comfortable furniture (with an absence of clutter and distraction) 
should be used for counselling. A distance of 30 to 39 inches between the counsellor 
and client and chair of 90 degree angel from one another promote counselling 
relationship. 
9. Psychoanalysis seems to be effective in treating several disorders like hysteria, 
narcissism, obsessive compulsive disorder, anxiety, phobia, sexual difficulties, etc. 
(Arlow, 1989). 
69 
 
10. Some techniques used in existential counselling include (i) free to use techniques as 
widely diversified as desensitization and free association; (ii) confrontation; (iii) 
paradoxical intention (iv) imagery exercises, awareness exercises, and goal setting 
activities are borrowed from other models. 
11. Some common negative script patterns are: (i) never scripts; (ii) until scripts; (iii) 
always scripts; (iv) after scripts; and (v) open-ended scripts. 
12. Rational Emotive Therapy (RET) makes use of two primary techniques: teaching and 
disputing. 
13. The stages of helping according to Gerard Egan’s (2002) influential approach is 
organised around these three stages: 
a. Stage 1: What is happening? Assisting clients in identifying the main problems that 
require changing. 
b. Stage II: What options make sense to me in? Assisting clients in setting goals. 
c. Stage III:  What must I do to obtain what I require or desire? Assisting clients in 
creating plans to achieve goals 
14. The foundation skills for a helper according to Sue Culley and Tim Bond 
(2004) are: Attending and listening; Reflective skills; and Probing skills. 
 15. Krumboltz (1966) has given three criteria’s for judging counselling goals: 
a. The goal of counselling should be capable of being stated differently for each client. 
b. They should be compatible with the values of the counselling. 
c. The degree to which the goals are attained should be observable. 
16. The ultimate long range goals reflect the counsellor’s philosophy of life. It 
includes self -actualization, self -realization, self -enhancement and self -transcendence 
to make the client autonomous. 
17. The David Hare Training College in Calcutta came to be identified with the 
guidance movement. 
18. In 2005, the NCERT included counselling as a core service in its national 
framework curriculum. The school industry has given counselling the most attention. 
The CBSE decided to make qualified school counsellors a requirement for all of its 
schools during its National Conference in 2001. 
1.11 Summary 
• According to, Pepinsky and Pepinsky (1954), counselling refers to an ‘interaction 
which occurs between two individuals called the counsellor and the client that 
takes place in a professional setting and is initiated and maintained to facilitate 
changes in the behaviour of the client’. 
70 
 
• Problems can occur/recur during any point in life, therefore counselling must 
necessarily be an ongoing and continuing process concerning persons of all age 
levels and placed in different life situations. 
• Counselling is characterized as a professional activity or a process that deals with 
‘comparatively normal psychologically healthy people’. 
• The counselling profession has its foundations in: laboratory psychology; 
innovative approaches to the treatment of the ‘insane’; the mental hygiene 
movement; the mental testing movement; the influence of humanistic psychology; 
and the vocational guidance movement. 
• Despite all the early school theorists who tried to investigate the complexities of 
the human psyche, there were no psychological attempts to treat emotional 
problems or emotional illnesses.  
• Greatly influenced by Wundt’s work in Europe, several students from United 
States came to his laboratory to learn the new experimental methods being used 
there.  
• The impact of Existential approaches have been significant in the areas of 
vocational counselling, job training, personal work, and rehabilitation counselling. 
• The counselling process begins with the initial session. The initial counselling 
interview is often critical to the ultimate success or failure of the entire course of 
the counselling process.  
• Basic attending behaviour and client observation skills helps in establishing and 
maintaining rapport which is vital for the disclosure of information and the 
ultimate success of counselling. 
• Just as the initial interview and building of a sound client -counsellor relationship 
is essential for the counselling process to be effective, so is the careful termination 
of the counselling relationship. 
• When to terminate the relationship is a question that has no definite answer. If the 
relationship ends too soon, the client may regress to earlier behaviour but if 
termination is never addressed, client may become too dependent. 
• Patterson and Eisenberg (1983) note that resistance is especially likely when the 
counselling relationship has been of long duration or has involved a high level of 
intimacy. 
• Sometimes narcissistic and grandiose people take up the profession of counselling 
believing that all problems are resolved through the expression of love and 
tenderness. 
• Overall, the effectiveness of counselling depends on numerous variables including 
the background, education, skill and experience of the counsellor. 
71 
 
• Rollo May and others believe that individual who have experience some 
emotional distress and have been able to successfully resolve it are better able to 
connect with their clients authentically in ways not possible otherwise. 
• Before a counsellor can attempt to understand a client, he or she must have an 
objective and satisfying understanding of the self. Counsellors must be able to 
recognize and accept their strengths and weaknesses.  
• The qualities that help a counsellor to relate and communicate more effectively 
with the client are genuineness, non -dominance, listening, positive regard, and 
communication skills. 
• Most studies agree that personnel traits are important in determining counsellor’s 
effectiveness for e.g., Jovne (1981) found that counsellor’s personality 
characteristics are more important than skills. 
• According to Rogers (1962) it means prizing the client as a person regardless of 
his particular behaviour at the moment. 
• Building a relationship, the first stage of counselling, is a continuous process. It 
begins by having the counsellor win the battle for structure and the client win the 
battle for initiative. 
• Freud was the creator of the psychoanalytic approach. The Freudian view of 
human nature is essentially pessimistic, deterministic, mechanistic, and 
reductionalistic. 
• Behaviourists believe learning and thus development occurs in one of the three 
following ways: (i) Respondent learning; (ii) Operant Conditioning ; (iii) Social 
modelling. 
• Cognitive theories of counselling focus on thinking. They believe that thoughts 
influence feelings and behaviours. If a person changes a way of thinking, feelings 
and behaviours will get modified automatically.  
• Rational Emotional Therapy is based on the assumption that psychopathology is 
originally learned and aggravated by the inculcation of irrational beliefs from 
significant others during childhood. 
• An integrative approach to counselling and psychotherapy is best characterized by 
attempts to look beyond and across the confines of single -school approaches in 
order to see what can be learned from, and how clients can benefit from, other 
perspectives. 
• Helping is a bond created between two or more individuals with the intention of 
providing and receiving assistance. Helpers must be able to comprehend emotions 
and experiences by putting themselves in the client’s shoes. 
• Helpers focus on education, relationships, and working with others to put their 
knowledge into practise. But it also overlaps with the counselling field. They 
72 
 
achieve this not by adopting the role of counsellor but rather by having a specific 
type of skills. 
• Being in tune with oneself and other people (in fact, the entire world) is what 
compassion is. It embodies goodness at its most innate level. 
• It has been asserted that the relationship between the helper and the helped and 
who they are as persons determines whether the help is helpful or not. 
• Goals are important because it provides a sense of direction and purpose which 
are necessary for a meaningful evaluation of the usefulness of counselling. 
• The various goals of counselling also have certain limitations as counselling goals 
frequently have to ‘tailor-made’ to meet the realities of clients and their situations. 
• Naturally, the beginnings of psychology in India should be the starting point of the 
counselling movement in that country. The first Chair in psychology was endowed 
at the Mysore University in south India. 
 · In order to offer counselling on a small scale, voluntary private organisations 
were first created in Calcutta and thereafter in Bombay. The David Hare Training College in 
Calcutta came to be identified with the guidance movement. 
1.12 Key Words 
· Vocational Guidance: It is  the process that helps students to access their career 
development as well as helps to enhance their skills, interests, as well as capabilities. 
· Functionalist: Functionalism is a theory about the nature of mental states. According 
to functionalists, mental states are identified by what they do rather than by what they are 
made of. 
· APA: The American Psychological Association (APA) is the largest scientific and 
professional organization of psychologists in the United States, with over 133,000 members, 
including scientists, educators, clinicians, consultants, and students.  
· CBSE: The Central Board of Secondary Education (CBSE) is a national level board 
of education in India for public and private schools, controlled and managed by the 
Government of India.
