2.1 Objectives 
After going through this unit, you will be able to:  
• Discuss counselling techniques, along with generic, micro and macro counselling 
skills 
• Explain the inside-outside and self-monitoring skills of counselling 
• Describe the indigenous model of counselling 
2.2 Introduction 
In order to build a solid bond between the client and the therapist, effective counselling skills 
are essential. When combined, these skills provide clients with treatment support and assist 
them in achieving their objective of overcoming the demands of modern life and having a 
more happy life. To encourage and maintain the psychological process, counsellors can study 
and improve a variety of counselling skills, such as effective communication, problem 
solving, and goal setting, as well as teach coping mech anisms like self-talk and visualisation. 
In this unit, we will discuss the counselling techniques, along with the generic, micro and 
75 
 
macro counselling skills. It will also focus on inside -outside and self -monitoring skills of 
counselling, along with an indigenous model of counselling.  
2.3 Counselling Techniques: Building Relationship, Core Conditions, and In -Depth 
Exploration 
The successful outcome of any counselling relationship depends on the working alliance 
between the counsellor and the client (Gesto and Carter, 1985). Clients arrive in counselling 
with certain cues of their lives open or understood and other areas hidden or suppressed; this 
is often represented by a conceptual device called the ‘Johari Window’ (Luft, 1970). 
At this time, involvement and exploration phases of helping are involved. After they are 
completed the counsellor works with the client to move into the understanding and action 
phases (Carkhuff and Anthony, 1979).To work effectively through, these phases several skills 
(as follows) are involved: 
 ·Changing perceptions: Through the process of reframing, the counsellor helps the 
clients to change distorted unrealistic objective and to move from functional fixity to a more 
appropriate and realistic objective by offering them opportunity to explore ‘thoughts and 
desires within a safe, accepting and non-judgmental environment. Goals are refined or altered 
using cognitive, behaviour, or cognitive -behaviour strategies (Okun, 1987)’. Also reframing 
offers the client another probable viewpoint of why an event might have happened. 
 ·Leading: Counsellors may use leads that vary in length and depends on the 
counsellor’s theoretical orientation and current phase of counselling to change the client’s 
perceptions (Robinson, 1950). Leads like silence, acceptance, and paraphrasing may be more 
appropriate in the beginning of the counselling process, whereas, others like persuasion may 
be more appropriate in the understanding and action phases of counselling (Patterson and 
Eisenberg, 1982). 
 ·Multi-focussed responding: Ivey (1983) and Lazarus (1981) think that ‘tuning into 
the client’s major modes (visual, auditory, kinesthetic) of perceiving and learning is crucial in 
bringing about change’. Baruth and Huber (1985), also point out the importance of the 
counsellor’s responding in the client’s language. 
 ·Accurate empathy:  Primary accurate empathy helps in the establishment of the 
counselling relationship, in data gathering and in problem clarification. 
 ·Self-disclosure: to be effective, the counsellor’s self -disclosure should match the 
client’s needs (Hendrick, 1988). Selective and focussed self -disclosure serves to primary 
functions namely ——modelling and developing a new perspective (Egan, 1990). ‘Client’s 
are more likely to trust counsellors who disclose their own personal information and are 
prone to make reciprocal disclosure if the client/counsellor relationship is strong (Curtis, 
1981)’. 
 ·Immediacy: Involves counsellor’s understanding and communication of the client’s 
feelings, impressions and expectations and the wants of the counsellor (Turock, 1980). 
Relationship immediacy is the counsellor’s ability to discuss with the client the quality of this 
relationship, whether it is strained, boring or productive. Here -and-now immediacy is the 
discussion of any given transaction as it is happening (Egan, 1990). Misinterpretation of the 
76 
 
counsellor’s message, unexpected outcome, influence on the client’s decision to terminate the 
counselling session are three main fears counsellor’s have with regard to immediacy (Turock, 
1980). It is best used in a directionless relationship where there is question of trust, 
considerable social distance between counsellor and client, client dependency, counter -
dependency and where there is an attraction between counsellor and client (Egan, 1990). 
 ·Reflection: Emphatic mirroring of feelings behind the words is called reflection. 
 ·Humour: Sensitivity and timing on the part of the counsellor is most 
important.Humour can circumvent client resistance, dispel tension and reduce psychological 
pain (Ness, 1989). Through an experience of ‘ha-ha’ often comes an awareness of ‘ah-ah’ and 
a clearer perception of a situation. 
 ·Confrontation: It challenges clients to examine, modify and or control an aspect of 
behaviour. A good responsible, caring, and appropriate confrontation produces growth and 
encourages an honest examination of oneself. It should be used only when the relationship is 
strong. 
 ·Probing: Refers to the use of close -ended or open -ended questions to elicit 
information. Why question should be avoided as they make the client defensive. 
 ·Contracting: There are two aspects of contracting —one focuses on the processes 
involved in reaching the goal; the other concentrates on the final outcome. While making 
contracts the counsellor must consider the background of the client, client’s motivational 
levels, the nature of the present problem and what resources are available to the client to 
assure the successful completion of the contract. Thomas and Ezell (1972) lists several 
advantages as follows: 
• Provides a written record of goals the counsellor and the client have agreed to 
pursue and the course of action to be taken. 
• The formal nature of a contract and its time limits may act as motivator for a client 
who tends to procrastinate 
• If the contract is broken down into definable sections, a client may feel that 
problems can be solved 
• Puts responsibility for any change on the client and thus serves as motivation 
• By specifically outlining the number of sessions to be held, it assumes that the 
client will return to counselling on a more regular basis than would otherwise be 
the case. 
Some of the limitations of contracting according to Thomas and Ezell (1972) as follows: 
 · Counsellor cannot hold a client to a contract 
 · Some client problems may not lend themselves to the contract system 
 · Such a way of dealing with problems focuses on the outward behaviour 
 · Also, the initial appeal of the contract is limited. 
77 
 
 ·Rehearsal: Refers to practising designated behaviour overtly (verbalize or act out 
what is going to do) or covertly (imagine or reflect on the desired goals) (Cormier and 
Cormier,1991). In rehearsal the feedback given by counselling helps the client recognize and 
correct any problem area (Geis an Chapman, 1971). 
In addition to the specific skills involved, clients and counsellors must work through any 
transference or counter transference issues that arise out of earlier situations or present 
circumstances (Gelso and Carter, 1985). 
Transference is the projection by the client of the past or present feelings, attitudes or desires 
onto the counsellor (Brammer, Shostrom and Abrego, 1989). It may be direct (expressed 
directly) or indirect (revealed in client’s statements or actions that are not directly relate to the 
counsellor) or positive (less harming) or negative (very harmful to the positive relationship). 
According to Cavanagh (1982)’ both positive and negative transference are forms of 
resistance. 
Working through the transference helps the client have more trust and confidence in the 
counsellor. Insight into the past and thus become free to act differently in present and future. 
Counter-transference refers to the counsellor’s projection of emotional reactions towards the 
client (Hausen, Stevic and Warner, 1986), thereby destroying the objectivity which is 
detrimental to counselling relationship. One approach sees counter -transference as the direct 
or indirect unconscious reactions of the counsellor to the client. Other see it as a diagnostic 
tool for understanding aspects of the client’s unconscious motivation (Kernberg, 1975). Still 
another conceives it as possessing both the above positive and negative aspects (Blanck and 
Blanck, 1979). It may be manifested in a number of ways (Corey et al, 1988): 
· Constant desire of the counsellor to please the client 
· Identify with the clients problems thereby losing objectivity 
· Counsellor’s development of sexual or romantic feelings towards the client 
· Compulsive advice giving 
· A desire to develop a social relationship with the client 
Like clients, counsellors have blind spots, hidden areas and aspects of their lives that are 
unknown to them. Working in a supervisory relationship with another counsellor, monitoring 
one’s own audio and videotapes of counselling sessions and self -analysis are excellent ways 
to address countertransference. 
2.3.1 Deeper Exploration Skills  
Deeper exploration aims at helping clients discover their true self. In counselling, a client’s 
revelation of self or concerns may not be complete because of ‘hidden areas’ and ‘blind 
spots’. 
 ·Hidden areas: A client may not want to disclose some of his thoughts and feelings 
or even certain incidents of the past.  
 ·Blind spots: A client may not know his or her inner feelings. He is either dimly 
aware of them or totally unaware of certain dimensions of his personality and orientations.  
78 
 
Deeper explorations thus help deeper understanding of the client’s concerns. Egan (1986) 
refers to this as advanced empathy. A counsellor may go for deep probing using questions, 
statements and reflections. Alternatively, challenging can be used.  
Challenging  
Challenging is a powerful approach to shift the client from one perspective to another. Instead 
of retaining a frame of reference and struggling to fit everything in it, it seeks to explore a 
new perspective by taking a new route of exploration. This process helps to discover the 
unknown weaknesses and strengths of clients and leads to identification of more constructive 
and useful solutions. Challenging aims at the following:  
 ·Finding gaps in information by identifying deficits, discrepancies, uncertainty in 
outcomes, self-defeating beliefs and unexpressed feelings.  
 ·Probing deep to bridge the gaps in information by discovering hidden concerns.  
 ·Searching to find new vistas of thoughts and feelings, to find an expression so far 
undiscovered and the potential of the client. 
 Challenging can be done in many ways. However, to be effective, challenging should 
be purposeful and thought provoking. Some useful tips to challenging are given here. 
 ·Be tentative: As a rule, a counsellor should never take full control of the thought 
process of a client. As such, a proposed view should be tentative and open for modification. 
You might say: ‘I wonder whether…’, ‘I guess or my hunch is …’ ‘How will it be if you...’. 
 ·Do not blame: The aim of challenging is to find gaps, discrepancies and 
inconsistencies in thoughts and so on. The loopholes or loose ends are to be used for further 
exploration but not for negatively criticizing the client. Having a positive regard for the client 
is important for counselling. Acknowledge the inconsistencies in a subtle and gentle manner 
and seek information to gain new understanding.  
 ·Be concrete: A challenge should be specific and clear. Vague expressions do not 
help. They may result in the client deviating from the expected line of thought. As a result, 
interaction may become less useful and sometimes complex.  
 ·Persist to achieve: Challenging may require a series of questions and moving 
through a sequence of interactions. It cannot be done by a single question or statement. It 
requires pursuing all the aspects under consideration till a full picture is obtained.  
Types of challenges  
There are a good number of alternative challenging strategies. These are given in brief in the 
following lines:  
 ·Confrontation: The counsellor confronts the client to make him aware of any faulty, 
distorted and baseless feelings or views that have been immobilizing or disempowering his 
personality. Weak-minded people try to hide their weaknesses by saying aloud something 
which they neither intend to do nor are capable of doing. They resort to the following actions. 
79 
 
o Rationalizing: They try to supply logic to their thoughts or actions. Consider this 
example: Client: I lost my job. There is an economic meltdown. Everybody is losing 
their jobs. 
o Delaying: Some clients keep on postponing actions to avoid facing them. Maybe they 
have bitter feelings or fear in facing them.  
o Blaming others: This is common in most cases. Instead of accepting the failure 
people often ‘pass the buck’ to others. They find relief in blaming others for their 
involvement or contribution. Though it is irrational and unacceptable, people do it to 
retain their identity or safeguard their ego. 
· Self-confrontation: The counsellor asks the client to talk to himself, pointing 
out his weaknesses and strengths. Generally, clients may either concentrate on self -
blame or self -praise. The client has to be guided to take a balanced view. It should 
help them to see through themselves and listen to themselves. Such self -examination 
and introspection will enhance their own understanding.  
· Feedback: Feedback refers to return communication to the client based on 
what the client has said. Such a communication should aim at helping the client to 
take a new angle or explore a new dimension. Feedback can be both positive and 
negative. See the example cited here:  
  Client: I hate my boss. I don’t think I can work under him. 
  Counsellor: You said in the beginning you liked him a lot. 
  Client: Yes! But not now. I put up with him all these days. 
  Counsellor: You have tolerance toward mistakes. (Positive feedback) 
 Client: I have tolerated the mistakes of many. Take the case of my team 
leader. He took so many wrong decisions. I told him several times to be more careful. 
What did he do in return? He pointed out his failures as my mistakes. I hate him. 
  Counsellor: You said you hate your team leader. 
Client: Yes. Not only for blaming me unnecessarily but also for saying it in 
front of others. 
Counsellor: You seem to be emotional and quick in forming impressions. 
(Negative feedback) 
· Additional information: Clients may lack the information necessary to make 
radical and different decisions. In career counselling, for example, the client 
may not have information about the career options or educational 
opportunities. Make sure that the information provided to the client has the 
following characteristics. 
o Relevant: Check for the relevance of the information to the client based on 
understanding of attitudes, expectations and capabilities.  
80 
 
o Precise: Information has value when put in precise form. Unnecessary elaboration 
leads to information overload and sometimes the right information may be lost in the 
maze.  
o Concrete: Information must be clear and realistic. It should be action -oriented and 
practical.  
o Not advice: Information should be neutral in presentation. It should not suggest an 
action to the client. It should be descriptive and not prescriptive.  
·Directives: A powerful way of challenging is instructing the client to do a particular 
thing. Examples:  
o ‘Say more about your feelings.’ 
o ‘Explain more about your experience.’  
o ‘Do not shift from this; elaborate and explain.’  
Immediacy 
Immediacy refers to focusing on the present. This is helpful in two contexts:  
 ·To focus on the future: Generally, the thought process progresses from past to 
present and present to future. After the client has finished talking about past events, feelings 
and views relating to his or her concerns, consolidating the discussion for future thinking is 
important. It is pertinent to focus on the present. Relevant questions are: What are your 
feelings now? How do you view your concerns today? 
 ·To move when stuck: When both the counsellor and the client are stuck during their 
interaction, the counsellor may use this tactic to progress. By opening a new window, he can 
lead the client to think about the issue with renewed energy.  
 ·To examine relationships: Another aspect that requires attention is the client –
counsellor relationship. Factors like trust and emotional feelings require scrutiny. 
o Is the relationship going to increase dependence on the counsellor in future?  
o Will the relationship, on termination, result in bitter feelings and negative thoughts in 
the client?  
o Is the relationship leading to unethical sexual relations or victimization of the client in 
any way? 
Check Your Progress 
 1. Define countertransference.  
 2. What are the limitations of contracting?  
2.4 Nature and Importance OF Generic Skills of Counselling 
A well-trained counsellor is a professional who can combine science with art. His approach to 
problem solving will be scientific and creative whereas his interactions with the client will be 
81 
 
humanistic. A client comes for counselling not only with a problem (latent or manifest) but 
also with emotions like fear, anger, disgust, etc.,  
 
These emotions, together with information they have about counselling, will form a set of 
expectations—positive or negative in the client’s mind. Some of the positive emotions are:  
·Effortless understanding: The counsellor is an expert; he will know my problems 
without my doing much talking.  
·Quick solution: The counsellor will immediately remove all my sufferings. He has a 
magic wand.  
·Right answer: I need not struggle any longer to find a solution. The counsellor has 
the right answers.  
 ·Enthusiasm: Well! From tomorrow I will be different. I am meeting a counsellor.  
 ·Relief: Thank God! I have a counsellor. I am not alone. He will support me.  
Understanding the client’s expectations and hopes will open the doors for interaction. How 
can a client be motivated to talk? By knowing his fears and hopes, by showing concern for 
him and by instilling confidence in him and encouraging him to play an active role in the 
process.  
Counsellors are also not immune to expectations. Based on the voice they hear over the 
phone and remarks made on the referral (‘a difficult person who speaks aimlessly’), a 
counsellor may be prejudiced. Professional counsellors should have a positive attitude 
towards a client and be enthusiastic about the case.  
A skilful counsellor will avoid behaviour that can create barriers in the counselling process.  
The qualities of an effective counsellor are as follows:  
 ·Concern for client: In general, counsellors will listen without interrupting or 
imposing their own values and beliefs. They will give the client space to explore their 
thoughts, feelings or behaviour. People often find it helpful just to have their concerns taken 
seriously. 
 ·Versatility: The counsellor may also employ a variety of techniques to help the 
client understand his feelings.  
 ·Positive regard: Many people meet counsellors with an uncertain mind which they 
express as follows: ‘You’ll think this is silly...’, ‘I don’t really know why I’m here…’, ‘I just 
can’t shift…’ or ‘I feel so guilty…’. Mutual respect improves the chances of changing (or 
maintaining) behaviour and achieving outlined goals. 
 ·Assurance of confidentiality: Counselling takes place in privacy and assures 
confidentiality of disclosures.  
 ·Source of help: Counselling is an effective mechanism for discharging hurt and thus 
recovering the ability to think rationally in any situation. A child who is experiencing, or has 
experienced hurt, will typically find someone —often an adult —and try to get that person’s 
82 
 
attention. The child will then talk actively, laugh, sweat, shiver, throw a tantrum, cry or yawn. 
If the adult can continue to hold the child, perhaps give a warm hug, the child’s pain will 
gradually subside. 
 ·Self-awareness and cultural awareness:  The counsellor must be fully aware of his 
values, needs and biases prior to counselling employees. Self -aware supervisors are less 
likely to allow their biases to influence the counselling process. In addition, supervisors need 
to be cognizant of the similarities and differences between individuals of different cultural 
backgrounds and how these factors may influence values, perspectives and actions.  
 ·Empathy: Empathy allows the counsellor to see the situation from the other person’s 
point of view. It provides a grounding effect on the plan of action, ensuring that it is feasible 
and achievable from the perspective of the employer and employee.  
 ·Credibility: Supervisors achieve credibility by being honest and consistent in their 
statements and actions.  
 ·Neutrality: Common errors made by supervisors when conducting counselling 
sessions are:  
o Personal bias  
o Rash judgements  
o Stereotyping  
o Losing emotional control  
o Inflexible counselling methods  
o Improper follow-up  
 ·Flexibility: The counselling style should fit the character of the employee and to the 
relationship desired. The situation should always dictate the style of counselling. Keeping this 
in mind, the counselling styles can change during the counselling session and the counsellor 
should not stick to any one style. 
 ·Supportiveness: Subordinates should be encouraged through actions of the 
counsellors. 
2.4.1 Micro Skills of Counselling 
Micro skills are specialised abilities a counsellor might employ to improve client interaction. 
These abilities allow a counsellor to effectively form a professional relationship and include 
clients in meaningful and beneficial conversation. Five of these fundamental counselling 
techniques will be briefly discussed in this article. We have already discussed these skills as 
generic skills. However, here we will discuss these in detail. Individually or collectively, they 
can assist a client in accessing their most inner thoughts or clarifying their future aspirations. 
Micro skill 1: Attending behaviour 
The behavioural component of developing rapport is attending. A counsellor must let a client 
know they are interested in listening to them and supporting them when they first meet. The 
counsellor can encourage the client to discuss and be candid about their concerns by being 
83 
 
present. In Western culture, making eye contact with another person while speaking or 
listening to them is crucial and polite. To demonstrate real interest in what the client is 
saying, the counsellor must make normal eye contact with them. 
According to Geldard and Geldard (2001), counsellors might incorporate non -verbal 
behaviour into their toolkit to help clients unwind. It may take some practice to master this 
talent, but it all starts with a counsellor seating in the same position as the client. The client is 
likely to follow suit if rapport between the client and counsellor has started to develop. 
Consequently, the client’s anxiety levels will go down. Although talking and listening make 
up the majority of counselling sessions, silence can occasionally have significant 
consequences on the client. We hurry to fill in the blanks when we first start out as 
counsellors because silence can feel a bit awkward, but as our experience builds, we learn to 
be more at ease with the idea of just ‘being’ with the client. 
Micro skill 2: Questioning 
During the counselling session, asking questions can help to uncover fresh discussion 
 topics. They can help the counsellor identify a problem and clarify material that at 
first glance could be unclear. A client’s path of self-discovery may be aided by questions that 
prompt thought or memory. Counsellors should be educated about the many kinds of 
questioning strategies, including when to use them and what to expect from the answers. It’s 
also critical to be mindful of and cautious with excessive questioning. A client may come to 
believe that the counsellor has all the answers if they are asked too many questions, since it 
conveys to them that the counsellor is in -charge. It is crucial to take into account the 
personality of the client, their continuous relationship with the counsellor, and the issue or 
issues at hand while choosing successful questioning tactics. In counselling, there are 
primarily two sorts of questions used: Closed and Open. 
 · Open questions: Open questions are those that cannot be answered in a few 
words, they encourage the client to speak and offer an opportunity for the counsellor to gather 
information about the client and their concerns. Typically open questions begin with: what, 
why, how or could. For example: 
o What has brought you here today? 
o Why do you think that? 
o How did you come to consider this? 
o Could you tell me what brings you here today? 
  ‘How’ queries frequently prompt the client to discuss their emotions. The 
answers to ‘what’ inquiries are more frequently facts. ‘When’ queries reveal information 
about its timing, which may include events and details that occurred before or after the 
problem. ‘Where’ inquiries provide information on the setting, circumstance, or location 
where an important event has occurred, while ’Why’ questions typically tell the counsellor of 
the causes of the occurrence or details that preceded it. It should be highlighted that the 
counsellor must use caution when posing ’why’ queries. Asking customers why can make 
them feel defensive. 
84 
 
 · Closed questions: Closed questions are questions that can be answered with a 
minimal response (often as little as ‘yes’ or ‘no’). They can help the counsellor to focus on 
the client or gain very specific information. Such questions begin with: is, are or do. For 
example: 
o Is that your coat? 
o Are you living alone? 
o Do you enjoy your job? 
  Ivey & Ivey (2003) describe the following five problematic questioning 
techniques: There is a very real risk of employing too many questions or questioning 
strategies that can have a detrimental effect on the session, even though questioning 
techniques can be used positively to explain concerns essential to the counselling session. 
When used inappropriately at the wrong moment by an untrained interviewer or counsellor, a 
client may experience undue discomfort and confusion. 
 · Bombardment/grilling: This occurs when counsellors get caught up into a 
pattern of asking too many questions one after the other. In doing this, the counsellor is 
always deciding which issue should be discussed next. 
 · Multiple questions: This occurs when counsellors ask several questions at 
once. For example: ‘Please tell me about yourself’; ‘how old are you’; ‘where were you 
born’; ‘do you have any children’; and ‘what do you do for a living?’. 
 · Questions as statements: This occurs when counsellors use questions as a 
way to sell their own point of views. For example, ‘Don’t you think it would be helpful if you 
studied more?’, ‘What do you think of trying relaxation exercises instead of what you are 
doing now?’, etc. 
 · Questions and cultural differences: This is where a counsellor needs to be 
aware of any cultural influences that may make asking questions inappropriate for clients 
from a specific culture. For example, the rapid -fire North American questioning style is often 
received less favourably by other cultures. 
 · Why questions: This is where the counsellor asks too many why questions. 
For example ‘Why did you do that?’ 
Micro skill 3: Confrontation 
In most cases, the term ’confrontation’ refers to confronting someone over a difference of 
opinion. However, in counselling, confrontation is an attempt by the therapist to gently attract 
the client’s attention to something that they may have missed or ignored. There are three steps 
to a confrontation.  
 · Identifying mixed or inconsistent messages (expressed through the client’s 
non-verbal or vocal behaviours) is the first step.  
 · The second phase calls for the counsellor to help the client become aware of 
these discrepancies and work through them.  
85 
 
 · The third and final step entails assessing the intervention’s effectiveness in 
light of the client’s development and transformation. 
During the counselling process there are four (4) discrepancies which the client could display. 
The discrepancy can be between: 
· Thoughts and feelings 
· Thoughts and actions 
· Feelings and actions or 
· A combination of thoughts, feelings and actions. 
Having identified a discrepancy, the counsellor highlights this to the client, using a 
confrontation statement such as ‘On the one hand …, but on the other hand…’. This is a 
standard and useful format for the actual confrontation. Of course, you may also use 
variations such as: ‘You say…but you do…,’ or ‘Your words say…but your actions say ….’. 
For example: ‘Your words say you would like to spend more time with your sister, but your 
actions say that it’s not a priority for you.’ 
Micro skill 4: Focusing 
Ivey and Ivey (2003) have identified seven areas a counsellor can focus on in the counselling 
session to bring about broader perspectives and potential solutions. The first is individual 
focus, in which the counsellor starts the counselling session by concentrating only on the 
client’s personal characteristics, background, and motivations for seeking counselling. To 
help bring the client’s attention completely to them, the counsellor will frequently utilise the 
client’s name. For instance: ‘Joan, tell me a little about yourself’ or ‘Are you the family’s 
eldest daughter, Joan?’  
The second is: Main emphasis or issues. The client’s motivation for seeking counselling is 
taken into consideration. The client will frequently discuss acquaintances, coworkers, 
extended family members, and other people who are somehow connected to the reason the 
client is seeking counselling because no problem is genuinely isolated. It is necessary to be 
flexible in the concept of ’family,’ as it can mean different things to different people, 
depending on whether they are talking about nuclear, traditional, single -parent, or close 
friends who are given family titles like ‘Aunt’ or ‘Uncle’. The client’s response to the 
counsellor is the subject of the mutuality since this could be a sign of how the client is 
growing in their relationships with other people. The counsellor asks, ’How can we work 
together?’ in an effort to place the client and counsellor on an equal footing. At this time, 
question like ‘how would you like me to assist you with this situation?’ is appropriate.  
The counsellor will be able to recognise how a client’s upbringing has influenced them, but 
this understanding can be expanded to include additional factors including gender, race, 
ethnicity, religion, and socio-economic status to better understand the client as they are in the 
present. 
Micro skill 5: Reflection  
The deeply held beliefs and meanings that underlie life experiences are referred to as 
reflection on the meaning of life. Counsellors that incorporate reflection into their practice 
86 
 
may discover that their clients delve deeper into the details of their own life experiences. 
Imagine, for instance, that two people go on a vacation to the same island resort at the same 
time of the year. One of them extols the wonders of sunsets, strolls down the beach, and a 
relaxed way of life. While the other laments on their boredom, discomfort from the sun, and 
heat. This example shows how different people’s interpretations of the same event can be 
completely different from one another. Consequently, the ability to reflect on meaning is to 
assist clients to explore their values and goals in life, by understanding the deeper aspects of 
their experiences. 
2.4.2 Macro Skills of Counselling 
By allowing clients to see their difficulties in the context of larger issues, macro counselling 
techniques assist clients gain a deeper grasp of the significance of their issues and their 
capacity for change. To solve the following problems, macro knowledge is required: 
 · Drawbacks of human situations: They are susceptible to anxieties about 
suffering, disease, injury, aging, and death as fragile living beings. They are vulnerable to 
anxiety, despair, aggression, and mood swings since they are emotional beings. The 
difficulties of clients are made more relatable by these common human experiences. 
 · Good things about people: By utilising the innate human desire for 
significance, fulfilment, and growth, we can rekindle our clients’ drive for self -preservation 
and constructive transformation. 
 · Social, economic, political, and cultural influences: We can lessen our 
clients’ propensity for self -blame and at the same time make them aware of the greater 
struggle for social justice and human rights by acknowledging how these macro forces can 
shape and limit individual values and behaviours. 
 · Underlying stories of myths, religions, and tales: They become aware of 
transcendental sources of wisdom and inspiration that can be uplifting and life -changing by 
connecting their issues to an acceptable and chosen overarching story. 
2.4.3 Inside and Outside Skills of Counselling 
Counsellors use their interpersonal and professional abilities to better understand and interact 
with their clients. These abilities enable a counsellor to assist a client in overcoming 
challenges standing in the way of living a happy life. These abilities enable you to develop a 
connection with your clients, win their trust, and make sure they feel heard and understood. 
As the word implies, inside skills could refer to the qualities that should be possessed by a 
counsellor to solve the issues of their clients. These qualities include patience, self -
awareness, empathy, etc. On the other hand, outside skills refer to how a counsellor should 
behave outwardly. These include body language, communication, attending behaviour, etc. 
We have already discussed these as part of generic, macro, and micro skills. 
2.4.4 Self-Monitoring Skills as a Counsellor 
To maintain track of your own behaviours and performance, counsellors should practise self -
monitoring. Self-monitoring is a personality attribute that entails the capacity to keep track of 
and control one’s appearance, feelings, and actions in response to social contexts and 
circumstances. It entails being conscious of your actions and how they affect your 
87 
 
surroundings. Additionally, it describes your capacity to change your behaviour in reaction to 
environmental, situational, or social factors. Self -monitoring practitioners are more inclined 
to alter their conduct to fit or comply with the circumstance. People lacking self -monitoring 
skills have a propensity to act in accordance with their own internal demands and sensations. 
Being conscious of oneself can help a counsellor establish ’ego boundaries’ and distinguish 
between what is theirs and what belongs to their clients. In addition, self -awareness allows 
the counsellor to ’consciously use the self.’ The skill enables the counsellors to gain a deeper 
understanding of themselves, making them less likely to delve on their own personal 
problems and instead focus on treating clients’ problems. Depending on the objective it seeks 
to achieve, Self-monitoring can be broadly classified into two major categories: 
· Acquisitive: Self-monitoring that is acquisitive seeks out other people’s attention and 
acceptance. It entails evaluating how other people are reacting and changing behaviour in a 
way that will help the person fit in or attract attention, prestige, or power. 
· Protective: This kind of Self -monitoring is meant to shield the person from other 
people’s judgement. People will keep an eye on the circumstances and reactions before 
modifying their own behaviour in a way that the group will find acceptable. This is done to 
avoid public humiliation and rejection. 
People might be impacted by Self -monitoring in many different ways. The ability to keep an 
eye on oneself helps extroverted persons engage with others and adjust to various social 
circumstances. People who employ Self-monitoring in this way are frequently liked and have 
good interpersonal skills. Other times, social anxiety may induce people to self-monitor. They 
pay a lot of attention to how other people are acting as well as how they believe others may 
see them since they are awkward in social situations. When dealing with others, people may 
find it challenging to unwind and be themselves due to their hypervigilance, which can also 
frequently make them feel more anxious. 
People with social anxiety are made to feel even more self -conscious about their actions in 
social situations when they engage in excessive self -monitoring. It is significant to remember 
that depending on the circumstance, self -monitoring may be beneficial or harmful. High -self 
monitors excel in making adjustments to the environment and interacting with others. 
Although they could be perceived as ‘fake’. In some situations, it is also a social ability that 
can foster interpersonal peace. People who have little self-monitoring are less inclined to alter 
their conduct to fit in. In some situations, this might be viewed as hostile or even antisocial 
behaviour. It can also indicate strong individualism and a lack of interest in following social 
norms in others. 
For a counsellor, self -monitoring is one of the most useful tools. This skill enables the 
counsellor to examine his or her own beliefs, sentiments, and biases during a therapeutic 
session. Without this procedure, counsellors risk reacting to their subconscious conditioning. 
The ability to relate to your clients more effectively as a counsellor will improve as self -
monitoring grows. A counsellor’s professional and personal development can both benefit 
from this skill. The self -monitoring process provides a t ool for tracking goal achievement 
over the course of the counselling relationship and aids counsellors in collaboratively 
working with clients to set goals and ways of evaluating progress toward those goals. Self -
88 
 
monitoring therefore functions as both a part of the counselling intervention process and a 
way to get evaluative data that may help with accountability goals. 
2.5 Indigenous Model of Counselling 
Indigenous therapy is the process of aiding people to deal with reality in their most recent 
social lives based on local knowledge, beliefs, and way of life, principles and practices. 
Counsellors must take into account ethnic and religious values because culture is ingrained in 
human beings. This is furthered by the perception of the Eastern resistance to Western 
counselling theory, which promotes the growth of indigenous counselling concepts. This 
shows that indigenous counselling develops through the use of counselling that is linked to 
cultural and religious values to produce something unique in the methodologies, 
interventions, strategies, and counselling approaches. In indigenous counselling, there are 
three types of criteria that are used: (1) Adding a few cultural and religious components to the 
application of pre-existing counselling; (2) Modifying the counselling approach with cultural 
and religious components; and (3) Adopting cultural and religious components to create a 
new counselling approach. 
While therapeutic counselling is gradually becoming more popular in urban centres, there is a 
need for indigenous therapy models for successful intervention and result in India. This is 
since Indian subcontinent residents have a distinct culture and worldview than what European 
and American views propose. There are no culture -specific theories that could define human 
behaviour, even though some Indian therapists incorporate yoga and meditation techniques 
into the counselling and psychotherapy processes (Clay, 2002). However, there are some 
exclusive counselling practices with specific settings and stages that could affect the therapy 
process and outcome for Indian clients. It is well -accepted that culture has a substantial 
impact on how well therapy works in counselling and psychotherapy. 
Psychologists are increasingly aware that, contrary to what the broad Euro -American 
psychological theories imply, individuals from other ethno -cultural groups do actually have 
distinctive ways of thinking, acting, and behaving. The background impacts of human factors, 
particularly the tradition, worldview, social, environmental, and geographic conditions of the 
clients, should be taken into consideration by a successful therapist. For a better outcome, 
therapy needs to combine and embrace Indian worldview, cultural background, and 
distinctive traditional healing practices. Comprehensive theories to explain the Indian psyche, 
various developmental patterns, and human behaviour also need to be constructed. It would 
be quite difficult to develop such an indigenous counselling and psychotherapy model while 
combining the current belief system and existing conventional techniques.  
Indigenous counselling offers a secure, nurturing environment that respects indigenous 
wisdom, healing, and experience. In order to understand the different cultural backgrounds of 
various clients, a counsellor needs to approach mental health and life concerns from an 
indigenous perspective.

2.7 Summary 
• The successful outcome of any counselling relationship depends on the working 
alliance between the counsellor and the client. 
• Counsellors may use leads that vary in length and depends on the counsellor’s 
theoretical orientation and current phase of counselling to change the client’s 
perceptions (Robinson, 1950). 
• Relationship immediacy is the counsellor’s ability to discuss with the client the 
quality of this relationship, whether it is strained, boring or productive. 
• Transference is the projection by the client of the past or present feelings, 
attitudes or desires onto the counsellor. 
• Counsellors are also not immune to expectations. Based on the voice they hear 
over the phone and remarks made on the referral (‘a difficult person who speaks 
aimlessly’), a counsellor may be prejudiced. 
90 
 
• In general, counsellors will listen without interrupting or imposing their own 
values and beliefs. They will give the client space to explore their thoughts, 
feelings or behaviour. 
• The counselling style should fit the character of the employee and to the 
relationship desired. The situation should always dictate the style of counselling. 
• In general, counsellors will listen without interrupting or imposing their own 
values and beliefs. They will give the client space to explore their thoughts, 
feelings or behaviour. 
• Counselling is an effective mechanism for discharging hurt and thus recovering 
the ability to think rationally in any situation. 
• Micro skills are specialised abilities a counsellor might employ to improve client 
interaction. These abilities allow a counsellor to effectively form a professional 
relationship and include clients in meaningful and beneficial conversation. 
• By allowing clients to see their difficulties in the context of larger issues, macro 
counselling techniques assist clients gain a deeper grasp of the significance of 
their issues and their capacity for change. 
• By allowing clients to see their difficulties in the context of larger issues, macro 
counselling techniques assist clients gain a deeper grasp of the significance of 
their issues and their capacity for change.

most recent social lives based on local knowledge, beliefs, and way of life,

• While therapeutic counselling is gradually becoming more popular in urban

3.1 Introduction 
We have already discussed the basic counselling skills which are needed by any counsellor in 
their profession. However, there are some specific skills that make the counselling even more 
effective. In this unit, we will discuss the problem -solving techniques such as paraphrasing 
and reflecting feelings, along with the asking questions and self -disclosing. It will also focus 
on other skills like interviewing, listening, and monitoring skills of counselling, along with 
the process of terminating counselling.  
3.2 Objectives 
After going through this unit, you will be able to:  
 · Discuss the problem-solving techniques used in counselling 
 · Explain the interviewing, listening, and monitoring skills of counselling 
93 
 
 · Describe the process of terminating counselling 
3.3 Facilitating Problem Solving 
In case of problem -solving it is important to assess the problems faced by clients. But the 
question is: How do counsellors assess the problems that clients face? What type of 
procedures may be adopted? What kind of techniques can be made use of?  Before learning 
specific tools and techniques for assessing human characteristics, let us first recognize some 
basic guidelines for effective assessment. 
 · Each individual is unique:  Assessment is a means of increasing 
understanding of the uniqueness of an individual in terms of traits, emotions and behaviour.  
 · Differences exist among individuals:  Each person is unique as well as 
distinct from others. This principle notes that individual assessment seeks to identify the 
strengths and positive attributes of a person, as well as weaknesses and shortcomings. 
 · Assessment requires direct participation of individuals: Assessment will be 
complete and accurate only when the individual is willingly and directly involved. This 
involvement includes inputs by the client, feedback and clarification. In addition, it implies 
taking a standardized test or completing a questionnaire.  
 · Accuracy depends on instruments and individuals:  The effective choice 
and utilization of assessment techniques is important to obtain reliable and valid measures. 
The client and the counsellor should take part in the assessment process in an unbiased 
manner.  
 · Assessment is bound by professional codes: It is important for counsellors to 
be aware of the relevant ethical guidelines established by their professional organizations. 
These guidelines are aimed at protecting both the client and the professional practitioner.  
3.3.1 Assessment Tools  
Assessment tools can be categorized into two sets:  
 · Standardized testing tools:  Intelligence tests, aptitude tests, interest 
inventories and personality tests.  
 · Non-standardized testing tools:  Observation, Socratic questionnaires, 
Alderian questionnaire, lifestyle analysis.  
Intelligence testing 
Intelligence testing procedures have matured with successive developments in design and 
procedures. The first intelligence tests were designed by the Frenchman Alfred Binet to be 
administered to individual students. In the early 1900s, this led to several American versions. 
The most popular of these was the Stanford -Binetest (1916) followed by the Wechsler Scales 
developed by David Wechsler, based on the assumption that intelligence was the sum total of 
the individual’s ability to think in a rational manner, to act purposefully, and to deal in an 
effective way with his or her environment. The scales record three scores:  
 · Verbal ability  
94 
 
 · Performance ability  
 · General ability  
The Wechsler Test III (WISC -III, 1991) for ages six to sixteen years, allows for the 
computing of four index scores of: 
· Verbal comprehension  
· Perceptual organization  
· Freedom from distractibility  
· Processing speed 
Another popular test was the Kaufman instrument which contained sixteen subtests of mental 
and processing skills, which yield five major scores in Sequential Processing, Simultaneous 
Processing, Mental Processing Composite, Achievement, and Non -verbal. The Otis -Lennon 
School Ability Test (OLSAT) came in 1918 as the Otis Group Intelligence Scale and later 
achieved great popularity in both industry and education as the Otis -Quick Scoring Mental 
Abilities Test.  
Aptitude tests 
Aptitude may be defined as a trait that indicates an individual’s ability to perform or learn in 
a given area. Aptitude tests assist a person in making educational and career decisions. They 
serve the following purposes:  
 · Identify the potential abilities of a person  
· Help predict the level of academic or vocational success a person might 
anticipate 
 · Assist in forming groups or teams for training or operations 
(a) Special aptitude tests 
Special aptitude tests help measure a person’s potential ability to perform or acquire 
proficiency in a specific area. These are sometimes referred to as single aptitude tests or 
component ability tests because they only secure a measure of one specific aptitude or a 
single special ability.  
(b) Aptitude batteries  
 A series of sub-tests related in varying combinations to a series of occupations may be called 
aptitude batteries. Batteries offer more convenience in measuring and comparing potential 
abilities than single tests. The General Aptitude Test Battery (GATB) used by the United 
States Employment Service, is very popular. 
(c) Scholastic aptitude tests 
Scholastic or academic aptitude tests propose to measure a person’s potential for performing 
in academic situations. Tests that constitute the School and College Ability Test (SCAT) and 
Scholastic Aptitude Test (SAT) batteries have merit for predicting academic performance at 
higher educational levels. 
95 
 
Interest inventories 
Young students often talk about their career interests. Comments like the ones given below 
are often heard in colleges and schools. 
 ‘I’ve always been interested in engineering.’ 
 ‘The thought of teaching really turns me off.’ 
 ‘I know I’d enjoy accounting.’ 
 ‘I can’t make up my mind between engineering and medicine.’ 
 ‘I’m really upset because I can’t think of any job I’m interested in.’ 
Exploration of interests is important in career counselling and guidance. Even a simple listing 
in hierarchical order of possible careers may be as valid in some instances as standardized, 
inventoried interests. 
Two of the earlier and more popular inventories still extensively used today are the Kuder 
Preference Record, V ocational (1976) and the Strong Interest Inventory (1994). 
Personality tests 
Personality tests are often fascinating and we find a great range, from the basic  
do-it-yourself personality test in the daily newspaper to sophisticated, projective techniques 
requiring highly specialized psychological training. 
Several of the more popular personality inventories or standardized personality assessment 
instruments are the Myers-Briggs Type Indicator, the Edwards Personal Preference Schedule, 
and the Minnesota Multi-phasic Personality Inventory (MMPI).  
The Myers -Briggs Type Indicator is a widely used personality test. The standard Form 
contains 126 items that determine preferences on the four scales of (i) 
extroversion/introversion, (ii) sensing/intuition, (iii) thinking/feeling, and  
(iv) judging/perceiving. The various combinations of these preferences result in sixteen 
personality types, helpful in making career and other decisions. 
The Edwards Personal Preference Schedule (EPPS) is a personality inventory based on the 
theory of personality presented by Henry Murray. The Edwards Personal Preference Schedule 
is designed to show the relative importance to the individual of fifteen key needs or motives: 
Achievement Affiliation Nurturance 
Deference  Intraception Change 
Order  Succorance Endurance 
Exhibition Dominance Heterosexuality 
Autonomy  Abasement Aggression 
 
 
96 
 
Observation  
Observation is a major source of learning. Human beings understand people, events and 
objects by observing. Observations may be classified by the level of sophistication and 
training required.  
 · Level One:  Casual observation. No specific purpose; informal and 
unstructured or unplanned. Requires little or no training. 
 · Level Two: Planned observation for a specific purpose; guided by observation 
instruments such as checklists and rating scales. Some training is desired. 
 · Level Three: Clinical level observation is made under controlled conditions 
and conducted over a long period of time. Sophisticated techniques are used. Observers are 
usually trained at the doctoral level.  
The following guidelines will be helpful in making observations effective: 
 · Observe one client at a time. 
 · Decide the criteria for making observations. 
· Choose an appropriate period and make observations over such a period of 
time. 
· Do not isolate behaviours. Observe the client in the context of the total 
situation. 
 · Integrate the observation data with other data to get a total picture.  
A variety of instruments, such as rating scales and checklists, are available to counsellors for 
observation.  
Rating scales  
In the rating scale method, specific characteristics are identified and observations are 
recorded on a scale, as shown in Figure 3.1. The following is an example of a rating scale: 
 
Fig. 3.1  A Sample Rating Scale 
 
97 
 
The merits of rating scales are:  
 · Helps focus on specific characteristics  
 · Increases the objectivity of the rater  
 · Provides for comparability of observations among observers  
 · Easy to use 
The rating scales are not without limitations: 
 · Improper use: Poor and unclear directions for the scale’s use 
 · Content deficiency: Inadequately defined terms, items that tend to prejudice 
how one responds, overlapping items, excessive length, etc.  
 · Scale problems: Limited scales for rating,  
· Rating problems: Ratings made without sufficient observations or real 
understanding leads to overrating or underrating. Biased rating leads to middle 
rating or distorted rating. 
Checklists 
Another instrument that may be used for recording observations is the observer checklist. 
Figure 3.2 is an example of a simple form of a checklist. 
 
Fig. 3.2 Observation Checklist 
98 
 
Lifestyle analysis  
Adlerian therapy makes use of lifestyle analysis during the first three stages of treatment. It is 
the projective use of early memories, whether they are true or fictional. These memories 
embody a person’s core beliefs and feelings about self and the world. They contain 
reflections of the person’s inferiority feelings, goals and style of life.  
Adlerian client questionnaire  
In addition to these early memories, the therapist uses the following to do the assessment:  
· Description of symptoms, the circumstances under which they began, and the 
client’s description of what he would do if he were not plagued with these 
symptoms;  
· Current and past functioning in the domains of love relationships, family, 
friendships, and school and work;  
 · Family of origin, group and dynamics, and extended family patterns; 
 · Health problems, medication, alcohol, and drug use; and  
 · Previous therapy and attitude toward the therapist.  
Socratic questioning  
The Socratic style places the responsibility for conclusions and decisions in the lap of the 
client. The role of the therapist is that of a ‘co-thinker’, not the role of a superior expert.  
Questioning can take place as indicated below:  
· Awareness of problems: Questions to gather relevant information, clarify 
meaning, and verify feelings. 
· Assessment: More penetrating questions uncover the deeper structures of 
private logic, hidden feelings, and unconscious goals.  
· Choices: New options are generated dialectically, examined, and evaluated to 
help the client take steps in a different direction of her own choosing. 
· Implementation: Questions to evaluate the progress of the client in new 
direction on the new philosophy of life.  
Anecdotal reports 
Anecdotal reports, as the label implies, are descriptions of a client’s behaviour in a given 
situation or event. Such reports are subjective and descriptive in nature and are recorded in a 
narrative form. Often a counsellor will collect several of these reports, which then become an 
anecdotal record of a client’s behaviour over a period of time or situations. 
Figure 3.3 is an example of an anecdotal report. 
99 
 
 
Fig. 3.3 Anecdotal Record: Form A 
Situational observations 
Situational observations are utilized to study the behaviour of individuals in structured 
situations. For example, a group might be given a problem situation but no leader is 
designated. The group is then observed to see how a leader might emerge, who it will be, and 
how different members react. Schoolchildren might be observed in a study situation in which 
the instructor observes the individual for an announced period of time. Workers on the job 
might be observed with and without supervision. 
Behavioural charting 
Another form of recording and reviewing observed behaviour is behavioural charting or 
tallying. In this technique a particular behaviour is observed (e.g., disturbing others, leaves 
work station, laughs without apparent reason) over specific time periods to deter mine how 
frequently it occurs.  
3.3.2 Paraphrasing and Reflecting Feelings 
Empathy is the skill of listening to someone and understanding his feelings. It also helps 
clients understand themselves batter and act accordingly. Table 3.5 shows the skills 
associated with empathy. Empathy involves two major skills: Perceiving and 
Communicating. According to Carl Rogers: ‘To be with another in this way (empathetic) 
means that for the time being you lay aside the views and values you hold for yourself in 
order to enter another’s world without prejudice. In some ways it means you lay aside 
yourself.’ To be empathetic is not an easy task. Here is a short check list to evaluate your 
abilities: 
 · Can I enter the private world of the client and see things as he or she does?  
 · Can I give up my temptation to evaluate and judge my client’s views? 
· Can I extend my understanding of the client’s inner feelings as felt by him, 
without distortion? 
· Can I sense the client’s inner feelings which are dimly seen or 
mixed in confusion?

Subsuming  
This is more than empathy. The counsellor actually strives to move along those inner 
pathways of the client’s experience for a short period of time to gain understanding. 
3.3.3 Asking Questions  
The autobiography has been one of the most popular forms of literature throughout the ages. 
The autobiography lets a person express what has been important in his or her life, to 
emphasize likes and dislikes, identify values, describe interests and aspirations, acknowledge 
successes and failures, and recall meaningful personal relationships. Such an experience, 
especially for the mature client, can be thought -provoking, insightful, and a stimulus for 
action. On occasion, the experience can also relieve tension. 
Self-expression essays 
Another useful technique that counsellors may want to employ on occasion is the self -
expression essay. This technique seeks to solicit the client’s response, usually in a short, 
written essay form, to a particular question or concern. The objective of this technique is to 
elicit spontaneous, uncensored responses to a topic or topics relevant to the counselling needs 
of the client. Examples of appropriate topics would include these: 
 · My biggest concern is … 
 · I’ll bet you don’t know that … 
101 
 
 · I value … 
 · My future plans are … 
 · My job is … 
It should be emphasized that such documents can elicit positive responses as well as 
descriptions of possible problems or concerns. 
The self-description 
The self-description is another client -participation tool that enables the counsellor to see the 
client through the client’s eyes. The person is requested to ‘paint a picture of yourself in 
words,’ in one page, if desired. 
Such a portrait may share whatever aspects the client wishes to have the reader know. It is 
usually desirable to do this in the early stages of counselling to give the counsellor an 
additional means of getting to know the client. This differs from the self -expression essay 
because the self -description is a person’s view of himself or herself, as indicated in Figure 
3.4; conversely, the self -expression essay may describe an individual’s attitudes towards 
activities, events, and beliefs.
