Self-awareness exercises 
Self-awareness exercises are designed to help people become more aware of their feelings, 
emotions, and values as a step towards more effectively expressing their emotions, feelings, 
and values.  
102 
 
Diaries and daily schedules 
Probably today’s clients are no more willing than their predecessors to share such recordings, 
but when a client willingly maintains and shares diary entries with the counsellor, they can 
provide valuable insights for understanding the client and his or her concerns. Some clients 
will find it easier to present some aspects of their behaviour and experiences in writing than 
in oral communication and, if so, the counsellor may decide to suggest keeping a diary for a 
period of time.  
Another technique for systematically recording the client’s daily activities is the daily 
schedule. This is a simple listing, usually an hour -by-hour account of a client’s daily 
activities. This technique can help the counsellor and client understand how the latter is 
organizing her or his time. Whereas the diary is usually a summary of the day’s activities, 
often with feelings and interpretations, the daily schedule is a more objective presentation of 
the day’s activities.  
Questionnaires 
A questionnaire is an extremely popular non -standardized instrument. Broadly speaking, it 
obviously provides an easy way to collect a great deal of information that may be useful in 
further understanding the client. Also, the questionnaire is a client -participation technique 
that promises opportunities to advance self -understanding, at least under certain 
circumstances, of those completing it. More specifically, questionnaires may be designed in 
such a way as to collect specific types of information related to specific needs of the 
counselling clientele. Questionnaires may also seek information that validates other data 
already available to the counsellor. Additionally, questionnaires can help identify problems of 
individuals or groups, as well as their opinions, attitudes or values. Questionnaires are a good 
way to collect needs assessment data as a basis for establishing programme objectives and 
evaluating data for program improvement. 
Structured interviews 
Another basic and popular technique for increasing a counsellor’s understanding of the client 
is the structured interview. This approach not only provides opportunities for client 
observation under certain controlled conditions, but, equally importantly, enables the 
counsellor to obtain specific information and to explore in -depth behaviour or responses. 
Interviews that are structured are usually planned to serve a particular purpose. Once the 
purpose has been clearly specified, questions are designed that are suitable to achieve the 
goal or purpose of the interview. These questions are usually arranged in some sort of a 
logical sequence, although the interviewer must be flexible so as to alter both the nature and 
sequence of the questions as circumstances suggest. 
3.3.4 Self-Disclosing 
Counsellors choose to share own experiences either to assure or re-energize the clients. When 
sharing experiences or feelings, the counsellor has to ensure that such disclosures are 
purposeful, direct, brief and tailor-made to the client’s situation. 
Self-disclosure, in its broadest sense, refers to a counsellor’s revealing of private information 
to clients, either inside or outside of a counselling session. Counsellor self-disclosure can take 
many different forms, from providing details about one’s own life and experiences to 
103 
 
expressing one’s own thoughts on specific topics and events. Counsellor self -disclosures can 
be classified into four categories: unavoidable, client-initiated, accidental, and deliberate.  
Accidental self-disclosures are encounters that happen by chance (unplanned), spontaneous 
verbal or nonverbal replies that occur during therapy sessions, or other events that happen to 
reveal the therapist’s private information to their client. Client revelations about a 
counsellor’s private life that are unavoidably made are those that result from the counsellor’s 
visible distinguishing features, such as his or her race, gender, attire, jewellery, etc. 
When clients look for information about their counsellor in print or online media, such as 
biographies, resumes, family background, volunteer work, professional accomplishments, 
etc., client initiated disclosures take place. The counsellor-client relationship is often regarded 
to be blurred by the exposure of personal counselling information to clients through 
unavoidable, client-initiated, or inadvertent means, and should be avoided wherever feasible.  
On the other hand, deliberate counsellor self -disclosure is when a counsellor purposefully 
discloses personal information to a client as a tool in the therapy process. Deliberate 
counsellor self-disclosure has been demonstrated to have some significant advantages, but it 
also involves some hazards. 
Benefits  
A variety of observable advantages to the counselling process can result from counsellor self -
disclosure: 
· Building rapport with clients, which is crucial to the therapy process, can be 
accomplished through self -disclosure. In order to foster the trust that is 
necessary for counselling to be effective, counsellors frequently decide to 
share personal information about themselves and their life. 
· Clients could feel uncomfortable sharing their thoughts, feelings, and 
experiences with a complete stranger. This unsettling feeling can be lessened 
by getting to know their counsellor better through the counsellor’s sharing of 
information on a personal level. 
· Clients occasionally believe they are the only ones going through this. Another 
justification for using counsellor self -disclosure is to show clients empathy, 
give them the impression that they are not alone in their challenges, and make 
them feel as though their feelings and experiences are being acknowledged 
and valued. 
· Because the emphasis is entirely on the clients’ presenting concerns, the 
counselling process can frequently feel one-sided to clients. Some clients have 
reported feeling uncomfortable as a result of this imbalance, and in these 
situations, the counsellor’s thoughtful personal sharing can help. 
· The credentials and position of a counsellor may scare clients. The impact of 
this perceived power imbalance on the development of an effective 
counselling relationship may be lessened by the counsellor’s personal 
revelation, which may help to “humanise” the counsellor in the client’s eyes. 
 
104 
 
Risks 
The hazards of counsellor self-disclosure include the following: 
· A change in the treatment’s emphasis away from the client’s demands and 
treatment goals is one of the most important concerns of a counsellor making 
their own disclosures. The client can interpret a counsellor’s excessive 
openness about his or her own personal issues as a symptom of the 
counsellor’s incapacity to carry out their professional obligations responsibly. 
· The client can view a counsellor’s excessive personal disclosure as self -
serving. It could suggest a lack of concern for the client’s problems, which 
could be detrimental to the counselling relationship. 
· If self-disclosure from the counsellor is given without taking into account the 
client’s current issue, it may be harmful. A client who struggles with concern 
or over -caring, for instance, can be prompted to participate (against the 
counsellor) in the precise caretaking behaviour that the counsellor is trying to 
cure if the counsellor reveals his or her struggles. 
· Counsellor self -disclosure can be harmful if it is given without taking the 
client’s value system into account. A client’s trust in the counsellor as a 
suitable source of aid may be threatened if the counsellor expresses personal 
opinions or experiences that go against the client’s values. 
· Oversharing by a counsellor can make the lines between personal and 
professional relationships more ambiguous. The client can start to see the 
counsellor more as a friend than as a trained aid. 
Check Your Progress 
 1. What are the different types of assessment tools?  
 2. Who was the first to design intelligence tests?  
 3. Mention any two risks associated with self-disclosure for counsellors.  
 
3.4 Other skills: Interviewing Skills 
These are other skills as well which are important in counselling. 
3.4.1 Interviewing Skills 
Interview is the  one of the most dependable and reliable  techniques of counselling. In this 
technique, observation is used as the medium with a definite purpose. The interview 
technique helps the counsellor to collect information and to help the client understand himself 
and his problems and to develop an insight into the entire situation. The interviewer 
emphasizes two points: First, face to face interaction and second, the collection of 
information for a definite purpose. In fact, interview method is an integral part of guidance 
process. It is a subjective method.  
105 
 
The term interview is interpreted in accordance with the area in which it is used. In other 
words, we can say that interview is described as conversation with a specific purpose 
between two persons. 
· According to Pauline Young: Interview may be regarded as a systematic 
method by which a person enters the life of a comparative stranger.  
· According to Goode and Hatt: The interview is in a sense, the foundation 
upon which all other elements rest, for it is the data gathering phase. 
· According to Bhigham and Moor: A serious conversation directed to 
definite purpose other than satisfaction in the conversation itself is an 
interview. 
The following elements regarding the meaning of interview are clearly observable by 
studying and analysing the definitions given above:  
 · In interview, there is face to face contact between counsellor and the client.  
 · It is a way to establish contact with one an unknown person.  
 · It is a purposeful conversation.  
· During the procedure of interview the counsellor or interviewer has the pre -
knowledge of the purpose of interview.  
 · Interview is a subjective matter. 
 · Interview is also considered as a focal point of counselling process                                           
Purpose of interview technique 
Interview technique has the following objectives:  
 · To create rapport through conversation.  
 · To obtain knowledge from a person.  
· To help the person in solving his educational, vocational and psychological 
problems and in ensuring adjustment.  
· To diagnose the various causes of the different problems of the person through 
a discussion.  
· To discover those physical and psychological ailments of the person which 
cannot be identified through psychological tests.  
 · To treat the person through psychoanalytical approach.  
Steps of an Interview 
The main steps or the parts of an interview are as follows: 
· Preparation for an interview: The following preparation is done for the 
interview: 
106 
 
  The main aim is to establish rapport with the interviewee because without such 
rapport, mutually fruitful conversation will become difficult. In this process it is essential to 
address the fear and hesitation of the interviewee so that he may express his thoughts and 
emotions freely and may truthfully answer the questions asked. Davis and Robinson have 
given the following suggestions to establish such rapport.  
o To express sympathy towards the interviewee.  
o At the time of interview, the interviewer should assure the interviewee that the 
solution of all his problems is possible because the interviewee comes to the 
interviewer with lots of hope.  
o During interview, the interviewer must accept the suggestions of the interviewee in 
order to encourage him.  
o Before starting interview, the interviewer must make clear his ideas with the help of 
proper examples.  
o About interviewee’s problems, the interviewee must ask some questions, so that the 
interviewee may make available maximum information to the interviewer.   
o During the interview, it is important that in order to provide freedom of conversation 
to the interviewee the interviewer take no immediate decision or react to the 
interviewee’s statements.  
· Body of the interview: The body of the interview is as important as its 
beginning because, in this part of the interview, any aspect of the problem may be 
touched upon and results of the interview depend on this part of the interview. This 
part can be made effective in following ways: 
o Use of motivating questions: At the time of interview the questions to be asked 
should be of motivating nature. The form of questions should be such that to answer 
them interviewee will need to speak more. Those questions should be avoided which 
can be answered in ‘Yes’ or ‘No’.  The interviewer should take much care in the 
selection of questions.  
o Proper use of silence: Use of silence during interview should be made very tactfully. 
o Limited information in a single attempt: During the interview, an attempt is made 
not to collect all the information in a single attempt. In one interview, only some 
limited information should be collected.  
o Understanding the client’s attitude: During the interview, pupils express various 
types of feelings and attitudes while conversing. These effect or influence both 
negative and positive expressions. The counsellor should try to understand and accept 
these expressions of feelings. Various modes of responding to feelings can be adopted, 
such as, yes, good, very good, ok, etc. 
o Control on interview: In the body of interview, client’s freedom should be limited or 
controlled if the freedom is being taken for granted or misused. In such a situation, the 
counsellor loses his control over interview and he fails to gather the desirable 
information.  
107 
 
· Closing of interview: The following two points should be cared while closing 
the interview.  
o Before closing the interview, the client should be satisfied.  
o Before declaring the interview closed, such an arrangement should be made that the 
next interview may be of shorter duration.  
· Follow-up: In this phase, the evaluation of effects of suggestions made after 
the interview in terms of progress made by the client indicates the success of the 
interview. As a result of this evaluation, if desirable effects are not observed, then 
these failures are discussed in the next interview and necessary changes can be made.  
Importance of Interview Method or Technique 
Now, let’s discuss the importance of interview technique from different viewpoints. 
The importance of interview to the teacher are: 
 · It is important for the teacher to solve the problems of the student.   
· Apart from obtaining information it is also useful in getting information about 
attitude, ambitions and other affective matters.  
· The fact -finding interview can be used to explore information needed for 
which previous data are not clear.  
 · It is very easy to use this method.  
 · Reliable information is received during the interview.  
 · Interview technique method develops insight in the client.  
 · Through interview, past events can also be studied.  
 · By interviewing method, a friendly environment can be created.  
 · Using interviewing, many doubts and misunderstandings can be removed.  
· It can be used directly by the teachers in connection with her everyday 
classroom problems. 
 · This technique is indispensable in diagnosis.  
· This technique is useful in studying the more general habits of work of the 
students. 
· This technique usually reveals conditions that the average teacher a would 
never have surmised 
The importance of interview to school workers other than teachers are: 
 · Counsellor’s interview assists students in the choice of subjects.  
 · It is prominent in social case study and in psychiatric work.  
108 
 
· The personal interview becomes the most important single device available in 
the differential diagnosis of mental disorders.  
The importance of interview in the field of studies are: 
· Interview is usually involved in carrying out investigations of the social and 
economic status of the families to which school children belong.  
· Ingenious interviewing helps us to secure essential differences in the thinking 
and procedures of successful and unsuccessful workers.  
· The personal contact with the respondent enables the field workers to delve 
more deeply into the character, living conditions and general life pattern of the 
client. 
Type of Interviews 
Interviews are used for various purposes. They can be classified as following:  
 · According to function:  diagnostic, clinical and research 
 · According to person’s participation: individual and group 
 · According to roles: Non-directive, directive, focused interview, depth, etc. 
Here are some of them discussed in detail. 
· Diagnostic interview: The purpose of this type of interview is to locate the 
possible causes of a pupil or individual’s problems, getting information about his past 
history, home and family relations, environmental situation and personal problems.  
· Clinical interview: Such an interview follows the diagnostic interview. The 
purpose of this interview is to prepare the stage for further interviews.  It is a means of 
establishing an atmosphere of friendliness and is also a step towards mutual 
understanding. 
· Research interview: The purpose of this type of interview is to find facts 
from individual or pupil about a problem, such as for selecting applications for a 
special course.  
· Group interviews : Group interviews are less common. In this case, you 
would be interviewed at the same time as a few other candidates. A panel of 
interviews will ask you questions by turns. The important thing to remember is not to 
interrupt others and the basic conditions set out by the interviewers. The participants 
must always answer honestly and to the best of their capability, and also listen closely. 
There are two types of group interviews: 
o Panel interview: The panel interview is quite common. Normally, two or three pupils 
will sit facing you and ask questions by turns. Sometimes in a panel interview, each 
interviewer will have a different role. One might be asking more challenging 
questions and appearing to be more involved than the other. The interviewer’s purpose 
for this interview is selection and treatment of the pupil. In case of selection and 
treatment purposes, panel interview are recommended methods.  
109 
 
o Directive interview: In such interviews, test results, abilities and achievement of the 
individual are interpreted in terms of his background and final decision about a career 
choice is made by the counsellor himself. 
o Non-directive: It means nothing is explained by the client. The interviewer is only 
there to help to understand the data.  
o Focused: It aims at finding out the response of individuals to specific events.  
o Depth Interview: It is an intensive and research type interview. It emphasizes certain 
psychological and social factors relating to attitudes, emotions, etc.  
Principles for Interviewer 
Blum and Balinsky have suggested the following basic principles or rules for interviewers:  
 · Establish rapport and maintain decorum during the interview.  
 · Give freedom to the student to speak.  
 · Do not discuss anything with the student unnecessarily.  
· When the individual is expressing his point of view, the interviewer should 
behave like an intelligent listener.  
 · The client should not be criticized by the counsellor. 
· During interview, questions should be asked by the counsellor at the proper 
time.  
 · The student should be enabled to understand emotional needs. 
Advantages of Interview Technique 
The following are the advantages of interview technique: 
· Creation of a friendly atmosphere: The counsellor may create a friendly 
atmosphere for obtaining proper responses. He may start a discussion and develop the 
interest of the client before showing the schedule. A right atmosphere is very 
conducive for getting adequate replies.  
· Removal of misunderstanding: The counsellor is personally present to 
remove any doubt or suspicions regarding the nature of enquiry or meaning of any 
question or term used. The answers are, therefore, not biased because of any 
misunderstanding.  
· Possible to secure confidential interview: The pupil may disclose personal 
and confidential information which he would not ordinarily place in writing on paper. 
The pupil may need the situation of personal contact in order to be drawn out.  
· Advantages of clues: The interview enables the counsellor to follow up leads 
and to take advantage of small clues in dealing with complex questions or topics.  
· Permit exchange of ideas: The interview permits an exchange of ideas and 
information. It permits ‘give’ and ‘take’. 
110 
 
· Useful in the case of some categories of children: The interview enables the 
interviewer to deal with children especially those with limited intelligence or in an 
abnormal state of mind.  
· Reliable information: The information gathered through interviews has been 
found to the reliable.  
· Interview technique very close to the counsellor: It is generally accepted 
that interview technique is as close to the counsellor’s work.  
· Possibility of repetition: Sometimes interviews can be held at suitable 
intervals to trace the development of behaviour and attitudes.  
· Useful for several purposes: Interviews can be used for student counselling, 
occupational adjustment, selection of candidates for educational courses etc.  
 · Cross questioning: Interview technique provides scope for cross questioning.  
 · Command of the counsellor: The technique allows the counsellor to remain 
in command of the situation throughout the interview.  
· Better opportunities to know the student: Through the respondent’s 
incidental comments, facial expression, bodily movements and gestures etc, 
counsellor can acquire information that could not be obtained easily by other means.  
· Useful for judging frankness: Cross questioning by the counsellor can enable 
him to judge the sincerity, frankness and insight of the interviewee.  
Limitations of Interview Technique 
The method of interview, despite its numerous advantages mentioned above, has following 
limitations also:  
· Very costly: It is very costly affair. The cost per case is much higher in this 
method. Generally speaking, a questionnaire costs less per unit than the interview 
costs.  
· Biased information: The presence of the counsellor while encouraging the 
respondent to reply, may also introduce a source of bias in the interview. At times the 
opinion of the client is influenced by the counsellor and his replies may not be based 
on what he thinks to be correct but what he thinks the counsellor wants.  
 · Time consuming: It is a time-consuming technique. 
 · Expertness required: It requires a high level of expertise. Among the 
important qualities to be possessed by counsellor are objectivity, insight and 
sensitivity. These qualities are not possessed by an ordinary counsellor in an adequate 
amount.  
· Subjectivity: The interview is a subjective method of collecting data which 
may be influenced by biases and prejudices of the counsellor to a great extent.  
 
111 
 
Suggestions for a Successful Interview 
Certain suggestions have been given for the success of any interview. However, one must 
remember that ultimately, the success of an interview depends upon the skill of the 
interviewer. Darley has listed the following points that should be taken care of essentially: 
· The interviewer should neither make very lengthy speeches nor should he try 
to suppress the client.  
· The interviewer should explore, first, what are the client’s aspirations that is,  
what he/she wants. Interviewer should suggest remedies after identifying that 
aspiration.  
· The interviewer should get himself introduced with client’s feelings and his 
attitudes in the beginning so that no problem may arise during the interview.  
· The interviewer should use easy language. He should provide only the most 
important information to the client because as a result of excessive conversation with 
the client, he will find himself too involved with the client’s problems and helpless in 
taking any decision.  
Bingham has also given the following suggestions for a successful interview:  
 · Suggestions for preparation for interview: The suggestions are: 
o The main objective of the interview should be decided before starting a successful 
interview. The interviewer should prepare a list of information nuggets or facts to be 
obtained beforehand.  
o There should be no favouritism during the interview i.e., the interview should be 
impartial.  
o The schedule for interview should be fixed by both the interviewer as well as the 
client. The time decided should be convenient to both.  
o The interviewer should collect the information regarding the client. For this purpose, 
the interviewer can prepare the cumulative record card.  
o To obey the principle of confidentially for the interview is must. Only after being 
assured of confidentiality, the client would be able to supply correct and clear 
information.  
· Suggestions for interviewing:  After making the preparation of interview 
successfully and effectively, the following suggestions can be used for effective 
interviewing: 
o The interview should not be rushed. 
o It is essential for the interviewer to have complete control on the interview. During 
interview, the interviewer should go on telling the objective of the interview to the 
client.  
o The interviewer should win the confidence of the client. For this, the interviewer 
should go on exhibiting his interest and confidence in the client.  
112 
 
o The interview should be skilled in making friends.  
o The interviewer should listen the client with attention and patience.  
o The client and the interviewer should cooperate with each other and the interviewer 
should have sympathetic feelings towards the client.  
o During interview, the interviewer should look after each and every comfort of the 
client.  
Qualities required for a successful interview 
For the success of an interview, an interviewer must have the following qualities:  
· Humorous: The interview situation is generally tense for a client. The 
interviewer should be humorous in order to eliminate this tension.  
· Tolerance for attitudes and feelings: A successful and effective interviewer 
is that who establishes a very close contact with the client. This he can do by 
accepting the client’s attitudes and feelings. In this way the client can remove his 
doubts. The interviewer should not give his verdict regarding the client’s attitudes and 
feelings.  
· Gathering limited information: A successful and effective interviewer is that 
who believes in collecting limited and pre -fixed information. If it is not done, then 
unnecessary information is gathered which proves meaningless.  
· Frank speaker: A successful interviewer is that who talks frankly with the 
client. Indirect and cursory conversation cannot be advantageous and can create 
doubts in the minds of the client.  
· No dominance upon conversation: During the interview, the interviewer 
should not go on speaking all the time. The client should be given ample time to 
speak. During such a conversation, if the client is speaking, he should not be 
interrupted by the interviewer. 
3.4.2 Listening Skills in Counselling 
We have already discussed the significance of these skills at length in previous Units. 
To reiterate it again, listening is a fundamental counselling skill. It is the skill of getting the 
data you require from a speaker while getting them to open up and discuss their problems. 
Your goal is to speak as little as possible and refrain from raising too many inquiries. You 
shouldn’t provide your personal viewpoint on the speaker’s situation as the listener 
(especially when counselling). You must stay impartial and limit your suggestions to those 
that are pertinent to the speaker’s problems. Since our natural impulse as humans, especially 
in our culture, is to fill the silence, learning this ability can be rather challenging. 
Dimensions of Listening Skills  
The fine art of listening has a broad scope, usually including the intention, presence, focus, 
curiosity and observations. It is a self -awareness thing, bringing into play a complex mix of 
small decisions to help in listening generously. What to listen for and when to intervene are 
also vital aspects of listening well. 
113 
 
First Dimension: Token Listening 
The First Dimension isn’t actually paying attention, it is token listening. One is not really 
concentrating. One is side -tracked by a conversation taking place elsewhere or are thinking 
about something completely different. One could even be daydreaming or listening to own 
thoughts. All of these situations involve the person is not paying attention to the other person.  
Second Dimension: To Make a Point 
In the second dimension, one is listening to the conversation but for a different reason. One is 
paying attention to when there is a chance to ask a question, make a comment, or express an 
opinion in the conversation. In real, one is simply paying attention to insert or interrupt. 
There is frequently a busy element and a sense of urgency. When the discussion is becoming 
top topic, one may want to be sure in order to get a chance to speak. When both sides are in 
this mindset, the talk quickly becomes heated and nobody can be heard. 
Third Dimension: Semi or Partial Listening 
When one is only partially hearing in the third dimension. It’s possible that one hears what 
someone is saying and believe that he/she understands them, but the emotions and ideas are 
getting in the way. One lacks the internal listening space necessary to pick up on tiny cues, 
such as the true message the other person is attempting to convey, their worries over what has 
just occurred, or what they are requesting of the person. 
Fourth Dimension: Active or Participatory Listening 
Active listening in another dimension that helps the person to cross the threshold into more 
expansive and tightly focused hearing. One pays far more attention and genuinely put 
themselves in the other person’s shoes emotionally. One is paying attention to what they are 
saying and maintaining eye contact. Finally, one is correctly contributing to the debate and 
are not interjecting. The speech volume, tempo, and tone often mimic the other person’s like 
a mirror. One is actively participating in their inner life and are extremely present. 
Fifth Dimension: Full- fledged Listening 
The last dimension is wide and has several layers of listening. It covers every aspect of level 
four listening and much more. It has a curious quality and has a non -judgmental aspect as 
well. Active listening in dimension four helps one to cross the threshold into more expansive 
and tightly focused hearing. One pays far more attention and genuinely put yourself in the 
other person’s shoes emotionally. One is paying attention to what they are saying and 
maintaining eye contact. Finally, one have correctly contributing to the debate and are not 
interjecting. The speech volume, tempo, and tone often mimic the other person’s like a 
mirror. One is actively participating in their inner life and are extremely present. 
This type of listening looks for patterns, themes, and metaphors throughout the whole 
spectrum of the topic being addressed, including broader concerns and systemic components. 
It measures the energy levels as the tales go and has a true feel of the session’s overarching 
storyline. It captures solitude and stillness well. 
In addition to hearing what is being said, listening in the fifth dimension frequently picks up 
on what is not being stated. It entails paying attention in a way that keeps the details in mind 
114 
 
while being receptive. As one may imagine, it feels wonderful to be truly heard and the centre 
of someone’s attention. 
The fifth dimension also entails listening to self, using the intuition and self -awareness, but 
keeping them in the background and reserving them for when the time is just perfect to share 
them—or not. 
3.4.3 Monitoring Skills in Counselling 
We have discussed the monitoring in the context of counsellors. Here, we will discuss it in 
the context of clients. Cognitive behavioural therapists almost always instruct their patients in 
the monitoring strategy. Clients are requested to methodically observe and record particular 
targets, such as their own thoughts, emotions, bodily sensations, and behaviours, as part of 
this type of data collection. For instance, a client who is depressed might be asked to record 
information about situations when their mood felt especially low, a client with post -traumatic 
stress disorder might be asked to complete a diary to record details of their trauma flashbacks, 
and a client in recovery from an addiction issue might be asked to record information about 
what they were doing and thinking just before using substances. Helping people understand 
how their actions and thoughts affect how they feel is one of the goals of CBT. 
Clients may gain a greater understanding of the connections among circumstances, thoughts, 
emotions, bodily sensations, and their responses through the practise of self -monitoring. By 
recording the frequency and intensity of intrusive thoughts throughout therapy, for example, 
self-monitoring can be a quick and easy way to evaluate client development and determine 
whether they are ready for other treatment intervention phases (For e.g., the introduction of 
cognitive restructuring or behavioural experiments). Although the concept of self -monitoring 
is very straightforward, it is frequently difficult for clients to carry out efficiently (Korotitsch 
& Nelson-Gray, 1999). 
Clients are requested to methodically watch and record particular targets, including  thoughts, 
physical sensations, emotions, and actions, as part of a self -monitoring technique. It serves as 
both an assessment tool and an intervention (Korotitsch & Nelson -Gray, 1999; Proudfoot & 
Nicholas, 2010), and it is a component of a larger practise of empirical research and 
measurement that is essential to CBT (Persons, 2008). Self -monitoring consists of two 
components: recording and discrimination (Korotitsch & Nelson -Gray, 1999). The client 
learns how to bring awareness to target phenomena during discrimination by recognising 
them and observing when they happen. Increasing clients’ awareness of their symptoms and 
behaviour is a crucial part of CBT, but for many clients, drawing attention to and raising 
awareness of particular thoughts, sensations, or emotions will be unfamiliar and challenging. 
The act of recording involves writing down events (usually through a written record). 
Discrimination becomes an explicit, deliberate process that may be examined and studied 
when it is documented. The client’s comprehension of their symptoms, as well as the order 
and method by which they occur, will be improved via the inclusion of more description or 
context in the documentation of individual events. Both recording and discrimination are 
abilities that must be developed. In order to effectively complete self -monitoring records, 
clients are likely to need assistance and training. Self -monitoring promotes clients’ 
engagement and motivation by encouraging a sense of self -control and autonomy. It is an 
easy method to introduce clients to the idea of active participation in therapy. 
115 
 
3.4.4 Training Clients in Relaxation 
Low tension in the body and mind is a sign of relaxation. Relaxation techniques are unique 
procedures that people usually apply to unwind. A state of physical stiffness in the body 
known as tension can result in pain as well as emotional responses like anxiety. In order to 
release this tension and gradually relax the muscles and muscle groups, physical relaxation is 
used. When the mind is largely free of tension and distraction, psychological relaxation takes 
place. When a person is mentally calm, they may still have stress in their lives or duties to 
perform, but stress and tension are not at the top of their minds. People who are under stress 
frequently experience physical aches and pains as well as muscle tightness. Psychological 
relaxation can impact physical relaxation. Relaxation comes more naturally to some people 
than others. Anxious people might experience fewer states of relaxation than other people. A 
person’s level of relaxation may be impacted by depression, anxiety, external stressors 
including unemployment, food, and drug usage. 
There are many different relaxation methods, and some people ask therapists for advice on 
how to unwind.  
· Aromatherapy: It uses pure essential oils and organic smells to soothe the mind and 
alleviate stress, is one of the most popular relaxation therapies.  
· Breathing: When someone is feeling anxiety, tension, or panic, relaxation techniques 
that emphasise slow, deep breathing, also known as diaphragmatic breathing, can help them 
relax. Even when practised on its own, diaphragmatic breathing has been demonstrated to 
significantly lower a person’s cortisol (the stress hormone) levels and soothe powerful 
responses to stress in the present. 
· Exercise: Numerous studies have demonstrated the ability of physical activity, such 
as yoga, running, and a number of other exercise methods, to reduce stress and anxiety. Even 
brief daily walks can aid in relaxation. 
· Herbal treatments: Some people take herbal supplements to help them relax and feel 
less anxious. While some specialists contend that the reported calmness brought on by herbal 
medicines may simply be the result of the placebo effect, others discover that taking specific 
supplements actually works. 
· Individual meditation: There are many different styles and methods of meditation, 
but they all entail employing a calm, relaxed environment and concentration to calm the body 
and mind. Numerous research have indicated that mindfulness meditation, in particular, may 
help reduce the symptoms of anxiety. 
· Massage: There are numerous massage types. Massage therapy frequently focuses on 
physically assisting clients in reducing a variety of muscle tension, including tension brought 
on by stress or worry. 
· Medication: One element of mental health treatment for an anxiety problem may 
include anti-anxiety medications. When taken as prescribed, medicine may aid in relaxation 
and tension alleviation for certain people. 
116 
 
· Music: To achieve a sense of serenity, ease tension, or as a means of soothing sadness 
or anxiety, some people listen to music they find calming. It has been demonstrated that 
music can lower stress by affecting the autonomic nervous system. 
· Progressive muscle relaxation: To relieve muscle tension that can develop as a result 
of stress or worry, progressive muscle relaxation is frequently employed. One area of the 
body at a time is tensed and relaxed throughout this workout. 
· Guided imagery: The client is instructed to picture herself/himself in a setting or 
circumstance that brings back fond memories. The client enters a state of relaxation thanks to 
the pleasant imagery. The physiological changes that take place when the client is relaxed are 
the opposite of those brought on by anxiety. 
Body stress is frequently brought on by problems with one’s mental health. However, 
stressors that happen over the course of a person’s life, such as taking a test or evaluation, 
giving a job interview, or taking care of children, can also cause tension. It may be helpful to 
select some relaxation techniques that are effective for you and put them into constant 
practice when tension develops as a result of a mental health issue or stressor.  
In behaviour therapy, relaxation techniques are frequently employed to ease tension, anxiety, 
and stress. In this modest form of ecstasy, the frontal lobe of the brain’s backward cortex 
communicates with the frontal cortex by way of a slight sedative. Research has shown it to be 
effective in a variety of disorders and conditions: 
· Primarily those related to anxiety, fear, and stress e.g., specific phobias;  
· Those in the realm of behavioural medicine and dentistry, such as acute and chronic 
pain (e.g., tension headaches); 
· Hypertension and coping with nausea related to chemotherapy. 
In the relaxation state, muscle tension starts to loosen and the heartbeat and respiratory rate 
start to return to normal.  
When should relaxation skills be used? 
Following a discussion of the stress and relaxation reactions, your client should understand 
how these ideas relate to their circumstance: 
· Do they feel angry, worried, stressed, or afraid?  
· What signs do they exhibit when these feelings occur? 
As a counsellor, you might discover that they struggle to remember to apply their relaxation 
techniques until it’s too late if they lack the capacity to identify triggers and warning 
indications. A review of triggers and warning indicators easily leads to a discussion of the 
situations in which relaxation techniques should be applied.  
· Does your client have anxiety before school in the mornings or perhaps just before 
presenting a presentation at work?  
· Do they experience consistent stress throughout the day?  
117 
 
Not only will this discussion be beneficial your client learn when to use their skills, it will 
help you determine what skills are a good fit. 
3.4.5 Improving Client’s Self-Talk and Self-Perception 
Self-talk refers to your inner voice or the way you speak to yourself. You almost probably 
talk to yourself even if you’re not aware of it. This inner voice creates an internal monologue 
throughout the day by fusing conscious thoughts with ingrained views and biases. Positive, 
negative, and instructional self -talk are the three main categories. ’I am really glad for 
myself,’ ’I am doing well,’ or ’That’s not great, but it might be worse’ are a few examples of 
self-talk. 
In actuality, you naturally chat to yourself during the course of a day. Positive self-talk is also 
becoming more widely recognised as a potent method for boosting self -esteem and 
controlling negative emotions. Individuals with mastery over positive self -talk are thought to 
be more confident, motivated, and equally productive. A person’s perception of who they are, 
or of any of the mental or physical characteristics that make them up, is referred to as self -
perception. Disorders like anxiety disorder, depression, post-traumatic stress disorder, etc. can 
be overcome with the aid of behaviour therapy. The core tenet of behaviour therapy is that 
you can modify your feelings by changing the way you think. 
Steps to improve self-talk and self-perception 
The steps are: 
· Fully acknowledging that we are largely responsible for creating our own 
emotional problems;  
· Accepting the notion that we have the ability to change these disturbances 
significantly;  
· Then recognizing that our emotional problems largely stem from irrational 
beliefs;  
 · Clearly perceiving these beliefs;  
 · Also seeing the value of disputing such self-defeating beliefs;  
· Moreover, accepting the fact that if we expect to change we had better work 
hard in emotive and behavioural ways to counteract our beliefs and the 
dysfunctional feelings and actions that follow;  
· Understanding what the rational alternative to these irrational beliefs are; and  
· In addition practicing REBT methods of uprooting or changing disturbed 
consequences and practicing their healthy alternatives for the rest of our life (Ellis, 1999, 
2001b, 2002). 
The mental story you tell yourself defines self -talk. It’s your inner voice, and you might or 
might not have given it any thought or attention. Truth be told, we often underestimate the 
power of our internal dialogue on how we perceive ourselves and the world. Self -talk is 
typically understood to be a combination of our conscious and unconscious prejudices and 
ideas about who we are and the world at large. The notion that we have both conscious and 
118 
 
unconscious levels of cognition, with unconscious cognitive processes influencing our 
behaviour in ways we are unaware of, was initially put forth by Sigmund Freud (Cherry, 
2019). Self -talk can be positive or negative and paying attention to which you most often 
sway towards, can help you start making proactive changes about how you take on life’s 
challenges. 
Negative Self-Talk 
We focus on preconceived notions that we’re ‘not good enough’ or ‘always a failure’ or ‘can’t 
do anything correctly’ in our self -talk patterns, which are all too frequently negative. We 
remember the times we didn’t quite get it right more often than the times we do because our 
brains are programmed to retain negative experiences over happy ones.  
Positive Self-Talk 
As you might have suspected, positive self -talk is the opposite of negative self -talk. It’s not 
about being narcissistic or tricking our minds into making false assumptions. It’s more 
important to have compassion for oneself and to comprehend who you are and what you’ve 
been through (Jantz, 2019). Positive self -talk sees our internal narrative switching to ideas 
like ‘I can do better next time’ or ‘I choose to learn from my mistakes, and not be held back 
by them’. 
As the research suggests, positive self -talk is important for a number of reasons. From 
helping to overcome body dysmorphia to sports performance, mediating anxiety and 
depression, to more effective learning, positive self -talk can make a world of difference. 
Three additional benefits include: 
· Helps to reduce stress:  According to research, those who are more predisposed to 
think optimistically also tend to engage in positive self -talk and more active coping 
mechanisms when confronted with stressful conditions and problems (Iwanaga, Yokoyama, 
and Seiwa, 2004).With the knowledge that you will handle issues with the best of your ability 
and that, regardless of the outcome, you did the best you could, positive self -talk helps you 
reframe the way you view stressful situations. Approaching these issues with an optimistic ’I 
can accomplish this’ attitude rather than a pessimistic ’This is too hard’ attitude offers up new 
perspectives and approaches to problem-solving. 
· Helps to boost confidence and resilience: Your self-confidence may be increased by 
approaching life with positive self -talk. People with high optimism and self -esteem are more 
likely to achieve their goals, perform well in school, and recover from surgery (Lyubormisky, 
2008). The confidence that the things you want are achievable and the ability to discover 
solutions to issues when they do emerge are two things that regular positive self -talk can help 
you to establish. 
· Helps build better relationships:  You undoubtedly know how it feels to be around 
someone who is upbeat, confident, and satisfied with who they are as a person. They radiate 
confidence, which has a favourable impact on others around them. According to Assad, 
Donnellan, and Conger (2012), couples that expressed more optimism reported better levels 
of cooperation and favourable results. Positive self -talkers are also exceptionally good at 
noticing the good qualities in individuals around them. 
119 
 
To start using positive self -talk, you must first determine how frequently and what kind of 
negative thinking and self -talk you engage in. Once you are aware of this, you can begin to 
train your thinking. Negative self-talk tends to fall into one of four categories: 
· Personalizing: Meaning you blame yourself when things go wrong. 
· Polarizing: Meaning you see things only as good or bad, no gray areas or room for 
middle ground. 
· Magnifying: Meaning you only focus on the bad or negative in every scenario and 
dismiss anything good or positive. 
· Catastrophizing: Meaning you always expect the worst. 
You may identify with a few or all of these categories. The idea is that once you begin 
classifying your thoughts in this way, you can start working on replacing negative frames 
with more constructive ones. You must put in the practise to fully focus on your inner 
dialogue and pinpoint the areas where adjustments are required because this won’t happen 
overnight. 
 
Check Your Progress 
 4. Define interview according to Goode and Hatt.  
 5. Mention any two qualities needed for a successful interview.  
 6. List any two categories of negative self-talk.  
 
3.5 Terminating Helping 
We have already discuss the termination of counselling process in detail as part of stages of 
counselling. Here, we will discuss it again in terms of the effects it has on the client.  
All that is good must come to an end. The counselling process is one that is deep and requires 
personal investment. If it has gone well, then there will be significant personal growth and the 
next step will come easily. If there is something occurring that is impeding personal growth, 
then the next step will be difficult. But regardless of the good or the bad, the end is inevitable. 
In the end, the counsellor has made client enable to think, feel and behave in the way he/she 
desires and to be empowered without the counsellor’s support. With termination, there may 
be some safety features put into place. Many counsellors feel the need to check in with their 
clients after some time or have their clients check in with them. This is commonly referred to 
as a follow up and involves communicating with the client to ensure stability and well -being. 
It’s no different than a doctor’s office calling in and checking up on you. 
Termination is considered not only at the end of a successful relationship, but also when it 
seems counselling is not being helpful. In successful cases, the relationship between 
counsellor and client continues. However, it should not result in the client’s dependence on 
the counsellor for every simple problem that arises during the course of implementation of a 
plan. Therefore, termination is important as a means of empowering the client by creating a 
temporary closure of relationships.  
120 
 
Plan to end  
Preparation for termination should begin long before the actual date of closing the discussion. 
A sense of independence and desire for individual action is to be created during the last stage 
of counselling. Before closing the session, the key points should be summarized and 
validated by getting acceptance from the client. The client should be invited to review the 
plan of action and say in his or her words, what he or she is going to do and what is expected 
of the counsellor.  
In fact, managed care requires the counsellor to play a key role in assisting the client in 
reviewing progress and chalking out necessary steps to ensure right developments. The 
counsellor may offer help by announcing an open door policy or by indicating interventions 
in the plan. The role of counsellor is critical in providing the employee with resources and 
time and periodically assessing the plan. Before terminating, in consultation with the client, 
the counsellor has to identify a date (or dates) in advance, for reviewing the progress and 
evolving corrective measures.  
At the end of counselling, the client may have varied emotions. Some may feel anxious about 
the new behaviour, some may fear criticism, some may feel sad and some others may have 
anger. Each of the emotion has a root cause behind it and the counsellor should understand it 
to remain ‘in-charge’ of the situation.  
3.5.1 Resistance to Termination 
Patients normally feel anger towards the counsellor, when nearing the end of their 
counselling relationship, perceiving it as abandonment. It is more likely to occur if a 
counsellor does not prepare them beforehand. Patients might feel anxious about having to 
handle things without the counsellor’s support. This thought can be very scary as they have 
become dependent on their relationship with the counsellor for support. Other reactions from 
the patients can be sadness on losing a relationship. The emotions that are most often easier 
and comfortable to express are anger or separation anxiety. Unsettled issues surrounding the 
past relationships can play out while terminating the counsellor -client relationship. However, 
if the process is handled ethically and honestly, the counsellor is in a great position to endow 
the patient with an end which is healthy, resulting in a productive relationship that they look 
back at positively and feel content with the end. 
Counsellors, themselves, can also have some bad experiences when forced -termination 
occurs and they feel emotionally overwhelmed. Guilt is also a fairly common emotion for 
counsellors on initiating the termination. If and when the goals which were set at the 
beginning are met, termination ensues. Also when the counsellor feels that the problem has 
been resolved, he may raise the subject of termination directly, if the patient feels they have 
fully recovered they might bring up the idea. In the latter case, if the counsellor agrees with 
the patient, termination can take place. However, the counsellor must also be attentive to the 
likelihood that effort to termination may have aroused due to lack of progress or it could a 
sign of conflict and resistance and should be handled carefully. If ending of the relationship is 
reasonable, the counsellor should use the last few meetings to make the patients comfortable. 
Post-termination, the patients often feel that their symptoms —grieving, sadness, anxiety, or 
separation—have renewed. When a counsellor successfully manages these symptoms, the 
counselee gets the most benefit of the counselling.  
121 
 
 
 
Forced-termination, by its very nature, means that the counselling relationship is ending too 
suddenly and prematurely. After allocating so much time supporting your patient and gaining 
their trust, making them feel open and honest, terminating the relationship abruptly can be 
harmful. This can leave counsellors feeling as though they are abandoning their patient, and 
feeling responsible for whatever might happen to the patient as a result of the end of the 
relationship. For many counsellors, a sense of annoyance feeling exists of not having ‘done 
the job’ or achieved what they set out to achieve for the patient. Counsellors who are under 
training often have a problem with a sense of omnipotence; they feel that they are the ones 
who can really help the patient. On the other hand, it is typical to feel impotent, or to get the 
feeling that the association was not at all obliging to the patient and that the patient will be  
better off with a different counsellor. When working with a patient for merely a short period, 
it can be tricky to see if any progress was made, especially if the patient is not letting the 
counsellor know about any improvements. In such cases, it is of paramount importance for 
counsellors to work to come at ease with that feeling of ‘not knowing’. It could be some time 
before a patient themselves realizes the value of counsellor’s help. Counsellors may feel a 
sense of failure and sadness for not being able to see their patients any longer, and other may 
feel a sense of respite. This relief often leads to culpability about being glad to move on from 
the therapy situation. It is very important to acknowledge your own feelings as you proceed 
through the end of counselling. 
 
Check Your Progress 
 7. What kind of safety features can be used with termination?  
 8. What is the effect of forced termination on counsellors? 
 
3.6 Answers to Check Your Progress Questions 
1. Assessment tools can be categorized into two sets: 
a. Standardized testing tools:  Intelligence tests, aptitude tests, interest inventories and 
personality tests. 
b. Non-standardized testing tools:  Observation, Socratic questionnaires, Alderian 
questionnaire, lifestyle analysis. 
2. The first intelligence tests were designed by the Frenchman Alfred Binet to be 
administered to individual students. 
3. The risks associated with self-disclosure for counsellors are:  
a. Counsellor self -disclosure can be harmful if it is given without taking the client’s 
value system into account. A client’s trust in the counsellor as a suitable source of aid 
may be threatened if the counsellor expresses personal opinions or experiences that go 
against the client’s values. 
122 
 
b. Oversharing by a counsellor can make the lines between personal and professional 
relationships more ambiguous. The client can start to see the counsellor more as a 
friend than as a trained aid. 
4. According to Goode and Hatt, an interview is in a sense, the foundation upon which 
all other elements rest, for it is the data gathering phase. 
5. For a successful interview, an interviewer must have the following qualities: 
a. Gathering limited information: A successful and effective interviewer is that who 
believes in collecting limited and pre -fixed information. If it is not done, then 
unnecessary information is gathered which proves meaningless.  
b. Frank speaker:  A successful interviewer is that who talks frankly with the client. 
Indirect and cursory conversation cannot be advantageous and can create doubts in the 
minds of the client. 
6. Negative self-talk tends to fall into following categories: 
a. Personalizing: Meaning you blame yourself when things go wrong. 
b. Polarizing: Meaning you see things only as good or bad, no gray areas or room for 
middle ground. 
7. With termination, there may be some safety features put into place. Many counsellors 
feel the need to check in with their clients after some time or have their clients check in with 
them. This is commonly referred to as a  follow up  and involves communicating with the 
client to ensure stability and well-being. 
8. Forced-termination, by its very nature, means that the counselling relationship is 
ending too suddenly and prematurely. After allocating so much time supporting your patient 
and gaining their trust, making them feel open and honest, terminating the relationship 
abruptly can be harmful. This can leave counsellors feeling as though they are abandoning 
their patient, and feeling responsible for whatever might happen to the patient as a result of 
the end of the relationship. 
3.7 Summary 
• Assessment is a means of increasing understanding of the uniqueness of an individual 
in terms of traits, emotions and behaviour. 
• Intelligence testing procedures have matured with successive developments in design 
and procedures. 
• Aptitude may be defined as a trait that indicates an individual’s ability to perform or 
learn in a given area. Aptitude tests assist a person in making educational and career 
decisions. 
• Personality tests are often fascinating and we find a great range, from the basic do -it-
yourself personality test in the daily newspaper to sophisticated, projective techniques 
requiring highly specialized psychological training. 
123 
 
• Observation is a major source of learning. Human beings understand people, events 
and objects by observing. Observations may be classified by the level of 
sophistication and training required. 
• Adlerian therapy makes use of lifestyle analysis during the first three stages of 
treatment. It is the projective use of early memories, whether they are true or fictional. 
• Anecdotal reports, as the label implies, are descriptions of a client’s behaviour in a 
given situation or event. Such reports are subjective and descriptive in nature and are 
recorded in a narrative form. 
• Interview is the one of the most dependable and reliable techniques of counselling. In 
this technique, observation is used as the medium with a definite purpose. 
• At the time of interview the questions to be asked should be of motivating nature. The 
form of questions should be such that to answer them interviewee will need to speak 
more. 
• Group interviews are less common. In this case, you would be interviewed at the same 
time as a few other candidates. 
• The counsellor is personally present to remove any doubt or suspicions regarding the 
nature of enquiry or meaning of any question or term used. 
• Clients may gain a greater understanding of the connections among circumstances, 
thoughts, emotions, bodily sensations, and their responses through the practise of self -
monitoring. 
• The act of recording involves writing down events (usually through a written record). 
Discrimination becomes an explicit, deliberate process that may be examined and 
studied when it is documented. 
• Psychological relaxation can impact physical relaxation. Relaxation comes more 
naturally to some people than others. Anxious people might experience fewer states of 
relaxation than other people. 
• Self-talk refers to your inner voice or the way you speak to yourself. You almost 
probably talk to yourself even if you’re not aware of it. This inner voice creates an 
internal monologue throughout the day by fusing conscious thoughts with ingrained 
views and biases. 
• As the research suggests, positive self-talk is important for a number of reasons. From 
helping to overcome body dysmorphia to sports performance, mediating anxiety and 
depression, to more effective learning, positive self -talk can make a world of 
difference. 
• Termination is considered not only at the end of a successful relationship, but also 
when it seems counselling is not being helpful. In successful cases, the relationship 
between counsellor and client continues. 
124 
 
• At the end of counselling, the client may have varied emotions. Some may feel 
anxious about the new behaviour, some may fear criticism, some may feel sad and 
some others may have anger. 
• Patients normally feel anger towards the counsellor, when nearing the end of their 
counselling relationship, perceiving it as abandonment. It is more likely to occur if a 
counsellor does not prepare them beforehand. 
• After allocating so much time supporting your patient and gaining their trust, making 
them feel open and honest, terminating the relationship abruptly can be harmful.  
3.8 Key Words 
CBT: Cognitive behavioural therapy  (CBT) is a talking therapy that can help you manage 
your problems by changing the way you think and behave. It’s most commonly used to treat 
anxiety and depression, but can be useful for other mental and physical health problems. 
Body Dysmorphia: Body dysmorphic disorder (BDD), or body dysmorphia, is  a mental 
health condition where a person spends a lot of time worrying about flaws in their 
appearance. These flaws are often unnoticeable to others.  
3.9 Self-Assessment Questions and Exercises 
Short-Answer Questions 
1. In what ways can asking questions and self -disclosing be employed by 
counsellors?  
 2. Write a short note on monitoring skills in counselling. 
 3. What is the significance of self-talk? 
Long-Answer Questions 
 1. Explain the different assessment tools used for problem-solving. 
 2. Describe the significance and process of interviewing in counselling. 
 3. Discuss the significance of training clients in relaxation.
