4.0 Objectives 
After going through this unit, you will be able to:  
 · Discuss the nature and importance of counselling skills and working alliance  
 · Explain the various models of counselling 
 · Describe the modern trends in counselling 
 
4.1 Introduction 
There are hundreds of clinical counselling methods to select from, and professional 
counsellors use a range of therapeutic approaches in their work. Over 300 different models  of 
counselling practice are listed in the most recent edition of The SAGE Encyclopaedia of 
Theory in Counselling and Psychotherapy. It is initially important to comprehend that there is 
no one counselling strategy that is superior to the others. This is due to the fact that 
counselling techniques are founded more on ideas of human behaviour and change than on 
concrete data. Because there are so many factors to take into account during the counselling 
process, it can be challenging to say whether one counselling style is more effective than 
another. Due to variations in client histories and situations, counsellor communication styles, 
and even client and counsellor moods on the day of the comparison, there may be significant 
variations in the therapy outcome. It is difficult to experimentally account for such 
disparities, making it nearly impossible to establish the superiority of any particular 
counselling strategy. Without such evidence, it falls to counsellors to make every effort to 
ensure that the treatment model(s) they use are the most effective for meeting the needs of 
each client. Acquainting oneself with the models that have proven to be most helpful in actual 
practice is the first step in fulfilling that responsibility. In this unit, we will discuss the nature 
and importance of counselling skills and working alliance, along with some of the models of 
counselling. It will also focus on the modern trends in counselling. 
4.2 Nature and Importance of Counselling Skills and Working Alliance 
To be successful, a counsellor has to be skilful in his approach to counselling. We will now 
discuss the important skills essential for any counsellor. We have already discussed some of 
these skills. Here we will again discuss their importance for a counsellor.  
4.2.1 Social Skills 
The relationship between the client and counsellor is certainly professional but it requires a 
human touch to be fruitful. A person -to-person relationship can be a basic foundation for an 
effective counselling process. As such, counsellors need social skills to establish working 
relationships. The three most important requirements for counsellors are —credibility, 
confidentiality and attention.  
Credibility 
Credibility is an important consideration in choosing a counsellor. It implies two things:  
 · Enhancing expertise by study and practice 
127 
 
 · Creating trust by maintaining integrity in relationships  
A counsellor will be successful only when he can apply knowledge of behavioural sciences to 
the art of counselling and guidance. 
Confidentiality 
Counselling takes place in privacy with an assurance of confidentiality. Confidential issues 
are fully discussed before embarking on the first session. Here, the parts of the discussion 
which cannot be revealed will be made clear to the counsellor. Counsellors are obliged not to 
reveal them to other professionals, friends or family members. 
In the beginning, many people are nervous and evasive. Some may be wrought with 
inferiority complex. Social skills are required to make interactions smooth and pleasant. The 
idea is to make clients ready to face and work with their issues. At the end of the counselling 
process, they should feel liberated, self -empowered, and stress free. As a part of social skills, 
encouragers are to be used. 
Attention  
Attention can be indicated by verbal messages and non -verbal signs. ‘Hello! Please come in’, 
‘How-do-you-do’-like greetings supported by statements like, ‘Well! We will now examine 
your problems’, or ‘I am interested in knowing your mind or thoughts going on in your mind 
right now’, can help create warmth in the ensuing relationship. 
4.2.2 Learning Skills 
Counselling is a process of learning about the client’s situation and extending help. Each 
counselling instance is a case study to be added to the knowledge base and a source of new 
experience in learning. 
Sensory skills 
This refers to the sensitivity with which one has to grasp information. Endowed with five 
senses, we receive a lot of information from the environment and the people with whom we 
interact. Counsellors have to sharpen their five senses in order to receive information from 
different perspectives. Some people have extra -sensory perceptions and some counsellors 
develop them. It means seeing beyond the obvious. It provides an unusual insight of others’ 
views and enables interaction with them.  
Perceptual skills 
Perception is the way we see things. It is the process of understanding messages without 
gathering further information. Perceptual blocks reduce the ability to understand information 
correctly. It results in misinterpretation of the client and his issues. Forming quick 
impressions, stereotyping (women are weak, so Suja is also weak), taking a neutral stance in 
evaluation (central tendency –not caring extreme points), attributing one quality to all other 
observations (he is good in mathematics, so he must be good in all other subjects), etc., are 
perceptual problems. It is important to check perception by cognition, to establish the right 
images or impressions. For instance, a client may appear to be rough and tough at first sight, 
but during interaction the counsellor may discover that he is soft and timid by nature.  
128 
 
Cognitive skills 
Cognition is a process of learning. It is a process of asking why, what, where, who, when and 
how to know the different dimensions of a subject chosen for understanding. It is a process of 
reasoning to establish logical understanding of the phenomenon. The analytical thinking 
process goes like: I am unhappy because my boss has shouted at me. Why am I unhappy? Is 
it because I could not finish the work, or because my boss shouted at me, or used insulting 
words?  
Reflexivity 
It is to do with the ability to adapt to the mode of explanation of the client. As the client 
changes his or her ways of construing events, so too must the counsellor. The counsellor has 
to be involved in a reflexive process, seeking to make his or her own interpretation explicit 
by interaction with the client.  
Suspension  
A counsellor has to develop the skill of suspending his own judgement and construing events 
so as to subsume the client’s reality. 
Use of suspension is also found in reconstruction of what the client has said. A specific 
experience or event that does not fit in the structure of understanding may be suspended for a 
while. It is kept in memory to be used if found relevant in the reconstruction process at a later 
stage.  
Dilation and constriction  
These terms refer to re -organization of construing systems when dealing with 
incompatibilities. The client can dilate the perceptual field in the following ways:  
 · Behave aggressively and widen his areas of interest 
 · Jump from topic to topic and see possibilities everywhere 
 · Start seeing things as being related to his problem  
Constriction 
This is a process of limiting issues. It helps in dealing with chaos and anxiety by avoiding 
incompatibilities. It helps shrink the world to a manageable size.  
Observation  
In the interaction process, words and actions are related to the articulated and observed frame 
of reference in a flexible manner. Observation of the client’s body language is important to 
make meaningful interpretation of messages. A counsellor needs meta -communication skills 
to ‘see through’ what the client says:  
Client: (loud, quick and harsh tone): My boss should not have spoken like that in front of my 
team members.  
Counsellor: Your voice suggests that you are very irritated.  
Client: Yes. You got me right. When I think of the incident, I experience a surge of anger.  
129 
 
Counsellors make notes of the discussion. This should not, however, interfere with the 
observation. If need be, a pause may be taken while making notes. Skill is necessary to 
balance observation and note taking.  
4.2.3 Communication Skills 
Communication is at the core of counselling. A counsellor should be effective in oral and 
non-verbal communication. He or she should be good in speaking and listening.  
Attention  
This is the most important skill required of a counsellor. If the counsellor does not pay 
attention to the client from the moment he enters the room, he feels unworthy of attention and 
develops anxiety. The counsellor’s verbal and non -verbal messages together send powerful 
message of welcome to the clients. It should be like this when interpreted: ‘Welcome. I am 
interested in you and in what you would say. I am willing to listen to your problems, 
understand your feelings and share your concerns.’  
Non-verbal signals 
The supporting non-verbal signals can be:  
 · Posture: Sit upright and relax. Do not slouch in the chair.  
 · Eye contact: Maintain direct eye contact, occasionally looking away from the 
client. The counsellor should not stare at the client; his looks should be comforting and invite 
the client to talk.  
 · Facial expression: The counsellor should smile with affection and show 
concern by remaining calm and observant, and not laugh.  
 · Seating: A distance of 3, 5 feet is appropriate. The chairs should be of equal 
height. They should be at 90 degrees to give freedom to the client to look at the walls or the 
counsellor.  
Encouragers 
A client may find it hard to continue talking and become silent. The role of the counsellor is 
to interpret the silence and if necessary break it. Some clients expect encouraging words of 
appreciation or inquiry to go on. So there is a need to use ‘continuers’.  
Continuers or encouragers can be verbal or non -verbal. They mean precisely that the 
counsellor is interested. Non -verbal expressions include smiles and grimaces, nodding the 
head, leaning forward, making sounds like ‘umm, ahh’, etc. The support can be expressed as 
follows: 
 · Assertive: ‘I am listening’, ‘I am with you’, ‘I follow you’, with a smile, 
nodding of the head, looking at the client and jotting down what the client is saying.  
 · Permission: ‘I want to listen, please go ahead’, with a curious expression on 
the face, eyes searching, head nodding and leaning forward.  
 · Request: ‘I want to understand more about your experience’, with an open 
posture, sitting upright and talking in a gentle voice.  
130 
 
· Direction: ‘Can you tell me more about the incident that angered you last 
week..’? Or ‘I’m curious about ......’ 
Listening skills 
Listening in the context of counselling is called credulous listening. It starts with the belief 
that whatever the client is construing is true. However, that does not mean accepting the 
client’s views without further questioning. 
Listening is a complex activity because the human brain is a limited processor of information. 
The cognitive view of learning sees listening as a process with ‘pre’ activities, ‘while’ 
activities, and ‘post’ activities. 
A pre-listening task gives listeners the opportunity to use what they already know —their 
prior knowledge—to help them do the task. Prior knowledge is organized in schemata (the 
plural form of schema), also called internal frame of reference. It consists of abstract, 
generalized mental representations of our experience that are available to help us understand 
new experiences. 
We process the information we hear in both top-down and bottom-up ways.  
· Top-down means using prior knowledge and experiences; we know certain things 
about certain topics and situations and use that information for comprehension.  
· Bottom-up processing means using the information we have about sounds, word 
meanings and discourse markers like first, then and after that to assemble our 
understanding of what we read or hear, one step at a time.  
Counsellors need both bottom-up and top-down processing skills in listening. They must hear 
some sounds (bottom-up processing), hold them in their working memory long enough (a few 
seconds) to connect them to each other and then interpret what they have just heard before 
something new comes along. At the same time, listeners use their background knowledge 
(top-down processing) to determine meanings with respect to prior knowledge and schemata. 
Listening can be done in two ways: listening for main ideas and listening for details. ‘What’s 
the most important idea in this conversation? What is the main thing they are talking about?’ 
Speakers do not always say what they want to say. Sometimes they do not have clarity in 
their thoughts.  
Listening is purposeful. Post-listening questions like what? how? how much? can be useful to 
check whether purposeful understanding is achieved.  
Feedback in the form of questions, paraphrasing, re -stating etc., is necessary to make 
listening effective. The reasons are:  
· Listening is a real time activity. There is no second chance unless, of course, 
the listener specifically asks for repetition.  
· Listening involves understanding all types of reductions of sounds and 
blending of words. The listener needs clarifications.  
· When there is a gap in understanding, listeners tend to ‘fill in the blanks’ with 
contextual guesses using their prior knowledge.  
131 
 
People do not always say exactly what they mean. That is, important aspects of meaning are 
sometimes implied rather than stated. Listeners have to ‘listen between the lines’ to figure out 
what really is meant. 
Some listening problems arise due to individual learning approaches. These are:  
· Problem of selective reception: Receiving requires not only an attentive ear but also 
an open mind. People tend to select the information that they think is useful and listen to that 
extent. In choosing what to receive, counsellors have to be careful. Key ideas are to be 
captured and listening should be done in a way to enhance their understanding.  
· Filtering: Understanding is influenced by filtering. People tend to wear coloured 
glasses of their choice and see the world through them. Such interpretations may be based on 
their own experience, and biases may be misleading. Care is necessary before establishing 
understanding.  
Responding to the client’s questions 
In the process of interaction, clients may raise some questions and expect counsellors to 
respond appropriately. The questions may be about the client’s own personality, behaviour or 
thoughts and the counsellor–client relationship. Some typical questions are: 
Client: Do you think I am a timid person?  
Client: What do you think about my joining the new organization? Did I make a mistake?  
Client: Am I troublesome, demanding more from you?  
The counsellor has to think carefully before answering such questions. The nature of response 
depends on the following needs:  
 · Need for assurance or sympathy: In the early stages of counselling, the client 
views the counsellor as an expert and seeks guidance at each and every step; that is, his or her 
dependence is high. What the client needs perhaps is an encourager and not a specific answer 
to the question. In such cases, instead of answering a question, encouragement should be 
provided.  
 · Need for advice: Some clients look for an informative answer. As a part of 
counselling, expert advice of the counsellor is sought. In such cases, answers that extend 
professional help should be provided. 
 
Speaking skills  
Good communication is essential to counsellors. As you can see, each stage of the 
counselling process paves the way for another –providing a base for progression from 
ambiguity to clarity. As you move through each stage, you will gain an understanding of the 
problems, perspectives and insights. Counselling is essentially a communication process and 
counsellors should be good communicators.  
Table 4.1 shows the communication actions required at the different stages of counselling.  
132 
 
 
Table 4.1 Communication during Counselling Stages 
4.2.4 Probing Skills  
Probing involves deep exploration to know extensively as well as intensively the problems 
and related aspects. The inquiry process may make use of several methods such as:  
 · Questioning techniques 
 · Statements  
133 
 
 · Reflective methods  
Questioning techniques  
We will now examine the different types of questions that a counsellor can make use of. We 
will also examine the possible effects of each type of question.  
(a) Open questions: These are invitations to a client to speak openly and enthusiastically 
about the issues bothering him or her. The questions usually begin with what, how, where 
etc., and seek elaborate explanations from the client. Consider this example:   
Counsellor: What happens when you go late to work? How many days were you late and 
what happened most of the times?  
(b) Hypothetical questions: These are open questions that encourage a client to speculate 
about their own feelings, views and behaviour, and those of others. This help  identify the 
future consequences and weigh their impact on the client’s future life. As a result, a lot of 
unknown fears become known, and clients gain confidence to treat themselves as challengers. 
An example is given here: 
Counsellor: If you leave the company what will happen? If you stay what will happen?  
 If the client’s reply is, ‘I don’t know’,  
 Counsellor: If you don’t know, what will you do?  
 If the client’s reply is, ‘I know’,  
 Counsellor: If you know the consequence, how do you see it? As a serious one? As an 
important one?  
(c) Why questions: Some clients may not know the reasons for their behaviour and some 
others may know but prefer not to reveal them. In either case, a question that seeks to elicit 
reasons for the behaviour may not get a proper answer. An exception is a rational transaction. 
When the client and counsellor have adult –to-adult transactions, questions like ‘Why do you 
hate our boss?’ may find answers.  
(d) Closed questions: Questions that require simply ‘yes’ or ‘no’ answers are less desirable. 
They limit the expression of the client and make the process of inquiry cumbersome. The 
counsellor has to put across several questions to obtain a complete picture of the situation. 
Note the following example:  
Client: I am planning to quit the job.  
Counsellor: Who all have been informed about your plan? (Open question)  
Instead of the open question, the counsellor may ask several yes/no questions as given under: 
Client: I am planning to quit the job.  
Counsellor: Did you inform your boss?  
 Client: No 
 Counsellor: Have you shared your plan with your team members?  
134 
 
 Client: No 
Counsellor: Did you discuss with your family members?  
(e) Either/or questions: These questions place the client in a dilemmatic situation and make 
him or her become reserved in expressing opinions. Exploration has limited power and does 
not help counsellors in getting full information. Here is an example:  
Counsellor: Is it your boss or the nature of work that is causing dissatisfaction in your present 
job?  
The options are limited to two. Instead, consider the open question, which offers scope for 
exploring multiple options.  
Counsellor: What in your view are the causal factors for dissatisfaction in your present job?  
(f) Multiple questions: When a counsellor asks many questions at one shot, the result is often 
unsatisfactory. The client may be baffled by the variety of questions, not knowing where to 
begin and what to say. 
Counsellor: Did you talk to your boss? Did you notify your employer that you are quitting the 
job? Did you collect your papers from the new employer?  
(g) Leading questions: Leading questions are those which communicate to the respondent that 
a certain reply is expected. These are loaded with a suggestion and influence the client’s 
mind. Sometimes they may be embarrassing to the client. An example would make the point 
clear.  
Client: The job is challenging and work environment is good. But I am unhappy with 
the pay and benefits.  
 Counsellor: Are you going to say that you feel like leaving the company? 
The client has not revealed his future plans. The counsellor did so by framing a question with 
a suggestive answer. These are undesirable as they affect the freedom of the client.  
To use the questioning technique in an effective manner, let us find out the purpose of 
questioning. Questions serve the following purposes:  
· Help clients to focus on a specific area or issue 
· Provide an opportunity to explain with an open mind  
· Allow clients to have full control of their thoughts and opinions. 
Effective questions are those which have the following characteristics:  
· Specific  
· Clear 
· Concise 
· Direct 
· Open 
135 
 
Statements 
Inquiry need not be based on interrogative approaches. By stating the questions in an 
assertive or declaratory form, one can secure information. This is a mild and gentle form of 
inquiry. Questions may be intrusive or impersonal, whereas statements are more friendly and 
supportive to the client. Consider the following examples: 
Counsellor: What are you going to do now? (question form)  
Counsellor: You said you are unhappy with your boss. I wonder how you respond to him.  
Reflective methods   
The ability to communicate with the client’s internal frame of reference is called reflective 
skill. It first involves understanding what is in the mind of the client. Such an understanding 
is possible by active listening – both words and body language – and interpreting the 
information in an appropriate manner using the probing questions and clarification methods. 
Second, it involves conveying rightly such understanding to the client. Reflecting ensures that 
the external frame of reference (the framework of understanding created by what the client 
has said) is the same as the internal frame of reference (what the client has in his mind). 
Reflective skills can be viewed as a set of three skills: Re -stating, paraphrasing and 
summarizing.  
(a) Re-stating: The counsellor can respond with a word or phrase to encourage the client to 
speak further. Example:  
Client: My boss always criticizes me. He never gives  me any credit …always finds fault with 
whatever I do … should I leave the company…I wonder? 
Counsellor: Criticizing?  
(To know more about the contexts and purpose of criticizing. Who is to blame? Client or boss 
of a client?)  
(b) Paraphrasing: This is the ability to state a key idea from what the client has said. It helps 
gain focus on the important tracts and themes.  
Client: Since childhood, I have been an active person. I won several medals and prizes. I 
stood first in examinations. Of course, due to interest in literature, I could not do well during 
college days. But I have authored two books and they brought me recognition in society. I 
was rated as a fine team leader and given a promotion last year.  
Counsellor: You mean you are a good achiever. 
(c) Summarizing: Summarizing is a process of condensation by integrating key ideas to make 
a precise explanation of what the client has said in detail. Summaries made in the first stage 
of counselling are called attending summaries.  
4.2.5 Problem-Solving Skills 
These refer to a set of skills that helps see the problem, and search for solutions.  
Scanning skills  
136 
 
Scanning is the process of scrutinizing the information relating to a particular problem area. It 
seeks to look in and look around to gather details about the problem. It presents a problems 
map.  
Futuristic thinking skills  
This kind of thinking is essential for making solutions durable. It examines the acts in terms 
of their impact both on other people and on oneself. The key question is: If this solution is 
chosen, what can happen?  
Multiple scenarios 
The counsellor asks his client to generate several imaginary pictures of what the future would 
be like. After identifying a range of possible pictures of the future, the client will be asked to 
choose one which is more realistic and workable for him or her. 
For instance, a client facing a work –life balance problem, may visualize the following 
imaginary pictures: 
 · I would stay at home till my children attain the age of ten years.  
· I would be doing a part time job. My husband will support me and take care of 
the children. 
· I would be doing a flexi -time job. I would adjust my work schedules to meet 
my family’s demands.  
· I would be a doing full -time job and my in -laws/parents will take care of my 
domestic needs.  
For a client who cannot receive support from either parents or in -laws, the last option would 
be unrealistic. For women, whose spouses are non -cooperative, the second option is 
theoretical. The first option is less feasible in case the woman needs to support the family. 
When flexi-time jobs are not offered by local organizations, the client has to make further 
exploration of opportunities to build scenarios.  
Sentence completion 
This is a very simple way of getting clients to think about the future. It allows the client to see 
the positive and negative ‘future states’ of a person and choose the one that is close to his or 
her own preferences and expectations. In the work –life balance problem, the counsellor asks 
the client to fill in the following sentences. 
 · If my husband is supportive… 
 · If my in-laws are supportive…  
Diagnostic skills 
It is a process of identifying the symptoms and causes of problems. Symptoms are manifest 
indications of a problem but they are not the root causes. In -depth analysis to find the cause –
effect relationship is essential to come out with the right problem definitions. George Kelly 
refers to diagnosis in counselling as transitive diagnosis. This means the counsellor will find 
137 
 
out how the client has moved from one situation to another and what factors have influenced 
the transition process.  
Analytical skills 
Analytical thinking is a process of breaking an issue into different aspects and examining 
each aspect or idea to gain a complete understanding. It is a process of identifying variables 
and cause -and-effect relationships, For instance, job dissatisfaction may be due to 
unpleasantness. What factors caused unpleasantness or what are its dimensions? Nature of 
work, relationship with boss, relationship with peers, work environment, pay and benefits, etc 
are the factors. Which ones are pleasant and which ones are unpleasant? Such questioning 
leads to analysis.  
Force-field analysis 
Kurt Lewin (1969) evolved this technique of examining the factors that inhibit/hinder or 
promote/facilitate a decision or a situation. Consider the work –life balance problem. 
Facilitating factors for a client may be: 
 · Attractive remuneration that improves economic stability  
 · Hiring support services is possible 
 · Company has flexi-time facility  
Hindering factors can be:  
 · Children need mother’s care 
 · Support services are not reliable and also costly 
 · In-laws are opposed to employment 
The role of the counsellor is passive if the client can evolve the force -field on his or her own. 
If the client requires help, the counsellor should suggest ways to explore the map of 
possibilities and factors influencing them.  
Creative thinking skills 
Creative thinking goes beyond what is apparent. Thinking within the system tends to be 
limited in scope and provides limited answers. It is important to expand the horizon of think 
and find new and unusual ways to respond to situations. Brainstorming is a creative thinking 
tool. It is an idea generation technique. It helps one think beyond existing options to 
experimental ideas that have not so far been considered infeasible or ruled out as unrealistic. 
The guidelines for brainstorming are:  
 · Invite clients to voice their ideas in a relaxed, open and free atmosphere.  
 · Ask them to suspend their judgement and focus only on idea generation. 
 · Help them build new ideas on the existing or stated ones. 
 · Record ideas.  
 · Limit the session to 15–20 minutes.  
138 
 
 · Evaluate the ideas for implementation and choose one. 
4.2.6 Negotiating Skills 
Clients meet counsellors for help. However, they take time to change their perspectives and 
find solutions. They look for solutions that do not require much behavioural modifications or 
attitudinal changes. They seek easy and quick solutions. Sometimes, counsellors might have 
to convince the clients to take difficult routes in order, to find durable remedies. In such 
cases,  negotiation skills become important.  
Change management skills  
George Kelly describes the cycle of change as a process of five stages, which are as follows:  
· Anticipation: The client looks into the future with expectations and emotional 
feelings like fear, anxiety and hope. 
· Commitment: The client decides to involve and experiment with the change 
process and invest his or her time and energy.  
· Encounter: The client faces the change process and acts in accordance with the 
agreement for change.  
· Confirmation/Disconfirmation: The outcomes confirm the expected results 
and reinforce new behaviour. In case of failure, the client and counsellor 
realize that the change is not effective.  
· Constructive revision: In case of both positive and negative outcome, revision 
is important. In the former, it aims at refinement. In the latter case, it seeks to 
creatively develop new change processes.  
Overcoming resistance 
Clients resist change. Such resistance is common in many contexts of change. However, the 
reasons for resisting change vary from one context to another. The general reasons for 
resisting change are outlined here:  
· Fear of the unknown: Uncertainty about the future and the inability to perceive 
benefits and risks create fear and anxiety in the minds of clients. For example, job 
change is a case of anxiety. How will the new work environment be? How will 
relations with people be there? Such questions and doubts become a source of anxiety.  
· Constraints: A person may face certain stumbling blocks on the path of 
change. For instance, in a job change problems like disturbance to children’s 
education, leaving ailing parents, and non -transferable job of spouse, can become 
stumbling blocks.  
· Perceived rewards are unattractive: When the perceived benefits of change are 
not attractive, change will be unacceptable to the client.  
· Lack of skills: Another barrier to moving into a new situation is lack of the 
skills required. When a client lacks the skills of managing the new situation, he or she 
will hesitate to accept the change plan. 
139 
 
 Check Your Progress 
 1. What does credibility imply in a counsellor?  
 2. What do you mean by reflexivity in counselling?  
 3. Name the stages in the cycle of change as described by George Kelly.  
 
4.3 Models of Counselling 
An analysis of a set of facts in relation to each other is better known as a ‘theory’ has 
an effect on how ‘client communication is conceptualized, new interpersonal relationships 
develop, how professional ethics are implemented, and how counsellor’s view themselves as 
professionals’ (Corey, Neukrug, Mc Aulilife, Prate and Lovell, 1990). Without theoretical 
packing, counsellors’ operate haphazardly in a trial -and-error manner and risk being both 
ineffective and harmful. 
According to Shostrom (1977) ‘theory helps to explain what happens in a counselling 
relationship and assists the counsellor in predicting, evaluating, and improving results. It 
provides a framework for making scientific observation about counselling. Theorizing 
encourages the coherence of ideas about counselling and the production of new ideas.’ Hence, 
counselling theory can be very practical in the sense that they help to make sense of the 
counsellor’s observation in work and thus provide direction. 
Boy and Fine (1983) further state that theory is the why behind the how of counsellor roles. It 
provides a framework within which a counsellor can operate. Boy and Fine have given the 
following six functions of theory that make counselling practical: 
· Theory helps counsellors find unity and relatedness within the diversity of 
existence. 
· Theory compels counsellors to examine relationships they could otherwise 
overlook. 
· Theory gives counsellors operational guidelines by which to work and helps 
them in evaluating their development as professionals. 
· Theory helps counsellors focus on relevant data and tells them what to look 
for. 
· Theory helps counsellors assist clients in the effective modification of their 
behaviour. 
· Theory helps counsellors evaluate both old and new approaches to the process 
of counselling. It is the base from which new counselling approaches are 
constructed. 
The ultimate criterion for all counselling theories is how well they provide explanations of 
what occurs in counselling (Kelly,1988) and to what degree they organize information which 
is grounded in the reality of people’s lives (Young,1988). 
140 
 
In short, an effective counsellor decides which theory or theories and methods to use on the 
basis of educational background, philosophy, and the needs of the clients. All approaches are 
not appropriate for all counsellors or clients, but a theoretical base is very important for every 
effective counsellor. 
An effective counsellor decides which theory or theories and methods to use on the basis of 
educational background, philosophy and the needs of the clients. Not all approaches are 
appropriate for all counsellors or clients, but a theoretical base is necessary for every effective 
counsellor. We have already discussed some of the theories as part of the approaches to 
counselling. Here, we will discuss some more specific ones. 
4.3.1 Rogers Model of Counselling Skills 
Carl Rogers developed client -centred therapy as a reaction against what he considered the 
basic limitations of psychoanalysis. For instance, 
· Rogers (1951) objected to the use of the word patient because of its 
connotation of debility.  He instead advocated the use of word client to convey ‘one 
who comes actively and voluntarily to gain help on a problems, but without any 
notion of surrendering his own responsibility for the situation’. 
· He reacted against the directives of Freudian therapy. He believed that the 
initiative for change should come from the client and hence stated that counselling 
should be nondirective in nature. 
· He also rejected the principal of unconsciousness and replaced it with the new 
concept of the phenomenological view of the client. The phenomenological view of 
the client suggests that the counselor should accept the client’s feelings and world 
view as they are presented, rather than looking for hidden meaning underneath. 
Essentially, the client -centered approach is a specialized branch of humanistic therapy that 
high lights the experiencing of a client and his or her subjective and phenomenal world. The 
therapist functions mainly as a facilitator of personal growth by helping the client discover 
his or her own capacities for solving problems. The client -centered approach puts great faith 
in the client’s capacity to lead the way in therapy and find his or her own direction. The 
therapeutic relationship between the therapist and the client acts as a catalyst for change and 
is a means of increasing awareness and discovering latent resources that the client can use 
constructively in changing their life. Roger (1957) sees people as socialized and positive 
forward-moving, constructive, realistic and trustworthy individuals who are striving to 
become fully functional and deep down are basically good. Each person is aware, inner -
directed, and moving toward self-actualization from infancy onwards. 
Characteristics of client-centred approach 
It focuses on the client’s responsibility and capacity to discover ways to fully face reality. It 
emphasizes the phenomenal world of the client with accurate empathy and an attempt to 
apprehend the client’s internal frame of reference. The therapist concerns him -self or her-self 
mainly with the client’s self -perception and perception of the world. It applies the same 
principle of psychotherapy to all persons— normal, neurotics, and psychotics. 
 
141 
 
The therapeutic process  
The basic goal of therapy is to provide a climate which will suitably aid the individual 
become a fully functioning person. The characteristics that constitute the basic goals of 
client-centred therapy are as follows: 
· Openness to experience: refers to seeing reality without distorting it to fit a 
preconceived self-structure. It also means that one’s beliefs are not rigid; and that one 
can remain open to further knowledge and growth and can tolerate ambiguity. One has 
an awareness of oneself in the present moment and also has the capacity to experience 
oneself in fresh ways. 
· Trust in one’s organism: refers to the experience that as clients become more 
open to their experiences, their sense of trust in self begins to emerge. 
· An internal locus of evaluation: means looking more to oneself for the answers 
to the problems of existence. 
· Willingness to be a process: it emphasizes the concept of self in the process of 
becoming as opposed to the concept of self as a product. 
In the client-centred approach the therapist does not choose specific goals for the client as he 
or she believes that the clients have the capacity to define and clarify their own goals. 
According to Rogers (1961), as a result of person -centered counselling clients should 
become: 
 · More realistic in their self-perceptions. 
 · More confident and self-directing. 
 · More positively valued by themselves. 
 · Less likely to repress aspects of their experiences. 
 · More mature, socialized and adaptive in their behaviour. 
 · Less upset by stress and quicker to recover from it. 
 · More like the healthy integrated well -functioning person in their personality 
structures. 
The therapist’s function is to establish a therapeutic climate that facilitates the client’s growth 
along a process continuum in which the client will ‘experience the necessary freedom to 
explore areas of his or her life that are now either denied to awareness or are distorted’. 
Instead of perceiving the client in pre-concerned diagnostic categories, the therapist meets the 
client on a moment -to-moment experiential basis and helps the client by entering the client’s 
world. 
The counsellor is aware of the verbal and non -verbal language of the client and the counselor 
reflects back what he or she is hearing or observing (Braaten, 1986).                             
Person-centred counsellors make limited use of psychological tests. Only if the client 
requests, testing is usually done. The counsellor then focuses on the meaning of the test for 
142 
 
that client rather than on test scores. One innovative test often used in evaluating clients is the 
Q-sort technique (Hergenhahu,1984). This procedure has three steps: 
· The client is given hundred cards, each of which contains a self -descriptive 
sentence, such as ‘I am intelligent’. 
· The client is asked to place the cards in nine piles from ‘most like me’ to ‘least 
like me’. After this self-sort, the client sorts the cards again by placing them according 
to how he or she would ideally like to do. 
· The final step of the process involves correlating the degree of similarity 
between the two sorts before, during and after counselling. 
The client’s experience in therapy  
Clients initially may expect the counsellor to provide awareness and direction or view the 
counsellor as the expert who can provide magical solutions. Within the client -ordered 
framework however, they soon realize that they are responsible for themselves in the 
relationship and that they can learn to be free by using the relationship to gain greater self -
understanding. 
During the beginning stages of therapy, the client’s behaviour and feeling might be 
characterized by extremely rigid beliefs and attitudes, much internal blockage, a lack of 
centeredness, a sense of living out of touch with his or her own feelings, an unwillingness to 
communicate deeper levels of the self, a fear of intimacy, a basic distrust in the self, a sense 
of fragmentation and tendency to externalize feelings and problems, just to mention a few. In 
the therapeutic climate created by the counselor, the client is able to explore in a safe and 
trusting environment the hidden aspects of his or her personal world. 
As therapy progresses, the client is able to express fears, anxiety, guilt, shame, anger, hatred 
and other feelings that he or she had deemed too positive to accept and incorporate into the 
self-structure.    
The relationship between therapist and client: The therapist and client relationship has a 
special ‘I -thou’ personal quality. It is the relationship with a congruent, accepting, and 
empathic counsellor that serves as the agent of therapeutic change for the client. According to 
Roger, there are certain attributes on the therapist’s part (genuineness, Non -possessive 
warmth and acceptance, and accurate empathy) that constitute the necessary and sufficient 
conditions for therapeutic effectiveness to occur within the client. Client -centred therapy 
focuses on the ‘here -and-now’ experience created by the relationship between the client and 
the therapist. 
According to Roger (1967), the following six conditions are necessary and sufficient for 
personality change to occur within the client-therapist relationship: 
 1. Two persons are in psychological contact. 
2. The first person called as the client, is in a state of incongruence, being 
vulnerable or anxious. 
3. The second person called as the therapist, is congruent or integrated in the 
relationship. 
143 
 
 4. The therapist experiences unconditional positive regard for the client. 
5. The therapist experiences an empathic understanding of the client’s internal 
frame of reference and tries to communicate this experience to the client. 
6. The communication to the client of the therapist is empathic and 
understanding and unconditional positive regard is to a minimal degree 
achieved. 
Roger further added that these conditions do not vary according to client type. Further, they 
are necessary and sufficient for all approaches to therapy and apply to all personal 
relationships and not just to psychotherapy alone. 
There are three personal characteristics of the therapist that form a central part of the 
therapeutic relationship .These are: 
 · Congruence or genuineness: Congruence implies that the therapist is real, i.e. 
the therapist is genuine, integrated, and authentic during the therapy. He or she is without a 
false front; his or her inner experience and outer expression of that experience match, and he 
or she can openly express feelings and attitudes that are present in the relationship with the 
client. The therapist can facilitate honest communication with the client by expressing (and 
accepting) any positive feelings. Client -centered therapy does, however, stress the value of a 
non-exploitive, authentic, personal relationship and the positive value of open and honest 
feedback when meaningful communication is blocked. 
 · Unconditional regard: Unconditional  regard is a deep and genuine caring for 
the client as a person. The caring is unconditional on that it is not contaminated by evaluation 
or judgement of the client’s feelings, thoughts, and behaviour as good or bad. The therapist 
values and warmly accepts the client as he or she is. In this sense acceptance is the 
recognition of the client’s right to have feelings. The therapist’s caring should be non -
possessive and should not stem from the therapist’s own need to be liked and appreciated, as 
then the change in the client would be inhibited. Like congruence, unconditional positive 
regard is a matter of degree on a continuum. The greater the degree of liking, caring, and 
warmly accepting the client; the greater is the opportunity to facilitate change in the client.  
 · Accurate empathic understanding: One of the main tasks of the therapist is to 
be sensitive and comprehend accurately the client’s experiences and feelings as they come 
forth during the moment -to-moment interaction during the therapy session. The therapist 
aims and tries ‘to sense’ the client’s subjective experience, particularly the here -and-now 
experience. “The aim of empathic understanding is to encourage the client to get closer to 
himself or herself, to experience feelings more deeply and intensely and to recognize and 
resolve the incongruity that exists within the client.” The therapist can communicate to the 
client an understanding of what is already known to the client and can also explain the 
meaning of experience of which the client is only dimly aware. Empathy is more than a mere 
reflection of feeling. It is a deep and subjective understanding of the client by the therapist. It 
can also be understood as a sense of personal identification with the client, when the therapist 
can grasp the personal experiencing of the client’s private world. 
 
 
144 
 
Techniques  
Person-centred techniques have evolved through the years. Hart (1970), identified three 
periods of this evolution, each of which stresses different techniques. These three periods 
have been described below: 
 · Non-directive period (1940 -1950): During this period the therapist -client 
relationship was emphasized by creating a permissive and non -interventional atmosphere. 
The main techniques used were acceptance and clarification. 
 · Reflective period (1950 -1957): The major emphasis was laid on creating a 
non-threatening relationship. The techniques used for the same were responding to client’s 
feelings and reflecting back to clients underlying affect. 
 · Experiential period (1957 -1980): During this period Roger emphasized that 
the empathy, positive regard and genuineness are three necessary and sufficient conditions of 
counseling. “The techniques that were used to promote the client -therapist relationship were 
active and passive listening; accurate reflection of thoughts and feelings; clarification; 
summarization; confrontation and open-ended leads.” (Poppen and Thompson, 1974). 
Overall, the person-centred approach places a minimum emphasis on formal techniques and a 
maximum focus on the therapeutic relationship. 
Application to school  
The philosophy underlying client -centred theory has direct application to the teaching or 
learning process. As Roger believes that the student can be trusted to discover significant 
problems that are related to the student’s existence. The students can become involved in 
meaningful learning, which can best occur when the teacher creates a climate of freedom and 
trust. The teacher’s function is like that of the client -centred therapist, where the teacher with 
his genuineness, openness, honesty, acceptance, understanding, empathy and willingness 
allows students to explore personally meaningful material and create the atmosphere in which 
significant learning can occur.  
Evaluation of client-centred approach  
Some of the major strengths of the client-centred approach are given below: 
· It revolutionized the profession of counselling by linking counselling with 
psychotherapy. 
· This approach has generated a great amount of research and initially set the 
standard for doing research on the counselling variables. In fact, Roger was 
the first practitioner to make audiotape recordings of the counselling sessions. 
· According to Grummon (1972), the person -centred approach is effective as it 
helps improve psychological adjustment, learning and frustration tolerance 
and helps decrease defensiveness. 
· The approach focuses on the importance of an open client -counsellor 
relationship and the short term nature of the process. 
145 
 
· It emphasizes that the client and not the counsellor is the centre or the focus of 
the counselling process and therefore the initiative to overcome the problems 
should come from the client itself. 
· It emphasizes the importance of the counselling relationship in facilitating 
change. 
· It also stresses the counsellor’s attitude than the technique used by him/her in 
making the therapeutic relationship effective. 
· The approach takes a relatively short time to learn and lays emphasis on 
mastering the listening skills. In this regard, it forms the basis for training 
many paraprofessional helpers. 
· It also adopts a positive view of human nature and believes that people can 
change and it thus emphasises the change process. 
However, this approach has certain limitations too: 
· It has limited applicability and is seldom employed with severely handicapped 
or young children (Thompson and Rudolph, 1988). 
 · The approach is considered to be overly optimistic by many critics. 
· Some believe that this approach cannot make a permanent impact on the 
person as it is short term and deals only with surface issues and it does not 
challenge the client to explore deeper areas. 
 · The approach seems to have the same goal for all clients of self -actualization. 
This general goal does not take into account the individual needs of clients. 
· This approach does not consider the category of people who accept little 
responsibility for their problems. 
· Also, it emphasizes the affective aspects of behaviour and ignores the 
important role played by the intellectual, cognitive and rational aspects of 
behaviour. 
In short, this approach has much to offer to the field of counselling. 
4.3.2 Carkhuff Model of Counselling Skills, 
Carkhuff’s book, Toward Effective Counselling and Psychotherapy, was published in 1967. A 
more thorough study of Carkhuff’s book, Helping and Human Relations, V olumes  I and II, 
was published in 1969. His work gradually transformed clinical psychology as well as 
counselling in educational setting. 
· Robert R. Carkhuff created the helping and human relationship theory to 
explain how people interact in the professional setting. 
· Creating definitions and observing interactions before engaging in any helping 
behaviour were the first steps in Carkhuff’s model. 
 · He thought that all interactions between people are helpful. 
146 
 
 · Some interactions are more balanced than others. 
· In some encounters, the dominant party tries to persuade the other person  to 
change, usually to their point of view. 
· Other interactions involve a transaction, where each participant is pursuing a 
distinct objective. 
· Carkhuff thought that most professional encounters were beneficial to 
relationships. 
· He listed repairmen, training, parenting, medical, and teaching among these 
professions. In other words, helping behaviours are a universal skill  among 
everyone. 
· In order to end successful relationships and assess their success, Carkhuff 
developed his helping model and human relations theory. 
· One of the helping professions is nursing, which involves engagement with the 
client to improve their condition. There is helper relationship theory in 
nursing. 
· This framework for the art of helping and human relationships developed by 
nursing theorists matches the nurse-client relationship quite well. 
The four elements of any helping relationship form the basis of Robert R. Carkhuff’s model. 
These elements are discussed below. 
Attending 
Attending entails starting the conversation. There is a greeting, an introduction of each party 
to the relationship, and an effort to make the client feel comfortable. In accordance with the 
human relationship theory and Carkhuff’s model of helping, the nurse must be there in 
person. This implies that the nurse is entirely focused on the client and that the nurse looks at, 
engages, and speaks to the client in order to ensure that the client is aware that the nurse is 
there for the client’s benefit. The nurse should keep an eye on all of the client’s responses. Is 
the client avoiding interaction with you? Is the client prepared to discuss their issue openly? 
Are they discussing something else to put off having the necessary conversations?  
The nurse must pay close attention to the client and make sure they fully comprehend what 
they are trying to express. He/she must wait for the customer to finish outlining their account 
of the events. To be able to confirm that the client has spoken what the nurse thinks she has 
heard, the nurse should receive training in professional communication techniques. The 
interaction between a nurse and a client depends on this procedure. The interaction will fail if 
the attending step is improperly completed. The following particular skills  help in the 
Attending stage: 
 · Taking note of both verbal and non-verbal cues 
 · Responding fundamental messages with paraphrases 
 · Bringing discussion into focus by making issues clear 
147 
 
 · Review the client’s perceptions of their issue. 
Responding 
The nurse should start informing the client about the expected response and any available 
options once the interaction’s purpose has been established and the client has consented to it. 
The helper starts responding to the client. She looks for feelings to which she can respond. 
She searches for information to explain why the client needs aid. Finally, the nurse must be 
able to comprehend what the client interprets as the issue. The client and the nurse should 
collaborate to come up with a course of action that they can both accept. Responding requires 
a unique set of skills just like attending.  
 · Emphasis should be placed on having empathy for the client’s requirements. 
 · Consider the perspective of the client. 
 · Verify your understanding of the client by using rephrasing. 
Personalizing 
Here, the client commits to take the actions required to carry out the previously set goals. At 
this stage, relating your goals to an earlier action or situation is the best strategy for success. 
The outcomes are better when the situation is more favourable. An unfavourable outcome can 
be utilized as a lesson, however, to demonstrate how the client might alter the outcomes in 
the future. Perhaps difficulties in an  addict’s life caused him to relapse after a period of 
sobriety. This interaction would be focused on helping him consider his options while under 
pressure and learn new coping mechanisms. Personalization requires a separate set of 
abilities. Here are a few to consider: 
 · Expression of feelings 
 · Communicating personal feelings to a client 
 · Communication techniques with clients 
 · Reflective language to confirm objectives 
Initiating 
The nurse helps the client execute or start these adjustments in their lives after they have 
agreed on the measures to change. This part entails creating specific objectives and checklists 
to achieve them. The client needs to be aware of every stage and how to contact the nurse if 
they run into difficulties achieving their goals. The client shouldn’t feel like a failure for 
having to come up with a revised plan if they are made aware that many patients need to 
modify their treatment plan once they begin implementing it into their lives. Success should 
be the main focus instead of strictly adhering to one and only one set of objectives and 
benchmarks. Each professional should also have a feedback phase when they consider both 
their achievement with this client and any areas where they had difficulties. Additionally, it 
will serve as a reminder of the behaviours the caregivers needs to work on in order to 
enhance client outcomes. This step prepares the path for achievement and interaction 
verification. Here are the abilities needed: 
 · The capacity to define useful goals 
148 
 
 · Ability to offer solutions in case of difficulties 
· Additionally, the capacity to conduct enquiries to confirm objective 
completion 
4.3.3 IPR Model of Counselling Training 
The IPR model is described by the APA as a technique used to comprehend psychotherapy 
procedures and to train counsellors and therapists. It entails recording counselling or 
psychotherapy sessions on film or with an audio recorder. They watch these tapes again later, 
and the therapist or counsellor reviews them in front of a supervisor. The client’s counsellor 
or therapist is questioned by the supervisor about their feelings. The IPR model of 
counselling was established by Norman Kagan and colleagues. A strategy for supervision is 
the IPR model. Counsellors are given the ability to comprehend and take action on 
observations that they might not otherwise pay attention to. The IPR model’s objectives are 
to:  
· Make counsellors more aware of their own and their clients’ secret thoughts 
and feelings. 
· To enhance the relationship between the counsellor and the client by practising 
the expression of  secret thoughts and feelings in the present without 
repercussions. 
The foundation of IPR is the idea that, more than any other factor, counsellors’ selective 
perceptions of issues thwart their therapeutic efforts. The foundation of IPR is two elements 
of human behaviour. IPR is founded on two aspects of human nature: the need for one 
another and the development of mutual fear. According to Kagan’s (1980) theory, humans can 
be the greatest source of happiness for one another. But because a person’s first imprinted 
experiences are of being a small being in a large person’s universe, underlying sentiments of 
fear and helplessness could linger all of one’s life. The majority of the time, these fears go 
unacknowledged and unspoken. 
In order to maintain a ‘safe’ psychological distance from others, people who need but fear 
others develop an approach -avoidance syndrome. People consequently frequently act 
diplomatically. According to Kagan (1980), counsellors’ ‘diplomatic’ behaviour can take the 
form of either seeming to be feigning clinical naiveté  or tuning out clients’ communications. 
When counsellors appear as if they did not comprehend the significance behind client 
statements, they are feigning clinical naiveté, which is frequently a sign that they are 
unwilling to become involved with clients at a certain level. 
The majority of time, unskilled counsellors who are preoccupied with their own thoughts will 
tune out. He might be considering his options for the future. As a result, the counsellor misses 
client communications, some of which may appear clear to the supervisor. As a result, neither 
the client nor the counsellor acknowledges a richness of information that is discussed in 
counselling sessions. There are numerous different degrees of interaction, but only a small 
subset of these exchanges are labelled by clients and counsellors (Kagan, 1980). Counsellors 
who tend to behave diplomatically may miss some dynamics of the counsellor/client 
interaction that IPR helps them become more aware of. 
149 
 
In rare cases, the inquirer may meet with the counsellor and his or her client in addition to 
conducting IPR with the counsellor alone. He might just as easily meet with the client by 
himself. Mutual recollection sessions frequently aid counsellors in learning to communicate 
with client. 
Steps in conducting IPR 
A recall session can be conducted using the guidelines below: 
 · Before the supervision session, listen to or watch the recording. Usually, the 
recall session does not allow for a full tape review. It’s crucial to pre -select the tape segments 
that matter most interpersonally. 
 · Tell the supervisee about the recall session. Establish a relaxed atmosphere 
while stressing that there is too much information in counselling sessions for a counsellor to 
cover. Additionally, the session’s goal is for the client and the counsellor to reflect on the 
thoughts and feelings that were discussed throughout the session. 
 · Start playing the recording; at appropriate intervals, one of the participants 
stops it and asks a pertinent lead to affect the study. The supervisee will speak first if the tape 
is stopped, outlining any ideas or emotions that were present at that point in the counselling 
session. By posing pertinent, open -ended inquiries, the supervisor aids in the discovery 
process. Pay attention to the supervisee’s nonverbal reactions during this period of 
questioning and consider any discrepancies between those responses and their spoken 
counterparts. 
 · Avoid using a teaching approach and telling the supervisee what they may 
have done differently during the recall session. Instead, let the supervisee work out their 
emotions and thoughts on their own. 
Conclusion 
IPR offers supervisees a secure setting to explore internal reactions by reliving the client 
interaction through a process recall supervision session. IPR has also been demonstrated to be 
helpful in peer supervision (Kagan, 1980), group supervision (Gimmestad and Greenwood, 
1974), and supervisor -supervised relationships (Bernard, 1989).   IPR has constantly been 
shown to be an efficient medium for supervision in research. For instance, Kagan and 
Krathwohl (1967) and Kingdon (1975) discovered that clients of counsellors who were under 
IPR supervision performed better than clients of counsellors under other types of supervision. 
With experienced counsellors, trainee counsellors, and paraprofessionals, they showed that 
the model works (Bernard, 1989). However, it is easy to magnify the client -counsellor 
interpersonal dynamics to the point of distortion (Bernard and Goodyear, 1992). As a result, 
they do not suggest IPR as the exclusive method of supervision. It is  beneficial when 
combined with other supervisory techniques. IPR gives counsellors the chance to face their 
interpersonal fears, comprehend intricate counsellor/client dynamics, and make the most of 
their client interactions (Kagan, 1980). 
4.3.4 Integrative and Eclectic Counselling 
In Integrative and eclectic counselling and psychotherapy, (Palmer and Woolfe, 1999), the 
authors make a clear distinction between integrative and eclectic approaches: “Integration 
150 
 
suggests that the elements are part of one combined approach to theory and practice, as 
opposed to eclecticism which draws ad hoc from several approaches in the approach to a 
particular case.” The eclectic counsellors do not let themselves be bound by the dogma, or 
standard practices of psychotherapy. Instead, they chart their own path and use the method 
that seem to be the best possible for a particular client. They fuse together various schools of 
psychological theories and come up with a unique one. Besides this, there are different 
aspects to integrative psychotherapy: 
 · Common factors approach: This approach attempts to establish the connection 
between the core ideas of different theories and thus find the commonality among them.  
 · Technical eclecticism: This approach tries to isolate the best possible therapy 
method for a particular problem by studying data on what has worked best in the past for a 
similar problem. This helps to demonstrate how a certain theory has helped in a specific case 
and can then be re-applied in some part in another case. 
 · Theoretical integration: In this approach, two or more therapy methods are 
merged in the hope that they will give better results than when used individually. A well -
known example of such integration is Anthony Ryle’s (2005) model of Cognitive Analytic 
Therapy, which integrates the psychoanalytic object -relations theory and cognitive 
psychotherapy. 
 · Assimilative integration: This theory is based on the recognized fact that most 
counsellors choose a theoretical orientation as the foundation and, based on prior experience, 
integrate methods and strategies from other orientations into the therapy they provide to a 
client. 
An integrative approach to counseling and psychotherapy is best characterized by attempts to 
look beyond and across the confines of single -school approaches in order to see what can be 
learned from, and how clients can benefit from, other perspectives (Arkowitz, 1997). Since 
all human beings are different and come from different backgrounds, it is not fair to try to 
apply the same theories to all of them and forcefully fit their experiences into the paradigms 
of one particular theory.  
To avoid such gross generalization, Arnold Lazarus came up with the multimodal theory. The 
multimodal orientation begins with the supposition that therapy must appraise seven discrete 
but interactive modalities (abbreviated by the acronym BASIC ID, which stands for 
Behaviour, Affect, Sensation, Imagery, Cognition, Interpersonal factors, and Drug/Biological 
considerations). This psycho -educational framework encourages therapists to improvise and 
tailor therapy to the client. This theory establishes the usefulness of systematic or technical 
eclecticism. As per Lazarus, it is dangerous to blend bits and pieces of different theories, as 
eclectics do. He believed that this selective mixing will only serve to further complicate 
matters. So, he maintained that a practitioner should remain consistent theoretically but vary 
in the technical approach towards various patients. The eclecticism, for Lazarus, was better 
left in the technical part of counselling, rather than the theoretical part. Lazarus’s theory has 
proved especially useful when looked at from the perspective of the different cultures that 
exist in the world. All theories do not fit into the context of all cultures, and a practitioner 
who claims to be ‘eclectic’ needs to be well -versed in various theories and use them 
selectively, as per the cultural and other case-specific requirements of a client.  
151 
 
 
Lazarus also warns that blending theories is in fact, trickier than employing diverse 
techniques advocated by various schools of thought. The counsellors who try to fit diverse 
theoretical orientations into their own unique integrative model will first need to determine if 
the various theories are even compatible with each other. This in fact, is the basis for 
systematic eclecticism. It tries to establish the basic connection among the core ideas of the 
various theories being considered.  
Human beings themselves are integrated creatures, a mass of contradictions, ideas, notions 
and beliefs. Only that counselling can prove effective for them which allows them to combine 
their cognitive, affective and behavioural aspects. Such a counselling experience would 
allow: 
 · To think about ideas and assumptions 
 · To feel the struggles and conflicts 
 · To act in new ways in their daily lives in order to transform their behaviours 
As per Arnold Lazarus, a counselor armed with the database of multiple theories, will need to 
ask himself or herself the question at all times: What is the key requirement of this person in 
order to suffer less, and to cope, heal and grow more effectively in the process? 
The main disadvantage of the integrative approach is that if a counselor is unable to detach 
from preconceived notions and personal prejudices, he or she will rationalize the choice of 
theory whether it makes sense or not and this may end up being damaging for the client.
