4.4 Indian Models of Counselling 
Modern India conjures the thoughts of a thriving technology sector and an economy 
that is expanding at an unheard of rate. Significant societal change has resulted from 
economic progress. The necessity for counselling in modern day India arises from a social, 
cultural, and economic attitude that has never before existed in this nation. Traditional Indian 
healing focuses on the individual as a whole. This would encompass not only the person’s 
physical form but also the person’s thinking, feelings, beliefs, spiritual preferences, 
employment position, and all other facets of his or her existence. It would also cover the 
nature of the person’s connections with society and the ties that bind them. Ayurveda, an 
ancient form of traditional Indian medicine, describes in depth how emotions are connected 
to physical ailments and how maintaining the right balance between one’s own needs and 
those of their social connections is the key to good health (Das, 1974). An approach to 
counselling that separates the individual from the family and the mind from the body will 
probably fall short of meeting the perceived need in the Indian context.  
 
152 
 
Determinism 
Karma and Samsara, two philosophical concepts, are frequently characterised as having a 
fatalistic outlook on life. The idea that past deeds determine the present may inspire a feeling 
of inevitability. Free will is not contradicted by the concepts of Karma and Samsara. The 
current effort has a direct impact on future growth and success. Therefore, how one lives their 
current life may have an impact on how their future lives turn out. This focus on personal 
accountability is a helpful cue for counselling strategies that lean on the client’s cultural 
readiness.  
Self-Observation 
The major questions about self-observation are: 
· Why should we be talking about something that is self -oriented when 
everyone is talking about inclusive prosperity?  
· What drives us to put our attention on ourselves when there is so much to be 
done for the overall development of humanity?  
 · Isn’t that acting selfishly?  
That seems to contradict the theory of human evolution. Actually, not quite; before ever 
considering the evolution of humans, one needs to think about their own evolution. Every 
single human being must contribute to collective evolution. However, this large -scale 
evolution wouldn’t be conceivable until there is a conscious effort put for micro evolution. 
Micro evolution, here, translates to self -evolution. Observing oneself involves more than just 
looking inward; it also entails becoming aware of oneself by focusing on oneself and working 
toward growth.  
Another well-known online dictionary defines ’Self -Observation’ as the study of one’s own 
feelings or ideas. Their definitions are accurate, and that is where this conversation begins. As 
you might have guessed, the questions that form the basis of this conversation start with the 
query, ‘What is Self-observation?’ A second question follows, and then there are some more. 
There are more questions that surfaces as answers to these questions, just like an onion, 
where there is a whole other layer of onion inside as we peel back the outer layer. 
Every person has a secret universe of thoughts, feelings, and interior moods that is mainly 
unknown to them. These internal states —which influence decisions, inspire behaviours, and 
mould life experiences —come from the subconscious. But they are hardly ever noticed or 
consciously understood. One can perceive and comprehend these inner states through self -
observation. Everyone has this innate ability, which can be increased and expanded with 
effort. One can learn about the inner workings of the mind and delve into the dark recesses of 
our psychology by using this straightforward yet effective technique. One’s interactions with 
life can change drastically because of learning more about oneself and others. Self -
observation is the cornerstone of effective inner transformation that yields great wisdom, 
understanding, and calm. There are several techniques for self-observation.  
 
 
153 
 
4.4.1 Role of Detachment in Indian Counselling  
Leading dictionaries define detachment as: 
 · The act or process of disconnecting or detaching; separation. 
 · The state of being separate or detached. 
 · Indifference to or remoteness from the concerns of others; aloofness 
 · Indifference to worldly concerns 
 · Absence of prejudice or bias; disinterest 
Detachment has been extensively discussed in ancient Indian texts including the Vedas and 
Upanishads. One of the basic concepts of detachment is discussed in Bhagavad Gita. In 
ancient Indian language, Sanskrit, the detachment means Vairagya. The Sanskrit term, 
Vairagya, which was used in ancient Indian philosophy, means ‘renunciation,’ ‘detachment,’ 
or ‘dispassion’, especially in the sense of renunciation from the pleasures and pains of the 
corporeal world. Hindu sages who promoted vairagya advised their followers that doing so is 
a way to reach moksha (salvation). 
The word Vairagya is derived from the word viraga, which is formed by merging the words 
‘vi’, which means ‘without’ and ‘raga’, which means ‘passion, feeling, emotion, interest’. 
With regard to things that most people would become attached to, this lends the term 
‘Vairagya’ its general connotation of ‘ascetic disinterest’. This way of thinking about life is 
‘dis-passionate’. A Vairâgika is an ascetic who has mastery over all passions and desires. The 
root ‘rañj’, which denotes colour, is further defined by etymology as follows: Vi – rañj + 
ghaiK = virága. Vaerágya is the virága state. Virága meaning ‘to go beyond colour’ or ‘to be 
uncoloured’. Vaerágya is the state of being fully present in the world but unaffected by it. A 
Vairagi is someone who practises Vairagya. 
The Patañjali’s Yoga Sûtras contain the idea of Vairâgya, which, with practice, is the key to 
controlling the mind’s fluctuations. The Bhagavad Gita uses the term Vairâgya three times 
and recommends it as a crucial technique for calming the racing mind. Additionally, it is the 
main focus of Mokcopâya or Yoga-Vâsicmha. Without attachment, we are liberated from this 
point of view of a wandering mind where the mind automatically goes to areas it has 
developed a habit of going to in the past. Non -attachment entails apathy for the outside 
world. The ultimate objective of enlightenment is one that necessitates self -sacrifice; it is a 
challenging undertaking and the state is very tough to accomplish with death being the final 
test; this emphasises detachment. The bodisatva is one who controls their senses, giving up 
all attraction and aversion, and setting aside the sounds and sights that might otherwise lead 
to attachment in their sense organs. 
Along with non -violence, detachment is one of Jainism’s highest ideals. One of the 
Mahavratas, the five main vows kept by Jain monks, is non -possession/non-attachment. 
Detachment has value if it is combined with the understanding that a person is a soul, and it 
can also be a technique of achieving self -realisation. According to Jain saint Shrimad 
Rajchandra, detachment and other qualities are necessary for achieving self -realization for 
lifeless ritualists; simple physical constraint is ineffective in this regard. He advises that one 
154 
 
should engage in such things but not become trapped in them. Without self -realization, the 
fundamental cause of birth and death cannot be eradicated. 
Arjuna is instructed by Krishna to behave with detachment, which entails taking the right 
action regardless of whether it succeeds or fails. Krishna advises Arjuna to keep trying to do 
his best because that is what destiny requires of him to do. His destiny is his continued good 
performance of his role. The detachment is in not being moved by the emotions connected to 
living with continued awareness of success and failure because both are irrelevant. Treating 
joy and sorrow equally, gain and loss equally, and success and victory equally. Treating these 
agitating, painful, or upsetting dichotomies equally may be considered detachment. Arjuna 
must perform his task without fear of harm or loss by being detached from the results of his 
deeds. Arjuna is advised that if he battles with composure and views all possible outcomes as 
equal, he will not amass negative karma. If one sacrifices their own interests and works solely 
to carry out their responsibility towards the Supreme Being, they are freed from any karmic 
repercussions. 
Detachment can take many different forms, but it is obvious that it can be destructive if used 
incorrectly. Krishna appears to advise detachment in the form of three broad categories in 
Chapter 12 of the Bhagavad-Gita: 
· A lack of attachment to material possessions, as evidenced by his statement that the 
ideal bhakta is someone ’who has nothing of his own.’ 
· A lack of concern for the results of our deeds, as seen by allusions to the ideal devotee 
as someone who ‘is the same in praise and blame’ and ‘does not care if good comes or ill.’ 
· The ability to remain emotionally detached, as seen by his description of the ideal 
bhakta as being ‘even-minded in suffering and joy.’ 
The issue is that when any of these three categories are taken too far, they can provide 
negative insights rather than ones that elevate us. 
· If the objective is to entirely free us from material wants, then the question arises: Are 
material possessions not essential to life?  
· Why should we maintain our ties to food, clothing, or shelter in this situation?  
· This may eventually give rise to the notion that the body is likewise material in a 
sense, then why must we stay linked to it or live?  
· It is feasible to believe that we can be separated from any motivation for acting if we 
choose not to be linked to the results of our activities. 
· Why bother doing good work, if it doesn’t really matter what comes from the work 
that we do?  
· If intense emotional response is bad, why bother caring about anyone? 
Such thoughts can be harmful, so it’s perhaps better to consider them from the other 
perspective and consider how detachment benefits us in daily life. Detachment is nothing 
more than attachment to something higher, whether that something ‘higher’ be God, an ideal 
trait we desire to adopt into our lives, or a goal we have, according to one point of view. We 
155 
 
can turn such queries into something constructive if we believe that detachment is something 
that will benefit us in this life: 
· It’s possible that being detached from material possessions means we shouldn’t be 
emotionally invested in this body, but that’s only because doing so entails being committed to 
something ephemeral. This doesn’t imply that we should despise our bodies or the concept of 
life, rather, it might imply that we should use our fleeting bodies as tools to pursue higher 
ideals, such as selfless service. In order to avoid becoming emotionally connected to things 
that are not ours, we should utilise our lives as an opportunity to help others and improve the 
world as much as we can. 
· Labour without attachment is considerably different from work without purpose. 
Therefore, perhaps it is more crucial to keep working for the greater good rather than being 
concerned with the meaning and effects of our labours. At the same time, we should 
acknowledge that working is all we can do because there is no practical way to influence how 
everything we do turns out. 
How can we afford to be careless? This seems to be a fairly difficult question. According to 
psychology, emotional reaction is a significant component of what makes us human and 
affects how we respond to social circumstances and make decisions. It is challenging, if not 
impossible, to survive without any emotions. The root cause of the issue is over -emotion. 
Simply establishing the boundary between proper and excessive emotion is the difficult part. 
Does detachment actually mean renunciation; renouncing all the worldly pleasures? We 
should be totally committed to achieving higher objectives while being detached enough to 
avoid becoming too frustrated by failure or overconfidence as a result of success. Detachment 
resembles attachment to a higher goal in this sense. Krishna teaches Arjuna the way to 
resolve the dilemma of renunciation and action. Freedom lies, not in the renunciation of the 
world, but in disciplined action (Karma Yoga). Put concretely, all actions are to be performed 
without attachment to the fruits of the action while being dedicated in loving devotion to 
Krishna. Disciplined action within the context of devotion is essential to the religious life 
envisioned in the Gita. If we choose to forgo our own interests, renunciation appears to be a 
preferable course. We relinquish the good and bad of the earthly world by renunciation. Our 
souls are ultimately cleansed because of this. A cleansed soul is pure. Therefore, we are also 
clean. All of this is accomplished without confronting the means -ends relationship or the 
contradiction of disinterested conduct. Undoubtedly, this purifying technique may be more 
challenging than Karma Yoga.  
Rejecting love, relationships, joys, suffering, and attachment calls for an almost limitless 
mental fortitude and iron will. Any advantages of renunciation will be outweighed by the 
wisdom gained by actively pursuing selfless conduct. This approach confronts a challenge 
head-on rather than ducking and diving. The teachings of Sri Aurobindo follow a similar 
pattern. Yoga and meditation have historically been seen as tools for renunciation in India, 
but according to him, these practises equip us to be present in life rather than serve as a 
means of avoiding it. Unfortunately, a lot of individuals believe incorrectly that devotion and 
attachment are synonyms. When we are in the finite and connected to the finite, it is when we 
are attached. When we devote ourselves to the Infinite and are set free by the Infinite, we are 
said to be dedicated. It’s wrong to be detached. We believe that someone is indifferent if they 
are disconnected.  
156 
 
The same error is made by spiritual seekers who believe that in order to be detached from 
someone, we must treat him/her with complete indifference and disregard. That is untrue. 
When we don’t care about someone, we don’t do anything for him/her. We are not involved 
in his happiness or sadness, success or failure. But when we are genuinely disengaged, we 
give all of ourselves to serving that person. Whether the outcome is success or failure is 
irrelevant. If we are not at anyway dependent on the outcomes, our consciousness 
immediately expands. If we don’t care about the results of our actions, the Supreme will 
reward us in the way that the Supreme chooses. ‘Thou hast the right to act, but not to the 
fruits of doing,’ remarked Lord Krishna. Action does not bound us if we work diligently and 
selflessly. If we labour without thinking about the outcome, working for God’s sake won’t be 
difficult. True detachment, or spiritual detachment, is what this is.  
4.4.2 Role of Self-Surrender in Indian Counselling  
Self-surrender is nothing more than a condition of complete detachment from oneself and 
submission to the will of the God. A continual occurrence of this situation triggers the 
development of negation. We start to draw a steady stream of the highest divine force from 
the great God when we give ourselves over to Him. A man only thinks or acts in accordance 
with his God’s wishes when in this state. He perceives nothing in the world as being his 
property, but rather, everything as a holy trust from the God, and he acts in accordance with 
this perception. His will becomes wholly obedient to the God’s will. 
It stops the mind’s oscillations and puts us to a stationary state. Over time, we start to feel as 
though we are being driven away from everything else by a powerful force. We stop engaging 
in undesired behaviours and constantly focus on ensuring that our organs are operating 
properly (the indriyas). Bharata, the son of Dasharatha, set a lovely example of surrender 
when he went to the forest with the residents of Ayodhya in an effort to persuade his brother 
Rama to come back. Rama seriously retorted that if Bharata requested him to, he would be 
more than happy to go back to the capital in response to their pleas. All eyes were on Bharata, 
who was present to persuade him to come back. ‘It is not for me to command, but merely to 
follow’, he calmly retorted.  
The idea of submission is discussed in the Bhagavad Gita as well. It is uncommon to achieve 
something easily. After utterly negating all sensations and faculties, we move forward 
according to the fundamental rules of dedication. This idea of Indian self -surrender can be 
used in counselling in order to help clients surrender to the circumstances in life which is not 
under their control. It seems that we as humans have such high rates of anxiety and sadness 
due to us spending all our time attempting to fit ourselves into a distorted societal reality. We 
are raised in households that implicitly want us to fit in, and as a result, we are socialised to 
do so. Then, as extra conditioning, we attend schools where we are expected to sit quietly and 
learn in a very linear fashion. Additionally, we might have been brought up in a religion that 
forbids us from sharing our radical viewpoints with others to fit in. Such radical people are 
shamed and humiliated if they publically express their ideas. Additionally, there are unwritten 
guidelines or things we must do to appease the other person (or people) in our personal 
relationships. The idea of ‘just be yourself’ seems ironic, when seen in the context of so many 
rules and structures put in place from early in life. What is the answer in this case? Do we 
have to battle thoughts, emotions, and relationships all the way to the grave?  
157 
 
 
Some difficulty is inevitable and even worthwhile to engage with, but that by practising 
surrender, we can also bring in greater ease. When you surrender, you stop fighting life and 
start accepting it. It’s not that you’re giving up, abdicating your duty, allowing others to 
control you, or abdicating your power. It’s very different from that. Fundamentally, giving 
yourself over to something else generates space. This is the essence of yielding or 
surrendering. It involves letting something other than your own concerns and feelings fill you 
up. However, it’s not necessarily for the best when that ‘something else’ is involving you to 
greater degree. For instance, take addiction as an example. People with addictions give in to 
their preferred substances, whether it’s food, relationships/love/sex, video games, etc., and 
are subsequently controlled by it. It is something over which they have no control. Addiction 
is compelled by a desperate need to have whatever it is in order to feel better, numb, or to 
otherwise escape the person’s current emotional reality. 
Dr. Marion Woodman quoted as saying: ‘Having a body that is like a musical instrument, 
open enough to be able to resonate, literally resonate with what is coming both from the 
inside and from the outside, so that one is able to surrender to powers greater than oneself.’ 
So how can you perform this nebulous act of submission/surrender? You must soften your 
mind, body, and soul in order to surrender and to relax into whatever the situation is.  
Although it can also be an exterior act, it is very much an interior shift. The physical act 
might serve as a metaphor for intensifying the shift occurring internally.

4.5 Modern Trends in Counselling 
In the ancient times, the form of counselling was informal and unorganized. Gradually, with 
an increase in complications in the society, it was felt that the process of counselling should 
be made scientific, well-organized and formal so that this can be utilized well and properly. In 
the recent times, the form of counselling has developed much, and different types of 
counselling have come into being for the solution of different types of problems in life. There 
are different approaches to counselling as we have already discussed. However nowadays 
these approaches are being merged and used according to the particular of the situation client. 
Counselling can be divided into following parts on the basis of their aim or scope: 
· Psychological counselling: Under psychological counselling, an individual suffering 
from a mental disorder is treated. A counsellor assists a counsellee by general dialogue to 
express his suppressed desires, emotions and sensations. Besides, he also gives him necessary 
suggestions and information. 
· Psychopathological counselling: Psychopathology is the scientific study of mental 
disorders, including efforts to understand their genetic, biological, psychological, and social 
causes; effective classification schemes; course across all stages of development; 
manifestations; and treatment. The term may also refer to the manifestation of behaviours that 
indicate the presence of a mental disorder. This also uses dialogue as the tool, and the 
158 
 
methods of hypnotism, psycho -drama, etc., are used. It comprises of the serious problems of 
maladjustment. 
· Diagnostic counselling: Under diagnostic counselling, a problem is analysed and then 
diagnosed. When a counsellee developed any improper behaviour in the adjustment or order 
of self-expression, he is treated with the help of behavioural psychology. 
· Marital counselling: Under this counselling, an individual is given suitable 
suggestions about the selection of his life partner and adjustment with him/her. The 
increasing number of divorces and broken families necessitates the setting up of a large 
number of marital counselling agencies/centres. 
· V ocational counselling: V ocational counselling helps an individual in selecting a 
vocation of his choice. Under this type of counselling, different types of data are gathered 
about an individual before he is counseled.  
· Student counselling: Student counselling is related to the common problems that are 
faced by the students. Different types of information are provided to the students regarding 
the selection of teaching institutions and curriculum, so that they can analyse the given 
information and take a decision accordingly. 
· Transfer counselling: Transfer counselling is given in the selection of a suitable 
vocation keeping in view the interest, ability, capability and outlook of a counsellee. 
The chief classification of counselling is based on its form. On the basis of form, the 
following types of counselling are in trend: 
· Directive counselling: Directive counselling gives complete importance to the 
counsellor. 
· Non-directive counselling: Non -directive counselling gives more importance to the 
counsellor. 
· Eclectic counselling: Eclectic counselling gives equal importance to both counsellor 
and counsellee. 
4.5.1 Directive Counselling 
Directive counselling is counsellor centred. A counsellor keeps his attention on the problem 
and establishes the relationship of friendship and cooperation with the counsellee and 
expresses his opinions freely. He evaluates the expressions of the counsellee and arranges an 
interview. He prepares a brief -answer questionnaire in the interview. He does not permit the 
development of his emotions and expressions, but renders counselling after self-observation. 
E.G. Williamson was an important supporter of this method. According to him, a counsellor 
considers himself responsible for the problem -solving of the counsellee. He investigates and 
explores the problem, defines it, diagnoses it and tries to eliminate it. 
Willy and Andrew, in their book Modern Methods and Techniques in Guidance, have 
mentioned the following approved estimates of directive counselling: 
· A counsellor is more able, skilled, trained and experienced; therefore he can give a 
better solution to a problem. 
159 
 
· Counselling is an intellectual process. The intellectual ability of a counsellee is not 
destroyed due to his maladjustment; therefore a counsellor lays emphasis on his emotional 
aspect as well as intellectual aspect. 
· The evaluation of the aims of counselling is determined from the situation of 
problem-solving. 
· A counsellee is not capable of problem -solving. He can solve his problems with the 
help of a counsellor. 
According to Williamson, the norms of counseling are as follows: 
· The aim of counselling is to affect the all -round development of a counsellee in order 
to assist him. 
· Counselling is based on an individual’s own desire and not on pressure. 
· The facility of counseling should be provided only when a student is faced with a 
problem and is unable to solve it himself. From this view, counseling is diagnostic. 
· The mutual relations in counseling are neutral. 
· Counselling lays special emphasis on self-approval in the context of a problem. 
· The counsellor respects the dignity of a counsellee. 
Thus, in Williamson point of view, a counsellor is like a teacher who enables an individual to 
understand his capabilities, attitudes, interests, motivations and lifestyle. 
Steps of directive counselling 
Williamson has mentioned the following six steps of directive counselling: 
 1. Analysis: Under analysis, reliable data are gathered from different reliable 
sources. These sources are cumulative records, interview, time division form, biography, 
amended article, psychological tests, etc. In some cases, case history is also used, by which 
data regarding family history, recreation, interests, etc., are found out. 
 2. Synthesis: Under this step, the compiled data is presented in an orderly, 
systematic and brief manner so that the qualities, limitations, adjustments and maladjustments 
of the counsellee can be found out. 
 3. Diagnosis: Under diagnosis, the factors responsible for the problem and its 
nature are found out and a student’s abilities and limitations are estimated. The three 
components of diagnosis are as follows: 
 (i) Introduction to problem 
 (ii) Exploration of cause 
 (iii) Prognosis 
 4. Prognosis: When prognosis of a problem’s outcome is estimated, then it is a 
part of the diagnosis, but under other situations it is an independent step. 
160 
 
 5. Counselling or treatment: Under this step, a counsellor counsels a counsellee 
on the basis of analysis. He removes his doubts and treats him accordingly. 
 6. Follow-up: Under this final step, the effect of counseling is evaluated.  
Characteristics of directive counselling 
The main characteristics of directive counselling are as follows: 
 · A counsellor is prominent in directive counselling. 
 · He suggests or counsels a counsellee. 
 · The problem is the focal point of the counselling, and not the counsellee. 
· In directive counselling, a counsellee is under a counsellor, and is not equal to 
him. 
· At the time of counselling, the directive method, lecture method, and 
analytical method is employed. 
· At the time of counselling, a counsellor lays more emphasis on his intellectual 
aspect than emotional aspect. 
Limitations of directive counselling 
There are some limitations to directive counselling too, due to which the process of ideal 
counselling can be obstructed. These limitations are as follows: 
· In this process, a counsellee becomes dependent on a counsellor and he is not 
able to solve the new problems of maladjustments. 
· In directive counselling, the experiences and aptitudes of a counsellee are 
absent. 
 · A counsellor is not able to awake a counsellee towards the errors in the future. 
· There is a possibility of giving wrong counselling due to the compilation of 
insufficient data. 
Advantages of directive counselling 
This type of counselling is beneficial in many ways. The advantages of directive counselling 
are as follows: 
 · This method is useful due to its limited time. It saves a lot of time. 
· In this counselling, more emphasis is laid on the problem and less on the 
counsellee, and this is justified in many ways. 
 · A counsellor can look at the counsellee directly. 
· In this, more attention is given to the intellectual aspect as compared to the 
emotional aspect, which assists in the rational solution of a problem. 
· In this process, a counsellor is readily available for assistance and a counselee 
is much contended and assured. 
161 
 
4.5.2 Non-Directive Counselling 
Non-directive counselling is counsellee -oriented. Its proponent is Carl R. Rogers. According 
to his opinion, an individual is prominent and not his problem; so the activities of a 
counsellor are not as important; rather it is his function to create such an environment in 
which a counsellee can act independently and determine his path himself. In this process, the 
emotional aspect of a counsellee is emphasized.  
No doubt, the counsellee-centred process revolves round a counsellee. In this, a counsellee is 
encouraged to converse, express his opinion, thoughts and attitudes, etc. Generally, a 
counsellor is passive and he does not obstruct the expression of a counsellee.  In this type of 
counselling, past data are not compiled about a counsellee, nor are conducted any types of 
tests. Only he is encouraged to speak up. He is fully free. 
Basic assumptions of non-directive counselling 
The basic assumptions are: 
· Trust in man’s dignity: Rogers has faith in an individual’s dignity. He thinks 
that an individual is capable of taking his decisions himself and he knows what he 
should do. 
· Emphasis on emotions and passions: In non -directive counselling, more 
emphasis is laid on the emotional aspect than on intellectual aspect. A counsellee is 
given numerous opportunities to express his emotions himself freely. It brings out his 
emotions and attitudes, and a problem is solved on the basis of this information. 
· Trust in the goodness of man: According to Rogers, an individual is basically 
good and reliable. Sometimes, under adverse circumstances, he behaves himself 
improperly and elimination this problem requires the all -round development of 
personality. 
· Proper environment: In this process, a counsellor is not a director or referee. 
His real work is limited to the creation of a suitable environment in which a 
counsellee takes his decision himself. He does not hold a counsellor responsible for 
the decision, rather he becomes able to understand himself better. 
William Snider has mentioned the following fundamental characteristics of non -
directive counselling: 
 · A counsellor is free to choose the objective of his life. 
· If a counsellee is given an opportunity, he would select those objectives which 
makes him happy. 
· Through this counselling, a counsellee becomes able to take decisions in a 
limited time. 
· Emotional conflict is the strongest obstacle in the proper adjustment of an 
individual. 
 
162 
 
Steps in non-directive counselling 
Carl Rogers has mentioned the following steps in non-directive counselling: 
· Define the problem situation: A counsellor at first defines those situations 
which create a problem. 
· Express emotions freely: In the second step, a counsellor creates such an 
environment in which a counsellee can express his emotions freely. 
· To classify positive and negative emotions: Under this step, a counsellor 
classifies the emotions of a counsellee under positive and negative types. 
· To develop insight: As a counsellee starts to have self-realization, a counsellor 
keeps explaining his emotions so that a counsellee can develop proper insight. 
· To give up counselling situation: In the end, after the objective of counselling 
is achieved, the counselling situation is given up, and this step can be taken by 
any of the counsellor or counselee. 
Characteristics of non-directive counselling 
The important characteristics of non-directive counselling are as follows: 
 · Non-directive counselling is a counselee cantered process. 
· This is based on the principle that an individual has so many abilities and he 
can develop them so as to be able to face any situation. 
 · A counsellor remains passive in non-directive counselling. 
· An individual is accepted in his originality and he is free to express any of his 
emotions. 
 · This type of counselling improves upon psychological adjustment. 
 · This eliminates mental tensions. 
 · This type of counselling functions in a reactionary manner. 
· This brings the picture drawn by a counselee and a desired ideal picture 
together. 
 · A counselee becomes emotionally mature. 
· The main objective of the non -directive counselling is to cultivate self -
confidence in an individual. 
 · Sometimes, directive counselling too has to resort to this method. 
 · In this type of counselling, the whole responsibility lies with the counselee. 
 · In this counselling, no diagnostic tool or test is used. 
· In this type of counselling, it is necessary to assimilate the factors of reality, 
sensitivity and naturalness. 
163 
 
Limitations of non-directive counselling  
The limitations of non-directive counselling are as follows: 
 · This type of counselling is not as intensive as psychoanalysis. 
· This gives freedom to a counsellee to express his attitudes and emotions 
freely, but the emotions are not analyzed; these are neither refined nor tested. 
· A major limitation of this type of counselling is that a counsellor’s role is not 
flexible. 
 · This type of counselling cannot be utilized in institutions, such as school. 
· This is a time-consuming process and a counsellor gets to speak very less, i.e., 
his role is limited. 
· A counsellee cannot fully depend on his own decision and intellectual ability, 
he often requires others’ counselling and decisions for problem solving. 
· Due to passivity of a counsellor, a counsellee is sometimes hesitant to speak 
out freely. 
Advantages of non-directive counselling  
There are certain advantages of non-directive counselling. They are as follows: 
 · Under this method, a counsellee develops the ability to solve his problems. 
 · It emancipates from many undesirable tests and techniques. 
· In this method, several things in the subconscious can be brought out into the 
conscious mind. 
 · This type of counselling has its long lasting effect. 
Thus, it is evident that non-directive counselling has certain advantages and at many times, an 
individual is able to solve his problems by giving vent to his emotions. 
4.5.3 Eclectic Counselling 
We have already discussed this to some extent as one of the models of counselling. Here, we 
will look at it in depth. The combination of directive and non-directive counselling depending 
upon the situational factors is known as eclectic counselling. In this type of counselling, a 
counsellor is neither fully active nor fully passive; rather he studies different aspects of a 
counsellee’s personality and selects certain useful techniques for him.  
Under this method, a counsellor can begin his work from the directive method and then can 
start to use the non -directive method keeping in with the time and place. In this, counsellor 
and counsellee are active and find the solution together. The major techniques in this type of 
counselling are compilation of data, assurance, case history, examination, etc. 
 
 
164 
 
 
Steps in eclectic counselling 
The steps through which the process of eclectic counselling passes is as follows: 
· Study of need of counselling and personality: In the first step, a counsellor 
locates the primary need of counselling and compiles relevant data regarding 
his personality traits. 
· Selection of techniques: Under this step, a counsellor selects the techniques, 
keeping in mind the needs of counselling and uses them accordingly. 
· Use of techniques: The selected techniques are utilized only under specific 
circumstances. Not only this, the utility of the techniques is determined on the 
basis of desire and need of the counsellee. 
· Evaluation of the effect: Under this step, the techniques used so far are 
evaluated to know their effect. 
· Preparation for counselling: In this step, due preparation is made for giving 
counselling and guidance to a counsellee. 
· To get advice from the counsellee and related individuals: Under the final step, 
the advice is sought from the counsellee and other related individuals 
regarding the topics of its programmes and other aims. 
Characteristics of eclectic counselling 
F. C. Thorne has mentioned the following characteristics of eclectic counselling: 
 · In this type of counselling, objectives and integrative methods are used. 
· In the beginning of the counselling, the role of the counsellee is active and the 
counsellor is passive, and for this, suitable techniques are used. 
 · The principle of frugality is adhered in this method. 
· In this type of counselling, it is necessary for a counsellor to have desired skill 
and professional efficiency in the use of required methods and techniques. 
· Keeping the need of counsellee in view, it is decided whether directive or non-
directive method should be used. 
· Emphasis is laid on begetting favourable opportunities to the counsellee by 
which he can find a solution to the problems himself. 
Limitations to eclectic counselling 
The limitations of eclectic counselling are as follows: 
· Eclectic counselling has no set of rules, as it is not clearly defined by the 
scholars, which makes this type of counselling unclear.  
· It is very difficult to present a synthesized form of directive and non -directive 
counselling, because these two are like poles apart. 
165 
 
· There is no definite rule as to how much freedom should be given to a 
counsellee.  
Despite this argument, the practical importance of eclectic counselling is much and it can 
help to get useful results in less time. Eclectic counselling is practical, while being a learning 
process, in which both counsellor and counsellee get to learn something. 
The difference between these two can be understood easily: 
 
 Directive Counseling Non-Directive Counselling 
1. No person is able to study himself, 
so he requires the services of a 
counsellor. 
An individual has numerous abilities to 
develop, but he is not aware of them 
many times.

4.5.4 Future of Counselling 
The following principles are the future directions for the profession of counselling. 
· The counselling profession shall present a clear definition of counselling to the 
public. 
· It shall promote optimum health and wellness for those served as the ultimate 
goal for counselling interventions. 
· The profession shall focus on a body of core knowledge and skills shared by 
all counsellors. 
 · It shall speak with a united voice at the state and federal levels. 
 · It shall have the highest respect for all counselling specialties. 
· Counsellor education programs shall reflect a philosophy that counselling is a 
single profession with specialized areas of training. 
· Counselling accrediting bodies shall reflect a philosophy that counselling is a 
single profession with specialized areas of training. 
166 
 
 
· The profession shall develop a fluid process allowing new specialties to 
emerge and existing specialties to evolve naturally and merge as 
developmentally appropriate. 
 · It shall develop an outreach/marketing process that reflects a shared  
identity. 
· The profession shall conduct ongoing outreach to ensure that the public 
understands who professional counsellors are, the credentials and skills they 
possess, and how their services are uniquely different. 
· Professional counselling organizations shall involve front -line practitioners at 
all policy and decision-making levels. 
· It shall speak with a united voice when educating the health care insurance 
industry and advocating for counselling, counsellors, or clients. 
· It shall offer ongoing education and training for counsellors to encourage 
client and student advocacy. 
· Professional counselling organizations shall collaborate to identify an 
advocacy project that would be completed annually within a selected 
community. 
· The counselling profession shall investigate and delineate the best 
organizational structure for the future of counselling. 
· It shall establish common preparation standards and a single training model to 
be used by all counsellor preparation programs. 
· It shall establish uniform licensing standards with LPC as an entry -level title 
for counsellor licensure. 
 · It shall create interest in research by practitioners and students. 
 · It shall emphasize both qualitative and quantitative outcome research. 
· Outcome research shall focus on delineating the counselling modalities that 
work best in different counselling settings and with particular counselling 
clients. 
 · Outcome research shall delineate best practices in counsellor preparation. 
· This profession shall encourage evidenced -based, ethical practices as the 
foundation for counsellors -in-training, and for professional counsellor 
interventions across settings and populations served. 
· It shall work with undergraduate programs to educate students about graduate 
work in counselling.

4.6 Answers to Check Your Progress Questions 
1. Credibility is an important consideration in choosing a counsellor. It implies 
two things:  
 a. Enhancing expertise by study and practice 
 b. Creating trust by maintaining integrity in relationships 
2. Reflexivity in counselling is to do with the ability to adapt to the mode of 
explanation of the client. As the client changes his or her ways of construing 
events, so too must the counsellor. The counsellor has to be involved in a 
reflexive process, seeking to make his or her own interpretation explicit by 
interaction with the client. 
3. George Kelly describes the cycle of change as a process of five stages, which 
are as follows: Anticipation; Commitment; Encounter; 
Confirmation/Disconfirmation; and Constructive revision. 
4. According to Rogers (1961), as a result of person -centred counselling clients 
should become: 
 a. More realistic in their self-perceptions. 
 b. More confident and self-directing. 
 c. More positively valued by themselves. 
 d. Less likely to repress aspects of their experiences. 
5. Carkhuff’s book, Toward Effective Counselling and Psychotherapy, was 
published in 1967. 
6. The main disadvantage of the integrative approach is that if a counsellor is 
unable to detach from preconceived notions and personal prejudices, he or she 
will rationalize the choice of theory whether it makes sense or not and this 
may end up being damaging for the client. 
7. Karma and Samsara, two philosophical concepts, are frequently characterised 
as having a fatalistic outlook on life. The idea that past deeds determine the 
present may inspire a feeling of inevitability. 
8. Bharata, the son of Dasharatha, set a lovely example of surrender when he 
went to the forest with the residents of Ayodhya in an effort to persuade his 
brother Rama to come back. Rama seriously retorted that if Bharata requested 
him to, he would be more than happy to go back to the capital in response to 
their pleas. All eyes were on Bharata, who was present to persuade him to 
come back. ‘It is not for me to command, but merely to follow’, he calmly 
retorted.  
9. Directive counselling gives complete importance to the counsellor, while non -
directive counselling gives more importance to the counsellor. 
 10. The limitations of eclectic counselling are as follows: 
168 
 
a. Eclectic counselling has no set of rules, as it is not clearly defined by the 
scholars, which makes this type of counselling unclear. 
b. It is very difficult to present a synthesized form of directive and non -directive 
counselling, because these two are like poles apart. 
c. There is no definite rule as to how much freedom should be given to a 
counselee. 
4.7 Summary 
• The relationship between the client and counsellor is certainly professional but 
it requires a human touch to be fruitful. A person-to-person relationship can be 
a basic foundation for an effective counselling process. 
• Counselling is a process of learning about the client’s situation and extending 
help. Each counselling instance is a case study to be added to the knowledge 
base and a source of new experience in learning. 
• Communication is at the core of counselling. A counsellor should be effective 
in oral and non -verbal communication. He or she should be good at speaking 
and listening. 
• A client may find it hard to continue talking and become silent. The role of the 
counsellor is to interpret the silence and if necessary break it. Some clients 
expect encouraging words of appreciation or inquiry to go on.  
• Inquiry need not be based on interrogative approaches. By stating the 
questions in an assertive or declaratory form, one can secure information. This 
is a mild and gentle form of inquiry. 
• Scanning is the process of scrutinizing the information relating to a particular 
problem area. It seeks to look in and look around to gather details about the 
problem. It presents a problems map. 
• Brainstorming is a creative thinking tool. It is an idea generation technique. It 
helps one think beyond existing options to experimental ideas that have not so 
far been considered infeasible or ruled out as unrealistic. 
• Without theoretical packing, counsellors’ operate haphazardly in a trial -and-
error manner and risk being both ineffective and harmful. 
• An effective counsellor decides which theory or theories and methods to use 
on the basis of educational background, philosophy and the needs of the 
clients. 
• Carl Rogers developed client -centred therapy as a reaction against what he 
considered the basic limitations of psychoanalysis. 
• According to Roger, there are certain attributes on the therapist’s part 
(genuineness, Non-possessive warmth and acceptance, and accurate empathy) 
169 
 
that constitute the necessary and sufficient conditions for therapeutic 
effectiveness to occur within the client. 
• Robert R. Carkhuff created the helping and human relationship theory to 
explain how people interact in the professional setting. 
• Attending entails starting the conversation. There is a greeting, an introduction 
of each party to the relationship, and an effort to make the client feel 
comfortable. 
• The nurse should start informing the client about the expected response and 
any available options once the interaction’s purpose has been established and 
the client has consented to it. The helper starts responding to the client. 
• The IPR model is described by the APA as a technique used to comprehend 
psychotherapy procedures and to train counsellors and therapists. 
• IPR offers supervisees a secure setting to explore internal reactions by reliving 
the client interaction through a process recall supervision session. 
• The eclectic counsellors do not let themselves be bound by the dogma, or 
standard practices of psychotherapy.  
• An integrative approach to counselling and psychotherapy is best 
characterized by attempts to look beyond and across the confines of single -
school approaches in order to see what can be learned from, and how clients 
can benefit from, other perspectives. 
• Karma and Samsara, two philosophical concepts, are frequently characterised 
as having a fatalistic outlook on life. The idea that past deeds determine the 
present may inspire a feeling of inevitability. 
• Rejecting love, relationships, joys, suffering, and attachment calls for an 
almost limitless mental fortitude and iron will. Any advantages of renunciation 
will be outweighed by the wisdom gained by actively pursuing selfless 
conduct. 
• Some difficulty is inevitable and even worthwhile to engage with, but that by 
practising surrender, we can also bring in greater ease. 
• Directive counselling is counsellor centred. A counsellor keeps his attention on 
the problem and establishes the relationship of friendship and cooperation with 
the counselee and expresses his opinions freely. 
• Non-directive counselling is counselee -oriented. Its proponent is Carl R. 
Rogers. According to his opinion, an individual is prominent and not his 
problem. 
• The combination of directive and non -directive counselling depending upon 
the situational factors is known as eclectic counselling. 
 
170 
 
4.8 Key Words 
• Probing: In psychotherapy, the use of direct questions intended to stimulate 
discussion in the hope of uncovering relevant information or helping the client 
come to a particular realization or achieve a particular insight. 
• Eclectic Counselling: The combination of directive and non -directive 
counselling depending upon the situational factors is known as eclectic 
counselling. 
4.9 Self-Assessment Questions and Exercises 
Short-Answer Questions 
1. Briefly explain the questioning technique as used in the case of probing in 
counselling. 
 2. Write a short note on components of the Carkhuff model of counselling skills. 
 3. What is the role of self-surrender? Briefly explain. 
 4. What do you think is the future direction of counselling? 
Long-Answer Questions 
 1. Explain the learning skills required by a counsellor. 
 2. Discuss the Rogers’ model of counselling in detail. 
 3. Describe the IPR model of counselling. 
 4. Evaluate the significance and working of directive counselling.

Publications.

Practice. United Kingdom: SAGE Publications.
